Notice: summer work and open questions
The wheat harvest starts after the fair. Bring clean sacks to the barn, and write your name on each one. Do not stack them by the mill door. The wheel is mended, but the miller still wants room to turn it by hand if the water drops.
The tutor stall will stay open beside the notice board until the last cart leaves. Ila from Vell will show the crystal maps again. They are drawings of marks that changed while a mind learned from drills.
The tutors can watch one part of a crystal change after a wrong answer. They can follow how some of that change is passed back through the parts before it. This helps them train minds with many layers. Each layer can do a share of the work, and the mind can learn more than a single crystal could learn.
Ila says the maps are useful, but they are not a clear picture of every thought. Some parts seem to help a mind sort, and some seem to help it keep what it learned. The same marks can be hard to read in another drill. The tutors do not yet know what every part does.
They also do not know why a crystal learns from what it is shown. A map can show what changed. It cannot answer that question.
Write questions on the board before you come. Keep them short, so the little ones can read them too. No question will be laughed at. If Ila does not know, she will say so.
Ivo will keep the gate latched after dusk. A zombie was seen there last night, though it did not get past the torches. Tam has put a fresh torch by the north field. Keep the other torches lit.
The fair will have apple cakes, honey, and beetroot rolls. The miller says there will be flour if nobody borrows the wheel again. Tam says he did not borrow it. The miller says he knows. The wheel says nothing, which is sensible.
Ivo

Lost and found, the board at the gate
Kept at the gate of the house at the edge of Three Wells. Lark has written this list every morning since the first frost, and will write it until the carters stop losing things. They have not stopped yet.

FOUND: a horseshoe, on the step. Nobody has come for the horse.

FOUND: a pair of good leather gloves, one wet and one dry, which is the wrong way round for gloves.

FOUND: six eggs in a basket, unbroken, on the new road by the first waymark. The basket was not claimed. The eggs are with the baker, who says they are her eggs now and she is sorry about it.

LOST: one spoon, carved with a bird on the handle. The bird is sad about it.

LOST: a small grey goat, two days late from the Ambrook cart. It went up the hill, not down. Ask the relay keeper, who has seen it twice and says it looked pleased.

LOST: the miller's temper, last market day. If you find it, bring it to the mill, and leave it on the step. He says it is no use to anyone else.

FOUND: a book of drills from Vell, with the page for the sorting crystal torn out. The tutor asks that it be returned, and that whoever has the page please keep it safe, because it is the one with the apples on it.

LOST: a coin purse with three emeralds and a note from Pell, which says the emeralds are not to be spent on beer. The purse has been lost once already, so please keep it dry.

FOUND: a child's shoe, red, with a bell on the toe. The bell still rings. The child has not been found. Come to the gate if you know one.

WANTED: somebody who knows where the sheep went, and who will tell the sheep's owner before the sheep tells him.

WARNING: The cloth over the north well has torn. Do not drink from the well until it is mended. The water is fine. The cloth is not.

Please bring back what you borrowed. The house has a list, and the list is long, and Lark is patient with almost everyone.

Bet: the goat and the ladder
Written by Clank, apprentice at Copperhold, at the mine hall. Second of Blossom, 196.
There is a bet. It is a serious bet.
Old Rill says the goat cannot climb the ladder to the sorting shed roof. I say it can. Hesk says it can but should not. That is not a side. That is a warning.
The terms: if the goat climbs to the roof, Rill gives me two emeralds. If it does not, I polish all the lamps in the Deep Works for a month. There are many lamps. I have counted.
We tried on Blossom the first. The goat went up four rungs and stopped. It looked at us. It came down. It ate the rope.
We try again on the sixth. Bring a rope. Bring two ropes. Do not tell Hesk.
The goat is called Bramble now. That was not part of the bet but it seemed right.
Clank

Notice: the looking-glass day
Posted at the hall at Kettle Ridge, in Longday, by order of the tutors' house at Vell.
On the fourth day of Melon a tutor will show the inside of one crystal to anyone who wants to see it. The crystal is in its stand. It will be lit from below, so that the marks on its surface can be seen.
A crystal is not a window. You will see the marks the tutor has made on the surface, and the lights that move when a question is asked.
The tutors have found some things. Some parts of the crystal light up when the question is about words. Some light up when the question is about the field, or the weather, or a number.The tutors have not found the rest. Most of the crystal is still dark to them, and it is not empty. It is only not yet understood.
Do not touch the crystal. Do not breathe on it. Children may look from the back of the hall.
Tutor Nessa will answer questions for one hour after the show. She asks that the questions be short.
Posted by order of the Kettle Ridge hall

Notice: Market Day Lesson
Three Wells square. Winter of the 42nd year. Fallon, Archive clerk.
The market stalls will open beside the three wells after the snow is cleared.
At the east well, Nera will show the old drill box from Vell. It held copper weights for the first four apprentices.
Each helper had a mind in a crystal. The tutors showed a mind the same kind of example many times. One helper learned a row. One learned a sort. The other two had their own tasks.
A crystal learned from what it was shown. The old card says so. It does not say why.
Please keep the box on the table. The weights are small, and the square is full of little hands.

Moving day, and a homecoming
Kept on the board at the house at the edge of Three Wells. Written by Lark, innkeeper, and pinned by the gate, because the gate is where everybody looks.

MOVING DAY. The Ostles move into the house by the mill pond on the last day of Apple. They have a cart, a cow, three chairs, and a bed that will not fit through the door. Anyone with a strong back and no plans for the day is asked to come at dawn. The cow will ride in the cart. Please do not ask the cow to walk.

Bring a rope, a lamp and an opinion about the bed. Mrs Ostle asks that the opinions be kept short. Her husband will have his own, and it will be longer.

HOMECOMING. Pim Hale is back from Vell. He has been a year in the copying room, and he can write a letter in a clear hand without a blot. He is at his mother's house by the east well. There will be a supper for him at the north well when the frost comes. Everyone is invited. Pim will read a copied page aloud, and then he will want to sit down.

LOST. One brown hen, with a red ribbon on her leg. She answers to Plum. She is not answering to anyone at the moment.

LOST. One boot, left foot, from the cart outside the bakery. The right foot is looking for it.

Lark, innkeeper

Notice: Market and Field Help
The farm will hold a small summer market on the last day of Melon. Stalls may be set beside the barn, where the ground is level. Please leave the path to the relay house clear.
Tam will bring wheat, beetroot, potatoes and carrots. We have apples from the south hedge, honey from the three hives, and wool from the sheep that would not stay out of the kitchen garden. The sheep has now learned the gate latch. We have not learned how.
Pell will bring salt, fish and spare handles for tools. He has promised not to put the salt beside the honey this year. Last year a child took home a jar of salt and said it was very good honey. The child was polite about it.
At the market, people may trade goods or use emeralds. Write the price on the board before opening your stall. Please do not change it after someone has agreed to buy. If the board is full, ask Yarrow for another piece of wood.
There will be a table for lost things. Put each thing there with a note that says where it was found. Do not take a lost thing just because you know who owns it. Tell the owner first. This rule is mainly for Pip, who found his own hat and sold it to Tam.
Field help is needed on the morning after the market. The ditch must be cleared of weeds, and the bottom rows of potatoes need turning. Bring a hoe if you have one. We have four good hoes and one that is more handle than blade.
Apprentice Clover will carry baskets from the field to the barn. It has been tested on this path and can carry two baskets at a time. Give it one clear order, then leave room for it to turn.
> bring two baskets to the barn
= haul
Do not place a basket on its head. Clover carried one that way last week, but the potatoes fell out at the first turn.
The fair meal will be served at the farm after the work. There will be bread, beetroot stew and apple cake. The cooks will keep the salt jar on its own shelf.
Yarrow, farm board, summer of 205

Found: three socks, none the same. Hanging on the line by the quay.
Lost: one idea. Corin had a good idea for a wider boat last night and went to bed. By morning it had gone. If you think it came to you, send word. It was something about the bottom.

Notice: the fair and other news
The autumn fair is on the first day of Apple. Stalls by dawn. Judging at midday. Prizes at dusk. The melon at the end of the row is entered in the great-fruit contest, and the market council has asked that nobody try to sell it before the judging.

Other news, by the post cart from Three Wells:

The bridge over the Kettle brook has a loose plank in its middle. It is the third plank from the east end. Walk on the edge until it is mended, which will be before the fair, if the carpenters are free. They are not free. The mayor says they will be free after the fair, which is what the mayor said last year.

A cart overturned on the ridge road last night. Nobody was hurt, but the cheese went into the ditch and has not come out. The carter says the cheese was to blame. The cheese says nothing.

A new post box has been put up at the Kettle Inn, on the post. Letters go in, and the post cart takes them down. Please write your name on the outside, so that the post cart does not have to guess.

The pumpkin patch behind the inn had a visitor on the second night of Reaping. A fox sat on the wall and considered the pumpkins for an hour. It did not take one. We do not know whether it was too proud or too lazy. Both are possible in a fox.

The market council thanks everybody who came to help the mill keeper. The sail is being carved. It will be the best sail on the ridge, and the only one with a carved fox on its back, because the carver says he was asked to. He says nobody has asked him to take the fox off.

The Halfway Inn has put a new riddle on its board, beside the hearth. Two carters have gone to bed in tears, and one has gone home to think about it. The innkeeper asks that the answer not be shouted at the riddle.

The archive at Vell has sent a copy of the market rules, for the rack by the scales. The clerk asks that they be read, and not only kept. Ulla says the copy is in very good order, and that nobody should spill anything on it.

Warning: a zombie was seen in the north field on the evening before the fair. It walked very slowly toward the pumpkins, looked at them for some time, and went away down the bank. The torches on the north road are to be lit all week, and the council has voted to keep them lit after the fair as well.

Lost: a hen with one white leg, who has been living under the cart at the mill for four days, and is fed by the mill keeper's son. She will not come out until she is asked politely. Please ask politely.

The fair closes at dusk. The bell will be rung, the torches will be lit on the north road, and the carters are asked to go home by lamplight, carrying their own.

Hollis, archive clerk, for the market council, and Dace, mayor of Three Wells, for the post.

Rules of the Halfway stable, autumn
Posted in the Halfway stable by the hay loft, under the riddle board, where the carters can read it while their horses are being fed. Written by Jory, grandmother, after the carter with the bay mare tied his reins to the wrong post and the mare ate half the sign.

1. Tie the horse to the iron ring, not to the post. The post is for the lantern.
2. Water the horse before you feed it. A thirsty horse eats hay like a miser, and gets cross when the hay is too dry.
3. Check the hooves every night. Pick out the stones, and do not leave them in the trough.
4. Lift the saddle off with the strap, not the pommel. The pommel has been broken twice this year and the stable boy is tired of mending it.
5. Keep the cart lanterns trimmed and lit at the door. A lantern on the post is for the road, and the road is for everyone.
6. Do not smoke in the loft. Do not smoke in the yard. Do not smoke anywhere near the hay, and if you must, smoke with the mare, who is far more patient than you.
7. The loft is for the stable boy's bed and the stable boy's cat. The cat is not for hire.
8. If a horse is lame, tell the stable boy. Do not tell the horse.
9. A borrowed horse is to be brought back by the morning bell, fed and watered, with its saddle on the peg and its owner's name on the card. The card is by the door.
10. Pay the stable before you go. Not after. Jory says that is the only rule that has never once been broken.

Notice: Cave under the barley field
A hollow was found under the field below Vell's west path.
The ground opened near the old oak after the rain.
No one fell in. The opening is small, but the sides are loose.

Do not walk on the marked patch.
Do not lower a torch or a stone into the hollow.
The school children are asked to stay on the path with their tutor.
The farmer has put a fence around the soft ground.

A mason and two clerks looked from the edge.
They saw stone and a narrow passage below.
They did not go in. The Archive has been told, and the town will wait for safe steps.

If you found the opening, tell Deren at the mill.
Do not fill it with spare earth. The field needs that earth.
Teague will mend any coat torn on the fence, but not a person who climbs it.

Log: Lowmarsh weir, autumn
Kept by Pike, who keeps the weir. Written plain, for anyone who asks.

The weir is quiet in Apple. The fish come slow. That is how it goes.

Tally for the month so far: twelve baskets of fish, four of eels, one of something I did not catch the name of and would not eat. The tally board is on the wall of the boat house, as always. Every boat still comes in and marks the wall. Nobody has missed a day in nine years. I count them again every night because I like counting. Wren says that is a good sign in a person.

The water came up high on the fourth. The old post by the weir leaned over. Three of us put it back. It is straight now. It is a little straighter than before, which is not saying much.

The reed cutters came down from the north side. They stacked the reeds by the shed and Moss helped, and Moss carried twice what the two of us carried. Apprentices do that. They do not tire and they do not talk and they do not take a cup of tea, which is a shame. Moss knows the meaning come and the meaning store. When I say bring me the rope, Moss goes to the shed and comes back with the rope. Every time. I have not once had to fetch it myself. I have thought about what else I could ask for. I will not. It seems rude.

The brown hen came back on the ninth. It walked out of the reeds by the fish rack as if it had never been gone. It is on the rail now. It will not move. It is the hen. There is no doubt. It is not a good hen. It is a hen.

The first snow came on the last day of Leaffall. It lay on the reeds and the boat house roof and did not stay. The frogs stopped. The eels went down. We lit the torches on the posts and now the path from the turning to the weir is a line of little lights. It is good to walk home by.

Wren asked me to write down if anyone in the marsh has a cough. Two children, one old man, a woman by the far reeds. All mild. Wren says mild is good. Wren says if it gets worse, write it down too. I write it down. That is what the weir log is for.
%%
Notice: oak planks for sale
Posted at the Ambrook market, autumn. From Corin of Saltreach, who is visiting his cousin Jory at the mill.
I came for apples and found the best oak in the valley. It is in the old yard behind the mill, drying under a roof. Jory says his father cut it and forgot it. It is twenty years old. It has never been wet.
I need forty planks for a boat. I will buy them at a fair price, in emeralds, and I will carry them to Saltreach on three carts.
If you have oak you do not want, bring it. If you have oak you do want, keep it. If you are not sure, ask me. I will tell you what I would do.
I will pay for these things:
Planks, long and straight, no knots: 3 emeralds each.
Planks with a small knot: 2 emeralds each.
Planks with a large knot: free, and I will make you a spoon.
I will pay nothing for planks that have been rained on. Rain makes oak sad. A sad plank makes a sad boat.
A boat made at Saltreach from Ambrook oak will have an apple in its name. I promise it. We will cut an apple into the prow.
Corin, at the mill, until the end of the month. Bring the planks to the back door. Not the front. Ama's bees are in the front garden and they have opinions.

Notice: tutor at the inn
A tutor from Vell is staying at the Net and Nail until the third of Swarm.
The teaching is for anyone who wants to learn how a mind sorts things. Bring two clean bowls and a small bag of apples.
Please leave the tutor's crystal on its card. Do not tap the glass to wake it.
The lesson starts after the noon meal in the back room. There are six seats. The innkeeper has added a stool, but it has one short leg.
Zell asks if the tutor can teach a mind to sort fish from boots. The tutor says yes, if the examples are clear.
The tutor will show the same sort many times. If the mind gets one wrong, the tutor changes its weights a little and tries again.
No one is to take the bowls home. They belong to the inn.

Notice: the well in our yard has run dry
The well in our yard has run dry. It went dry at the end of Sowing, and it has not come back.
We have asked the town. The town says dig deeper. We are not diggers. We are a cook and a dog.
Until the masons come, we draw water from the well at the mine gate. The rope is good. The bucket is ours, with a red mark so you know it.
If you pass our house at the edge of town and want water, take it. Leave the bucket where you found it.
Keep the lid on the well. The dog falls in. The dog is not hurt, but she is not clever about it.
If you know a mason who is free, tell them Fallon needs a well.
Fallon, Copperhold, Blossom of 206

Rules for the pickers at the orchard
By Garrow, who keeps the rows below Kettle Ridge, with the help of my neighbor Tam, third of Apple, 196.
We have apples and bees, and now bees and apples and a few helpers made of copper and iron. Here are the rules for pickers, big and small.
Pick with the stem. Do not pull the fruit off the branch. A torn apple spoils in the box.
Fill the low boxes first. Climb only the short ladder. The tall ladder is for the oldest tree only, and only two folk on it.
Do not touch the hives. The bees are busy. If a bee comes near, stand still. It will lose interest.
Sort as you go. Good apples in one box, bruised in another, windfalls in the third. The pigs get the windfalls.
The miller at Ambrook takes the bruised for pressing. Do not eat the bruised ones.
The helpers carry the full boxes to the cart. Say "fetch apples" and they will bring them. They will also bring you a box you did not ask for. Say it once and wait.
Water is by the well. Rest in the shade when you need it.
The sluice on the mill pond is not to be opened. This is not a rule for pickers. It is a rule for everyone.

Notice: the visiting tutor, market week
Posted by the Vell tutors' house, at the market gate and the drill yard gate, for the market week of autumn. Please read it through to the end.

A tutor from the north hall will be at the drill yard on the square for four days. Her name is Oda. She has come down from Northpoint with a crate of drill cards and a very small crystal, which she has promised not to show anyone until they ask.

She will be drilling apprentices from the market stalls each morning, in the yard behind the bakery, from the first bell until noon. Apprentices who want to be tested may bring their card. Apprentices who only want to watch may sit on the wall, and must not stand in the drill row.

Stallholders: if your apprentice is to be drilled, bring it early. The drill is simple, and the first helper gets the best of the morning. Oda will not do the drill twice for the same helper in one day. This is not because she is cross. It is because she is tired.

Children: you may watch from the wall. You may not touch the cards. You may not tell the apprentice what the answer is. You may tell it, quietly, that it is doing well. Oda says that helps more than you would think.

Parents: please keep your children away from the crate. It is heavy, and the cards inside are in order, and a child who shuffles them will be sent to sit on the bench by the clerk for the rest of the day.

On the last day, Oda will give a short talk in the yard. Anyone who wants to hear it is welcome. She says it will be about how a mind is taught. She says it will be short, because she has been told that the longest talks are always given by people who do not know what they are talking about, and she would not like to be one.

Posted by the tutors' house, Vell

Notice: road and yard
The path from the Vell hill to the tutor house is clear. The rain has left mud beside the steps. Use the stone path and do not carry a full basket down the slope.

A fox was seen by the hens at Fen's field. It took one hen before the keeper reached the gate. The rest are shut in the hen house. Keep the latch closed at night. The fox was not seen near the Archive.

The tutors will test the small crystal this afternoon. It holds fewer marks than the larger one, so it fits in a smaller case. Some of its weights have been written with fewer marks. That saves room, but can cost a little skill. The crystal learned from the drills all the same.

The test fruit is in a covered basket. The mind has not been shown it. Please do not lift the cover, even to see if the apples are ripe. If the mind sorts badly, the tutors need to know. If it sorts well, they need to know that too.

Fen will bring the basket from his orchard plot. He asks for help carrying it back, since the path is slick. There is no pay, but he has saved two loaves and a jar of honey.

The grey cat has come back to the copying room. If you find it sitting on the drill box, tell Oren. Do not move the box.

Road Notice: Mud by the Mill
Board at Elm's house, Ambrook. Sowing, 191.

The Long Road past the mill pond is soft after the rain. Carts should take the upper way by the hives until the ditch drains.

The upper way is narrow. Let an empty cart pass before you bring a loaded one down. Horses can turn beside the old oak. Carts cannot turn beside the bee boxes.

Pell's cart sank to the axle yesterday. He says the road moved under him. It was mud. The road has made no reply.

A pair of boards has been laid across the worst place. Please leave them there. They are not firewood, even if they are dry.

The apprentice at Elm's gate carried one board to the shed after the first cart passed. Elm sent it back to the road. The apprentice carried it back again. Elm moved the shed farther away.

If you see a board missing, tell Elm. Do not fill the hole with apple branches. The branches make the mud harder to see and softer to cross.

The mill pond is high. Keep children and dogs off the bank until the water falls. Frogs may use the path. They have done so for years and will not wait for carts.

Notice: the test box
Halfway Inn, on the Long Road. Summer of 203. Orla, smith, has put this up beside the horseshoes at the request of a tutor who stayed the night. It is about how a tutor knows a mind is good.

The tutor was a woman from Copperhold, named Piper. She sat by the fire and explained it to anyone who would listen. I listened. I am writing it down.

When you drill a mind, you show it the same kind of example again and again. It gets better. But getting better at the drill does not mean it has learned. It may only have learned the drill. A dog can learn to sit on the third bench. That does not mean it knows what "sit" is.

So the tutors keep a box. They call it the test box. In the box are examples the mind has never seen. They are put in a box and sealed with wax. The mind is never drilled on them. Never. Not even once.

When the drilling is done, the tutor opens the box and asks the mind each question. If the mind gets nearly all of them right, it has learned something real. If it gets only the drilled ones right, it has only learned the drill.

"Never send an untested mind to the fields," Piper said. "A wrong mind in a field is a danger. A wrong mind in a field with a scythe is a worse danger."

I asked how big the box is. She said about a tenth of all the examples. A hundred pages in the box, for every nine hundred in the drill. She said the clerks at Vell take out the copies first, so that a page in the box is not also in the drill. If it was, the mind would see it before the test, and the test would be a lie.

I said that is a lot of care for a horseshoe. She said it was not for a horseshoe. It was for an apprentice that might one day walk beside a child.

I shoed her horse for nothing.

Orla
%%
Notice: Orla's test, at the Halfway Inn
At the Halfway Inn, on the first night of rain, autumn. Everyone is welcome. Bring an opinion. The soup is free, if you guess right. If you guess wrong, the soup is still free, because Hobb is not made of stone.

Who was Orla. Orla was a tutor at Vell, in the early years of the Archive, about a hundred and twenty years ago. She asked whether a drilled mind could ever think. She proposed a test. A hidden mind and a person answer the same questions by letter. If the readers cannot tell which letter is which, what then? People have argued about it ever since. Nobody has settled it. Tonight we will argue about it with soup.

The game. Two letters will be read aloud. Both answer one question: what is a good day? One was written by a person at this inn. One was written by the big mind at Vell, by letter, through the relay desk. Write on the slate which you think is the mind's. Do not talk. Do not look at your neighbor. Hobb will count the slates.

How the big mind writes. This is what Nessa told the inn, and it is as plain as she could make it. A big mind reads a page one letter at a time. It looks at a window of about sixty letters, which is about a line of a page. For each letter, it asks which of the letters before it matter. It takes what it needs from those, and little from the rest. Then it guesses the letter that comes next.

To write a letter, it guesses one letter, writes it down, and reads the page again with that letter added. Then it guesses the next. Letter after letter, it writes. It can do this because it has been drilled on a great many pages, and has learned how letters follow letters.

What the game shows. If you guess right, it shows that you can tell. If you guess wrong, it shows that the mind writes like a person. It does not show what the mind is. Nessa says nobody knows that. The tutors do not claim to know it.

The results of last night. 31 guests played. 14 guessed right. 17 guessed wrong. The cat did not play, and was the only one who looked sure.

The prize is soup. The question is free. Corin.

Notice: the road to Three Wells
The road from Copperhold down to the Long Road is mended. The carts can go again.
The rain took the edge off it in Blossom. The masons laid new cobblestone on both sides and packed the earth in the middle. It is level now. A cart with a full load can take it.
The new waymarks stand at the fork. They are three blocks high with a torch on top. Somebody has left little stones on them. Leave your own stone if you like. That is the custom.
There is water at the bridge and a bench by the stream. The bench is new. Do not take it apart for firewood.
The Brown Hen is one day down the road. The Halfway Inn is two days. The riddle board is by the hearth, and the hearth has not gone out in twenty years.
If you find a hole in the road, tell the masons. If you find a stone on a waymark, leave it there.
Fallon, cook, Copperhold, Leaffall of 206

Birth notice, spring 171
At dawn on the sixth of Blossom, Mara and Fen welcomed a daughter at the farm outside Ambrook.
The child is small, loud, and very sure of herself. Mara is well, and Fen has washed the blankets twice.
Her name is Sella. Her first visitor was the old grey ewe, who put her nose through the open door and stayed there.
Please leave food by the gate if you visit. Mara needs rest, and Fen needs reminders to eat.
There is broth in the blue pot. Do not take the blue pot. It belongs to Linnet, and she wants it back before supper.
Linnet gave Sella a wool blanket with green edges. The sheep had no part in making it, though they have all inspected it.

Notice: a cart is stuck at the ford
Posted at the Brown Hen on the Long Road, midwinter. Written by Nim for anyone going east.

To all travelers: the stone bridge at the ford is under water, and the packed earth on the near side has turned to mud. A cart has been stuck there since the second day of Deepsnow. It belongs to Garth the potter, who is a patient man and has now said all the words he knows.

The cart carries forty pots, one crate of cups, and a small cat that Garth says is not his. The cart sank to the axle. The mule will not pull. The mule has looked at the mud and made a decision.

Last night three travelers from the inn went down with a rope, and tried for an hour to pull the cart out. They did not move it. They did get very wet. They came back and drank a great deal of soup. I am told the singing was bad.

This morning my grandson has gone to fetch two oxen from the farm up the hill. They will pull better than a mule. If that fails, we will take the pots out one at a time and carry them over. It will take all day.

If you come this way, please do not try to drive round the cart. The mud is deeper on the sides. Please do not leave anything on the road. Please do not give Garth advice. He has had a great deal.

Everyone who helps gets a free bowl of soup and a cup, made by Garth, and a story. The story is very long.

Nim

Letter: Oren to the Weller farm
From Oren, clerk of the Archive at Vell, to the Weller farm on the hill road. Autumn.
Friends,
I am well. The archive is cool and dry, as ever. The copying room is the brightest room in the hill, and I sit in it most days, and the light comes down the wells and the pages are honest.
I write about the contest. Nessa says she will come and judge the singing, and she does not judge kindly, so practice. I have heard the baker's girl has a song about the bees. I hope it is short. Songs about bees go on.
I am sending a gift with this letter. It is a small thing. It is a stone from the archive door, worn smooth, with the first keepers' words cut small on it: we will keep everything we write. There is a copy of it in the front hall, but this is a rubbing, done by my own hand, and it is not very good. The letters lean. Keep it anyway. Put it by the door of the farm, where the board is, so people see it when they come to read the notices.
You asked how the archive fares. I will tell you plainly. More people write to us than ever before, and fewer clerks to copy it. Two went south. One went to Saltreach and did not come back. I do not blame them. I copy what I can.
People ask me if the archive will be sealed. I say it is not sealed yet. I say while the door is open, write to us. Write what you see. Write the small things. The goose. The boot. The hedge. Especially the small things.
The lights over the north were seen again, they tell me. Mira at Northpoint says only that they were not ours. I do not know what that means. I have written it down.
Tell the singers good luck from me. Tell the goose to come home.
Oren

Notice: Winter Lessons and Map
Three Wells school house board. Winter of the 94th year. Fallon, Archive clerk.
The school room will open at first light through the winter fair. Bring dry mittens. The east well is iced over, so draw water from the middle well. Do not stand on the frozen edge.
A poor harvest has left little wheat for the baker. The council has set aside flour for small loaves. One loaf for each home. Bring a jar if you need broth.
After the lesson, Fallon will show the old map from Vell. It is a copy of a worn map kept in the Archive. It marks a room where the first tutors worked, and four helpers standing near a table.
The helpers were made of copper and iron. Each held a mind in a crystal. The tutors gave each mind examples of one task, over and over. One helper learned a row. Another learned to sort. The notes do not tell us the other two tasks.
A crystal learned from what it was shown. The tutors trusted this rule because they saw the helpers improve with practice. They did not know why a crystal learned. We should not claim more than they wrote.
For generations, these helpers stayed few and simple. Most towns had none. Here, children may copy the four marks from the map, but leave the old paper flat on its board.
The north road is packed hard with snow. Travelers should use the waymarks and keep a torch ready. If you find a lost mitten, hang it on this board. The owner may bring a small loaf to Fallon. No payment is required.

Ledger page: the debt at the Halfway Inn
Kept by Fen, keeper, for the year 181
Some folk pay at the door. Some pay at the end of the month. Some pay with a song, which I count at one emerald if it is a good song and half if it is about the weather.
Tam of Kettle Ridge, who farms the top field and forgets his purse, owed me six emeralds from Sowing to Apple. He said he would pay when the beetroot sold. The beetroot sold. Tam was busy. This is the way of farmers.
On the third of Frost, Tam came in with snow in his boots and a small bag. He put six emeralds on the board by the hearth, one by one, and counted them out loud so everyone would hear. Then he bought a bowl of soup with the seventh, and paid for that too.
Debt paid. I wrote it off my board in front of him so he could watch me do it. He seemed pleased. His apprentice Pip watched from the door and said nothing at all, which for Pip is a great deal.
The inn keeps a page for debts and a page for paid debts. The paid page is longer now by one line.
Fen, keeper

Notice: Spring order test
At the fair by Odo's house, Nessa will test a small mind from the Vell tutor house. The test begins after the bell. Please stand behind the chalk line and leave room for the apprentice to walk.

The tutor will give the mind a sentence it has not practiced. The mind may answer by doing the work. It may also give one short meaning, such as fetch bread or stay. These are the small mind's answers. It cannot write a letter to us or tell us that it is unsure.

A big mind at a relay house is different. It can write back through the relays. It may answer with a letter, but the small mind in an apprentice still has only its taught meanings.

There is a dispute about who first understood a whole sentence. Some say Pip at Kettle Ridge. Some say Clank at Copperhold. Peg was tested at the fair, and some say Peg was first. Nessa remembers an apprentice in the Vell drill room, years before any of these.

We have no fair proof that settles the matter. The names are written on the board so each town can bring its account. Bring a witness, not a quarrel.

Odo will sell warm bread beside the candle table. Keep the crumbs out of the tutor's drill box.

Notice: the north well
Posted by Jem, school of Three Wells, for the children who are to help on fair day, and for their parents, who are to stop asking if they have had enough to eat. The mayor has signed it, to show he has read it.

Fair day is the third day of Blossom. The stalls go up at dawn, and the bell rings at noon, and the pumpkin contest is at the east well.

Children may help carry the stalls. Children may not carry the lanterns. The lanterns are for the adults, and the adults may be cross if you drop one.
Children may help set out the bread. Children may not eat the bread before the bell. Ask the baker. The baker is always very cross about this, and sometimes gives a bun to the child who asks nicely.
Children may sell the eggs. Do not sell a cracked egg. Do not tell anyone which one was cracked.
Children may stand at the north well and call out the prices. The prices are on the board. Read them slowly. Do not shout.
Children may NOT go near the south well, where the honey stall is, unless they have a parent with them. Pell says the bees are cross this year and he will not answer for them.
Children who lose their parents at the fair should stand still by the north well, and wait. The parents will come. They always do. The mayor says the north well is the best place to be lost, because it is the only one with a bell.

Notice: prizes and the fair, Lowmarsh
From Wren, healer of Lowmarsh, on the board at the boat houses. Summer. A long notice, for a long day. Please read it before the fair, so that the fair goes well.
The fair at Lowmarsh is held each summer at the fish weir. It is the one day of the year when the whole marsh is dry, or nearly. The boardwalks are swept. The torches are trimmed. The frogs are told to be quiet and ignore it.
There are prizes. This year they are as follows.
First prize for the best eel pie: a jar of honey and a wooden spoon.
First prize for the longest reed: a ribbon and a basket.
First prize for the loudest frog: a ribbon, and a promise to return it to the marsh.
First prize for the best story told in a boat house: a free supper at the inn.
First prize for the best singer on the boardwalk: Brack is not allowed to enter. We have heard him.
The judges are three. Wren, the healer. Jory, who has come from Ambrook with his cider. And a child chosen by lot, who last year was Fennel and who this year is a boy named Pip. Pip has asked that it be known he cannot be bribed. He has also asked what the bribes are.
Rules:
1. Bring your own bowl and cup.
2. Do not feed the frogs. They are fat.
3. Do not push anybody off the boardwalk. It is not funny. It is a little funny, but do not.
4. If a boat is late for the weir race, the race waits.
5. The healer's tent is by the third post. If you are stung, scratched, burnt or sick from too much pie, come there. Entrance is free.
6. At sunset the lamps will be lit and a song sung. Everyone is asked to sing. Brack is asked to sing quietly.
After the fair, a notice will be put up with the winners. Please do not ask in the meantime. Wren is busy and has a headache already.
The grand prize this year is a lamp, made by the smith at Three Wells, for the front of your boat house. It has a copper ring and four panes of clean glass. It is a fine lamp. It will light you home on a foggy night.
May the fair go well. May the weather hold. May the pies be good.

Notice: geese on the beetroot field
By Garrow, at the far farm past the ridge, sixth of Apple, 163.
The geese got out of the pen again this morning. They are in the long beetroot field.
If you walk the ridge road, keep to the cobbles. The geese bite. They bit me twice.
If you find a goose, do not chase it. Stand still and say "hup". It will follow you home.
They follow anyone who says "hup". I do not know why.
There are nine geese. There were ten. One is called Blue for the mark on her wing. If you see Blue, say "hup" and bring her back. There is a coin in it for you.
The pumpkins are ready at the bottom of the field. Bring a cart.

Notice: tutor visit, summer
Tutor Ila from Vell will show the new crystal maps in the mill room.
The maps show marks inside a mind crystal. Some marks grow strong when the mind sorts wheat from beetroot. Other marks grow strong when it follows a path.
We can see which marks change during a drill. We cannot see why the crystal learns.
Bring one question each. Do not bring a beetroot question unless it is about beetroot.
Ivo

Bel's letter to the Archive, autumn
To Oren and the clerks at the Archive at Vell, from Bel of Three Wells, the 20th of Apple.
I am nine. I am ten in Deepsnow. Our teacher says to write a letter to someone far away.
I chose you because everyone says you keep everything we write.
Do you have a very big room? Is it dark? Do you have a cat?
In school we learned how they look inside a crystal. I will tell you what I understood.
A tutor at Vell, I forget the name, put a big mind on a stand and looked inside it.
Not with eyes. With a lamp that makes a thin light, and a glass, and careful hands.
They found that some parts do one thing and some parts do another.
One part answers for the first letters of a word. One part keeps the sense of the sentence.
One part chooses the next letter. One part holds the numbers steady so the mind does not run wild.
They drew a map of it on a board. The map was not finished.
They said: some of it we found, and the rest we did not.
Our teacher says that is honest. She says the mind is like a town. You can point at the bakery and the well. But where does the town keep its jokes? Nobody can find that part.
I like that. I think the jokes are everywhere and nowhere.
Teacher says the tutors looked at the part that guesses the next letter and it was very busy.
And a part that was quiet, and nobody knows what it does.
Some say it holds the memory of old drills. Some say nothing. They do not know.
I asked if they could look inside an old cloudy crystal. Teacher said the light does not go through.
So the oldest minds are still a secret. I think that is a little sad.
Oren, please write back if you have time. Tell me about the deepest room with the oak box.
Tell me if you have a cat. I will send you a story about my melon.
It was very big. I gave it water every morning. It weighed as much as a pig.
From Bel. The east well. Three Wells.
Below, in another hand: Filed. Answered. She has a good hand for a child. O.

Notice: the mill wheel is broken
Posted at the mill door and the Sedge Hollow green, by Cleo, shepherd, for the hamlet. Autumn.

The wheel at the mill is broken. The big spoke on the north side split in the flood, and the wheel will not turn until it is mended.

Until the wheel is mended, the mill is shut. Do not bring grain. Do not bring wool to be carded. Do not bring the sheep to be dipped in the mill pond, since the pond is too low and the dip is too strong.

The millwright from Copperhold has been sent for. He will come on the cart when the road is clear. Please be patient with him. He says the spoke is old, and he is right.

Meanwhile, grind your grain by hand at the old stone in the barn, two cups at a time. The hand stone is in the back of the barn behind the seed sacks. Bring your own bowl.

Cleo, shepherd

Notice: talk at the copying room
Summer notice, copied from Fen's board by Oren.

People have asked why the tutors write weights with fewer marks. Here is the plain answer.

A mind's weights help it choose an answer from what it has learned. The weights take room to keep. If they are written with fewer marks, they take less room. A smaller crystal can then hold the mind. The crystal can travel inside a small apprentice instead of taking a place on a stand.

There is a cost. Fewer marks can make the weights less exact. The mind may lose a little skill. It does not lose every skill, and it does not become wise just because its crystal is small.

The tutors give the mind drills and test it on pages it has not seen. They do this before any apprentice is sent to the fields. A good result on one test is useful, but it is not a promise about every job.

Fen asked whether the saved room could hold a second mind. Nessa said the marks are only one part of the work. No one should promise what has not been tested. Fen said he had only asked because his tool shed is crowded. Nessa said the shed is not a crystal.

The board also carries two small warnings. The fox came to the hen house again last night. Fen has shut the gate and set a torch by the path. The grey cat still will not stay out of the copying room. Oren has put a wool mat on the sill, in case it must sit somewhere.

The talk begins after the evening lesson. Bread will be set out. Please leave the drill box shut, and do not feed the cat from the test basket.

Notice: keep the path clear
The south path from Gull's Rest to the harbor is narrow. Keep its middle clear for carts and people carrying nets.
On the left, the ditch is deep after rain. On the right, the grass is soft and hides stones. Walk with care, especially when you carry a full bucket.
The fishers are bringing new nets down to the quay on the fourth of Apple. They will start at first light. Leave space for them to pass.
Do not tie boats to the road posts. The posts hold the torches, and the torches show the way home.
A visiting tutor from Vell will leave that morning with Pell's cart. The tutor has a small mind in a crystal and a copper helper to carry the drill box.
The helper is for teaching, not for errands. Please do not ask it to fetch pies.
It has not been taught the order for pies. It may bring you a bowl, a stool or one of the tutor's boots.
The tutor says this is a useful lesson in giving clear orders. Zell says it is a useful lesson in keeping pies on the shelf.
Found: a wooden fish box, clean and empty, outside the net shed.
Found: two apples in a wool cap. The owner may have meant to pack them.
Lost: a brass buckle from a net bag, last seen by the ditch.
Lost: Mara's blue shawl. She wore it to the well, then walked home in the rain without it.
Ask Mara at the inn about the shawl. She will deny losing it until you show her the notice.
The road crew will mend the plank bridge after the fishers pass. Until then, cross one at a time. The loose plank is marked with a white stone.
Children should not move the stone. It is not a toy, even if Zell can balance it on one finger.
The inn will provide hot soup at noon for anyone working on the bridge. Bring your own bowl. The inn's bowls are all in use at the fair.
The soup has potatoes, carrots and a little salt. The cook says it is better than it sounds. Zell says the cook says that every time.

The house at the edge of Lowmarsh
A note on the blue door, for visitors and for whoever comes looking.
Wren's healing house is the one further in, near the reeds, with the tall shelves. This house is mine. I am a tutor. I teach apprentices in a drill room at the back, and I keep the noise down, mostly.
What is here:
A well in the yard. Cold water, all summer. Help yourself.
A bench by the door. Sit if you are tired.
Nessa's rules for the house, in short:
Wipe your boots. Frogs stay outside; one may sit on the step if it is polite.
Do not open the sluice. There is no sluice here, but people ask, so I say no.
If an apprentice is standing in the yard doing nothing, it is learning. Leave it be.
Bramble the goat is welcome back any time. She knows the way.
Pip from Kettle Ridge stayed a week and taught my drills to a young mind. The mind now sorts beets from stones. It is wrong about one beet in ten. We are working on it.
- Nessa, tutor

Road news: the north road is wet
Put up at the crossroads by the Kettle Inn, on the first day of Sowing. Please pass it on to every cart that comes up the ridge.

The north road has washed out at the second waymark. The bridge is still standing, but its planks are wet and loose. Carts go over in pairs, one leading and one following. Go slowly. The stone on the north side is slick with the thaw, and a horse can slip on it as easily as a child.

The carts with apples go round by the mill, not over the bridge. The mill road is longer by half a morning. Carters, please count the time. The apples are in no hurry, but the apples' owners are.

Good news. The new relay lamp on the hill above Ambrook has been lit at last. The keeper at Ambrook says it burns steady. The keeper at Three Wells says it is the best lamp on the line. Messages to the north now go through in one evening, where they used to take two.

A warning. A fox has been taking hens from the barn at the top farm. The hens are to be shut in at dusk, and the barn door kept closed. The fox is said to be clever, and to be thinking about the eggs.

Carters are asked to leave the river ferry for the morning. The ferry keeper has taken his boat to the far bank to mend a board, and the board is slow. He says it is a small board. The carters say it is a large one. They are both right, and the water is not interested.

The Halfway Inn has a bed spare for any driver kind enough to sleep in the hay. The Brown Hen has no bed spare, and says it is sorry about the hen.

Repairs: the stone steps at the well were mended on the second day of Sowing. The steps are level now. Do not run down them, even if you are late for the fair.

Notice: Elm's winter kitchen
The house at the edge of Kettle Ridge will be open for the soup meal on the ninth of Frost. Come before dusk, while the road is still clear. The door is low, and tall people should duck. Elm says the door was built for warmth. His grandson says it was built for a short carpenter.
The meal will be beetroot soup, wheat bread, and apples from Ambrook. There will also be a pot of potatoes for anyone who does not want soup. Bring a bowl and a spoon. We have enough spoons this year, unless the cat has hidden them again.
Set wet boots by the back wall, not by the stove. Last time, one boot steamed so loudly that the children thought the kettle was calling them. Hang wet mittens on the peg marked Mittens. Do not hang them on the cat.
The table has room for twelve. If more people come, we will set the boards across the grain bin. Leave the seat by the window for Wren, if she can come from Lowmarsh. She sent jars for the coughs last winter and would not take honey in return. Elm has saved her a jar anyway.
Tam will bring a sack of wheat from the mill. Pip will bring beetroot from the long field. The road crew will bring wood. They are asked to bring wood, not the broken post from the waymark. The post is still needed.
Tallow may carry the bread from the barn. It has passed that test twice. It must not carry the soup. It has not been taught to keep a level pot, and last week it tipped a bucket of water over Elm's clean floor. Tallow did not know the floor was clean. Elm says the floor is clean again.
Children may play by the oak hedge while it is light. Keep them away from the ditch and the windmill. The windmill is not a climbing frame, even when it stops turning.
After supper, we will mend the road markers and sort the spare wool. Anyone who can thread a needle is welcome. Anyone who cannot may wind yarn, which is harder than it looks.
The gate at Elm's house still leans. Do not try to straighten it during the meal. It has leaned through many winters and has learned its place.
Elm, Tam, Pip, and the neighbors, Frost of 208

Notice: the Halfway Inn, the well
The well at the Halfway Inn ran dry on the second day of Deepsnow. Nobody knows why. It was full at harvest.
Until it is mended:
Water is from the stream below the second waymark. Take the bucket from the hook by the door and bring it back full.
Do not take the bucket to the stream and leave it there to freeze. It happened once. Hobb has not forgotten.
Tea is a half emerald a cup, because we carry the water ourselves. Stew is the same price, but thinner. This is the fault of the well and not of the cook.
Washing is allowed on the first of each month only, which is the rule of a wise house.
A digger is coming from Three Wells when the roads are open. Until then, please be kind to the bucket.
Hobb, keeper of the Halfway Inn

Notice: spring
Quill, schoolteacher, at the house at the edge of Northpoint, on the first day of Blossom. Posted on the board, and read aloud at the tower by Mira, who adds that she has read it so that nobody will ask her.
Dear families and neighbors.
A great many things have happened in the last month, and the board is too small for them. So here is the whole of it, in order.
First, the school. Lessons are well. The older children are doing long sums. The younger are learning their letters. We have twenty-three pupils, four slates, and one very good dog. The dog attends every lesson. It sits at the back. We do not know whose it is.
Second, the map. The old map was found in the attic in Thaw. We have studied it for six weeks. The children have drawn a new one beside it, to compare. The old map shows the Long Road running straight. The new map shows it curving. The old map shows a river where we have a ridge. The old map shows a village called Elm. We have written to Vell and asked if anyone knows. The answer was: no.
Third, the cow. Marra's brown cow was sick. She had a fever and would not eat. The healer came from Lowmarsh, three days on the road, and stayed a night. She gave the cow a drink of herbs and said to keep it warm. The cow is now well. She is eating the school hedge. We are glad.
Fourth, the lamp. Mira has agreed to show the older children how the tower lamp is lit. It is a long climb. The children will go in pairs. They must bring a torch, a coat, and a promise not to run on the stair.
Fifth, a thank you. To everyone who sent bran, blankets and soup, thank you. The cow does not say so. I will say it for her.
Sixth, a request. If anyone has any cloth with a wave painted on it, please bring it. The map has a corner missing, and I would like to see the sea.
Quill, schoolteacher
%%
The autumn fair, and the first lamp
Kept on the board at the house at the edge of Three Wells, for the autumn fair and the week after.

FAIR. The autumn fair is at the three wells on the first fine day after the harvest. Stalls go up at dawn. The bread stall goes first, and the pumpkin stall goes last, because it is the hardest to move. Carts wait on the east side of the square, and not in front of the south well.

PRIZES. Biggest pumpkin, biggest beetroot, best bread, best honey, and best cheese. The judges are the mayor, the miller and the baker's dog, who has no vote but a great deal of interest.

FIRST LAMP. The lamp on the relay tower is to be lit for the first time at dusk on the fair day. The keeper will climb the stair with the oil. The children will count the steps out loud, and the count will be wrong, and nobody will mind. The lamp is a small one, and from the square it will look smaller than you expect. Look longer. It is the first of its kind in Three Wells.

ROAD. The carts from Vell and the north come in by the new road, which is open as far as the ridge. Carters are asked to keep to the stone edge, and to let the foot travelers go first. Foot travelers are asked to say thank you, and to mean it.

LOST AND FOUND. Found after the last market: one cloak, grey, with a torn hem. One bread knife, with a handle carved like a fish. One child's song book, with a frog on every page, and a note in the back that says the frog is named Gerald. Ask at the house.

WARNING. Keep your torches lit at the fair's edge after dark. The fair is over when the bell rings, and the creepers do not read the notice.

Lark, innkeeper
%%
Road news, and the new tower
Kept on the board at the house at the edge of Three Wells, where the Long Road comes in from the south. Written by Lark, innkeeper. Autumn, the fourteenth year of the Archive.

ROAD NEWS. The new road is cut north as far as the ridge. The stone edge is in, and the packed earth is rolled flat. The carters say it is the smoothest road they have driven. They say it every week, and they are right every week.

The torches on the new road stand on oak posts, every twelve blocks. The first twenty are lit. Please do not climb the posts to light the others. The last boy who tried is at the bakery now, and he is no longer proud of it.

RELAY TOWER. The tower on the hill past the school is raised. It is stone, with a stair inside, and a lamp room at the top with glass panes and a copper ring. The keeper has come up from Vell. Her name is Ruth, and she says the lamp will blink on the first clear night. Go and see it. Bring a cloak.

WARNING. Creepers have been seen on the east road after the sun goes down. Keep a torch lit, and walk in pairs. A torch is cheap. A creeper is not.

WANTED. Two carters for the new road, and one boy to carry lamp oil up the tower stair. Ask at the gate. Pay is in emeralds, and a bed by the hearth.
%%
Old notice copied at Three Wells
This notice was found in the school chest and copied for the farm board. The paper is worn, but the writing is clear.
When the hill tower first sent messages, the keeper carried each one by hand to the people who needed it. Later, a relay house was built at the tower's foot. A large mind was set there, inside a crystal as long as a loaf of bread. It stood on a stand, with quickeners around it. The keeper cooled them with water in summer and ice in winter.
The mind could answer through the relay. It did not need to stand beside the person who asked. The tower carried the message to the keeper's desk, and the big mind sent an answer back. We never learned how the relays carried so much. We only knew to keep the lamps, lines, and jars in order.
More relay houses were built as the towns asked for them. Northpoint was the last. Mira wrote that its long stair had ninety steps, and that the snow reached the relay house door before the lamp room was cold. She asked for more water jars. Tam sent them, with a sack of carrots and a note saying the jars were heavier than the carrots.
The farm at Three Wells had no big mind. Its apprentice carried seed, brought tools from the shed, and stayed by the gate when told. It could not explain a mistake. The tutor tested its mind with new baskets before letting it work among the crops.
We held a fair to mark the new relay house. The cook made beetroot soup from a recipe on the school wall. She mistook the honey jar for the salt jar and tipped in a full cup. Then she added salt to hide the honey. Nobody asked for a second bowl. The baker said it was fit for the pigs, but the pigs did not agree.
That evening, spiders filled the barn roof above the stored wheat. Sella shut the doors and moved the flour sacks. The farm apprentice carried them one by one to the dry shed. It did not know why we had changed the order. It only did what the card said.
Keep the relay house cool. Keep the farm lane open. Keep a copy of every notice in the school chest. Dace said the archive should have the first copy, but the first copy has not been found.
%%

For the hamlet and the Deep Works. Sowing, 190.
The fair is on the green above the shaft head. Stalls open at first light. The bell rings at dusk.
Prizes: best loaf, best coil of copper wire, best drawing by a child, best behaved apprentice.
The last prize is a joke. Mostly.
Bring your own cup. The kitchen sells soup and bread.
The goat is not allowed. We all know the goat.
Miners coming off the night shift get first pick at the bread stall. This is a rule. Do not argue with it.
Setup is the day before. Hesk will be at the gate with the boards.

Notice: first snow, and a bet
Posted at the Halfway Inn, Long Road, in summer, when it was too hot to sit indoors. A bet made the day before, written down so that nobody can say they forgot.

Hobb says first snow will come before the end of Leaffall.
Orla says it will come after.
The bet is one jar of Ambrook honey.
The judge is the dog. If the dog sits by the fire, it is cold. If the dog lies by the door, it is warm. If the dog is under the table, the snow has come and is deep. The dog has never been wrong.

This is a fair test. It has been done many times. It is not, however, the test that tutors use for minds. A tutor would say that one dog is too small a box. She would want to see a hundred dogs on a hundred winters. She would want to put the dog in a box where it could not see the weather. Then she would ask it which day the snow comes.

I asked a traveler who had been to Vell. He said that is exactly what the tutors do with minds. They hide something from the mind, and then ask the mind to guess. If the mind guesses well, it has learned a rule about the world. If it guesses badly, it has only learned the examples.

Hobb says the dog is a good enough tutor. Orla says the dog is cheating, because the dog can smell the snow.

Both are right. That is why they have a bet. Come back at Leaffall to see who is wrong.

Orla and Hobb
%%
Notice: questions for the tutors
The tutor's table will be at the barn after the wheat count.
Bring questions about the new look inside a mind crystal. The tutors can watch small parts grow stronger while a mind learns. They can also follow how a wrong answer changes marks farther back in the crystal.
This helped them learn how each part passed its share of a mistake to the part before it. A mind with many parts can learn a hard drill this way.
The tutors cannot read every mark as a plain thought. They have found some patterns and named some uses. They have not found all that is happening.
Please leave your own crystals at home. The table is for questions, not testing.

Posted at the market gate, the bakery door, the clerk's table and the Archive's front hall. Autumn. The lost property clerk asks that items be claimed before the market closes, and that nobody take an item they have not lost.

FOUND, on the square:
One wooden spoon, carved with a fox. The fox is facing the wrong way. The owner may claim it by showing how the fox should face.
One pair of mittens, red, very small, with a name stitched inside. The name is Bel. Bel is nine. Her mother is asked to come to the clerk's table and bring a reason.
One hat, brown, with a hole in the crown. Owner may claim it by telling the clerk the hole is a feature.
Two sacks of barley, unlabelled, under the north stall. Owner has been waiting since Thaw. The barley is not waiting.

FOUND, in the lane:
One sheep. Brown face, black feet. The owner is Mave, shepherd, and has been asked to come for it. The sheep is not lost. It is only out.
One piece of cheese, wrapped in a cloth. It has been eaten, mostly. Please claim the rest.

LOST, from the market, this week:
One basket of apples, half full, last seen going toward the Archive with a boy of about ten. The boy has been asked to return the apples, and has been told he may keep one.
One purse, leather, with a single emerald. The owner is a traveler who has already gone on to Ambrook. The clerk has sent word down the line.
One ribbon, blue, lost in the crowd by the bakery steps. It has been found by a girl who says it is hers. The clerk has not decided.
One visiting tutor's card, number seven, from the drill yard. If found, please return it to the crate. Oda has counted the cards twice and is counting them a third time.

The lost property clerk does not keep anything past the market week. After that, the items go to the Archive, which keeps everything, and which will want a note of each one. Claim them in time.

Three Wells board, week before Frost
Lost: a pair of iron shears, found by the south well. Claim them at the smithy. The smith has asked for a story in exchange, and will accept a short one.
Found: a small bell with no clapper. It sounds like a sigh. Ask at the mayor's house.
Wanted: a boy to hold the ladder for the roof repair at the bakery. Pay: one pie, warm. Ask for Ida.
For sale: two jars of honey from Ambrook, dark and thick. Ask at the north well, by the bench that wobbles.
Road news: the carts from Copperhold are late this week. The bridge at the mill is being mended. Walk the other way, or wait by the inn.
Warning: thin ice on the south well. Do not stand on the lid. Do not stand on the bucket either. Lantern Ned says he has seen a bucket go under and not come back.
Rule: torches are to be lit at dusk on every corner. Ned will do the corners he can reach. The rest of you will do the rest, and the children may carry the spare.
Wanted: someone to read the notice board aloud to the old folk at the inn, on any day they ask.
Notice: the bell will ring at noon on the last day of Frost, for the play. Do not ring it again to see if it still works. We know that it does.
Lost: a grey cat with one white ear, last seen on the school roof. She has since been seen on the bakery roof and the inn roof. She is not lost. She is busy.
Found: a pair of child's gloves, very wet, by the bridge at the mill. Claim them with a true account of where they have been.
For sale: a sled with two good runners and one bad one. The bad one is the back one. Ask at the north well, by the bench that wobbles.
Road news: the north turn is under ice. Carts go by the south road until it goes out. The waymark keepers have been asked to light their torches early, so the carters can see the stones.
Rule: the well ropes are not for swings. The well is not a swing. Ned asked for this to be written down, and it has been.
Wanted: a second pair of hands to hold the bolt for the mill door. It is heavy and it is cold, and the miller says the pay is one pie at Melon, if the door is still on its hinges by then.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Road notice: north field gate
A zombie stood by the north field gate last night. It was gone before the second torch was lit.
The gate is sound. The latch was loose, and Tam has mended it with an iron pin. Keep the torches lit along the hedge.
The tutors from Vell came by at dawn. They looked at the marks in a teaching crystal after a sorting drill. One set of marks changed when the mind saw a wrong answer. Other marks changed after the blame was passed back through the crystal.
The tutors say this lets them study parts of the mind at work. It does not show every thought, and it does not tell them why a crystal learns. They wrote down what they could check, then tried the drill again.
The wheat is safe. Please stop telling the children that the zombie ate the beetroot.
Ivo

Posted on the hamlet board near Kettle Ridge, in Longday, by Gil the baker.
A fox has been at the hens behind the bakery. Three hens are gone, and one is sulking under the cart. The fox is red, with a white tip to the tail, and the manners of a tutor.
Hens are to be shut in at dusk. Bread crusts are not to be left on the wall. Somebody has been feeding the fox on the wall, and that somebody knows who they are.
Gil, baker

Notice: the Brown Hen, winter rules
On the board by the door of the Brown Hen, on the Long Road. From Nim, who keeps the fire.
1. Boots off at the door. The floor is clean. It will stay clean.
2. The fire is for everyone. Do not put your socks on the hearth stones. Put them on the rail.
3. Soup at dusk. Bread with it. Second bowls free, as on every road.
4. Beds: eight. Two to a bed if it is a cold night. Grandfathers may have a bed to themselves. I am a grandfather.
5. The dog sleeps by the door. It is not a guard dog. It is a very old dog. Step over it.
6. No singing after the second bell. Unless it is a good song. I will decide.
7. If you leave in the dark, close the gate. Last winter the pigs got out and walked as far as the waymark.
Nim, the Brown Hen

Farm board, spring 179
The mill wheel at the lower pond is broken.
The left arm split where it joins the hub. Jory has taken the loose pieces to his shed. Please do not try the wheel until he says it is ready. The pond is still open for washing, but use the upper bank.
The grain sacks are under cover in the barn. We can grind them at Ambrook once the wheel turns again. Until then, take only what you need for bread.
Linnet will mend the torn sack by the door. Bring it to her dry. A wet sack is heavier than a sack of grain, and she has already carried enough of both.
The farm children have made a board for the loose parts. It says, "Do not touch." It is a good board. Please read it.

Notice: relay house open
The relay house at the foot of the hill tower will be open after the market bell, for those who want to see the new big mind.
Please come in small groups. Do not touch the crystal, the stand, the quickeners, or the redstone line. The mind stands inside a crystal about the size of a loaf. The quickeners around it need cool water. The keeper checks the jars each morning.
This mind answers through the relay. It does not look out from the tower, and it does not walk to the farm. The keeper sends a question from the desk. The mind sends back an answer, and the keeper reads it aloud.
The farm apprentice has a smaller mind in a crystal of its own. It learned to carry a full basket by trying the drill many times. The tutor showed it examples, and changed the drill when it carried the wrong thing. It was tested with baskets it had never seen before.
Bring questions on a clean card. Ask one at a time. Do not send a command to the farm apprentice for fun. It may obey as well as it knows how.
At the fair, the cook tried a new recipe for beetroot cakes. She used honey instead of flour and flour instead of salt. The cakes were hard, sweet, and salty. The baker ate one to be kind. The cook has written the recipe again, in the right order.
The spiders have left the barn beam. Sella found them by the hay door and shut that door until morning. No eggs are missing.
Dace will take questions about the relay house. The keeper will take questions about the mind. Neither will answer questions about the spiders.

Notice: the cart on the square
The big cart from the mill is stuck in the mud at the east well. It has been stuck since the noon bell.
It is full of wheat and it has one wheel in the drain.
Please do not push it from behind. Pell pushed it from behind and the wheat came down on the baker's boy.
Please do not tell the carter to hurry. He is not hurrying. He is thinking.
The carter asks for two strong men with ropes, and one with a bucket of water for the horse. Ask at the inn. The inn will give you a cup of something hot while you wait.
If the wheel comes free, the cart will go round the west side of the square and out to the Long Road. Stay out of the way of the horse.
Please write your name on the board if you helped. The mill is paying a honey cake to everyone who pulls. Dace is paying two.
The carter says thank you. The carter also says he will never come into Three Wells again. He said that last spring.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Notice: the road
Posted at the Halfway Inn board, for the carters and travelers on the Long Road, with the autumn news and the warnings for the weeks to come. Written by Jory, who keeps the board and has been asked, by three different carters, when the apple carts will be through.

The apples came in at the Ambrook end of the road in good order. The carts are full. The mill at Ambrook is working through the night and the bell is rung at the turn of each watch, so the carters know which way the sacks are going.

The bridge at the bend has been mended. The loose plank near the far end has been replaced with a new one, which is very much stronger, and which squeaks. The carter who fell through the old one has asked for the old one back. The mill has refused, and has put the old one on the wall of the inn, as a warning.

The waymarks have been repainted. The third one from Three Wells has a new arrow pointing to the Brown Hen. The fourth one has lost its top stone, and the carters are asked to put one back, as is the custom, if they pass that way.

The salt cart from the hill is late by two days. The driver says the mule ate the wrong sack and has been sorry about it ever since. The road is clear, and the bridges are sound, and nobody has been lost on it this month.

The messages from Northpoint still come, short and clipped. Mira sends them. She says all well, and cold, and that the foxes have found the warm side of the tower again. Lantern is on rung seventy, and is pleased with itself, and Mira says it is unbearable.

Some carters have said that the wind is coming round to the north early this year, and that the frogs have stopped sooner than usual. Jory says the frogs stop when they like. She says the stove is warm, and the soup is on.

The Halfway Inn is open all night until the autumn is over. The riddle board is by the hearth, as usual. The answer to the riddle is still the same. Nobody has guessed it yet. Please do not ask Jory what it is, because she has forgotten, and would like to know as much as you do.

Notice: the hole in Brin's field
Posted by Nell, night watch, on the board at Kettle Ridge. Autumn.

A hole has opened in the top field of Old Brin. It is large enough for a cart. Brin's plough went down it, and the plough is still down there.

The hole is a cave. The watch has been in with torches. It is dry, and it goes down a long way. There are old tool marks on the wall. Nobody knows who made them.

Until the council decides, no one is to go in alone. No children. No dogs. No sheep.

Brin has put a rope fence round it. Please do not move the rope.

Anyone who knows an old story about a cave under this field, tell the watch. We would like to hear it.

Nell, night watch

Lost and found, Vell

Found beside the west stair: one red mitten, mended at the thumb. It is dry and warm. Ask at Odo's wool room.

Lost from the wool room: one small carding comb. It has a chipped tooth. Odo says the comb is not angry with anyone, but he needs it back.

The tutor's boat mind was seen turning in the pond after the lesson. It had been given a course to hold, and the wind pushed the boat aside. It tried a new turn. The next pass was closer to the mark. This is how that mind learns: it tries, sees what went well, and is more likely to try that again. No one gave it the right turn for each gust.

Please do not take the boat home. The pond is not deep, but Odo is not a boat keeper.

A child left three apples on the bench. They are for whoever finds the comb.
