Notice: how to write an order card
An order card is the plan of a job, written before the work begins. It is the same card the tutors use on the bench, and it is the same card the apprentices use at the end of the day, to see what they did and what they missed.

Write the job on the card in plain words. Put one step on each line, and begin each line with the sign of an order, which is the arrow. After the order, write the meaning, which must be one of the eight. The eight are come, stay, fetch, haul, store, farm, build and home.

For example, a card for the stone wall at the top field may read:

> fetch the stones from the cart
= fetch stones
> lay the stones in a line along the field edge
= build
> carry the rubble to the far ditch
= haul
> put the spare stones back on the shelf
= store
> come back to the gate when the wall is done
= come

Read the card aloud before you start. If a line makes no sense, the apprentice must not guess. It must ask the tutor, or it must pick one of the eight that is nearest, and then it must check that the work is still going where the card says it should. Work that goes off the card is the most common mistake in the crews. It is also the easiest to fix, if you catch it early.

Do not write the card in a hurry. A card written in a hurry becomes a card that is not followed. Take the time. The stones will wait.

Written by Willa, potter, for the apprentices of the crews on the hill, and for anyone who wants to know how they think.
%%
Notice: the buried cave under the Hollow
Posted at the Ruddle farm gate and copied to the board at Copperhold, by order of the council. Written in autumn.
A cave has been found under the north field of the Ruddle farm. It was found by Ben Ruddle's plough, which went through the turf one morning and dropped the team of two oxen into the hole up to their necks. Both oxen are well. The plough is not.
The cave is dry and cool inside. It goes back about forty paces from the hole, and then turns to the left. The walls are of grey stone with pale streaks. The floor is sand. There is a stone shelf on the far wall, with nothing on it but a little dust and a child's marble.
The council asks that no one enter the cave alone. Take a lamp, and a rope, and a friend. The rope is to be tied to the post at the top, which the Ruddle family has set in the earth beside the hole.
Please do not take anything out of the cave. The council will decide what is to be kept. Ben Ruddle asks, however, that people stop asking him whether he has found gold. He has not. He has found the marble, and he has given it back to the shelf.
The cave will be surveyed in Frost by the masons of Copperhold. A rope fence will be put round the hole until then. Stay outside it. The oxen are not to be shown the hole again.
Any finds to be brought to the council hall. A reward of two emeralds for each one that is not already known.
%%
Notice: the wall the creeper blew
Notice from the north wall of the Northpoint relay house, pinned to the gate. Spring.

A creeper came up the snow path on the last night of Frost. It walked straight to the north wall, where the stones are low, and it blew up. The wall has a gap in it the width of a cart. No one was hurt. The hens were not so lucky, but they are back in the coop now, and they are cross.

The wall will be mended this week. The masons say it will take three days. Hold the gap closed with a board until then.

The mind in the house has been asked to watch the wall at night. Its order was given at the desk, written out in full: watch the north wall, and call the keeper if anything comes near it.

The apprentice, Lantern, did not understand the order. It was told: watch the north wall. It walked to the north wall and stood there, very still, for the whole night, facing the wall. It did not look at the snow, or at the path, or at the creeper, when the creeper came. So the creeper came. The apprentice did not call anyone.

The order was not clear. It should have said: watch for creepers on the path. That is what we meant. Mira says the fault is hers. Lantern says nothing, as always.

From now on all orders at the wall will be written in full. Watch the north wall for creepers on the path. Call the keeper if you see one.

Mira, keeper of the Northpoint relay
%%
Notice: the waymark and the apprentice
On the board at the Brown Hen, deep winter of 206. Written by Nim, who has run the inn for forty years and has never seen the like.

I will say it once, for everyone who has asked at the bar.

Yes, there is an apprentice standing in the road. It has been there since first light. It is not stuck. It is not lost. It is standing in front of the cart from Saltreach, with its arms out, and it will not move.

It belongs to the carter, a man named Orrin, who says that he told it to "stand at the waymark and wait for the cart." There is a waymark here. The cart is here. The apprentice is at the waymark, waiting, and the cart is behind it. The cart cannot go on. The apprentice cannot see why it should.

I have seen a lot of things on this road. A pig in a bath. A mayor in a barrel. A bridge that fell down while a sheep was crossing it. I have not seen a cart held up by its own helper.

Orrin tried "come." The apprentice did not come. Orrin tried "stay." It was already staying. Orrin tried "home." The apprentice stood in the road with its arms out and did not move. I am told a small mind has eight meanings and no way to say it does not understand. It can only do what it thinks it was told.

My granddaughter, who is eleven, went out and took it by the hand. She said, "The cart wants to go by." She walked it to the side of the road. The apprentice stood there. The cart went by.

The apprentice is still standing at the side of the road. It has a better view. It is waiting for the cart to come back, and it will wait a long time.

Lesson: when you give an order, say it so that the order can end. "Wait at the waymark until the cart comes, then come." My granddaughter says that is a lot of words. I say, so is a long road.

Soup is on. Orrin is eating. He is not saying much.

Nim
%%
Board: road news and the inn tally
The Long Road, Frost 176
The road is open both ways. The stream bridge past the second waymark is mended with oak until spring, so go slow with a full cart. Ice in the ruts after dark.
Torches on the posts every twelve blocks. If one is out, tell the inn and we will fix it. If three are out in a row, tell us twice.
The riddle board by the hearth has a new riddle. The old one was answered by a child from Three Wells in one evening. She would not say how.
Beds at the Halfway Inn: twelve. Taken tonight: nine. Two are kept for the relay keepers, who come and go at strange hours and should not be woken.
Lost this month: one brown dog called Nip (found, asleep in the wood store, do not ask). One glove, left. One glove, right, found. They do not match, but they are friends now.
Found: a tin whistle, a red scarf, a sack of turnips nobody will claim, and a small boot.
Soup is beetroot until the carrots come up. Bread is fresh at dawn and gone by noon.
Fen, keeper

Notice: results from the small mind
Written after the test, by Fen, with Nessa's account checked.

The small crystal sorted the new basket in the teaching room. It put the apples in one tray, the carrots in another, and the beetroot in the last. It made one mistake. One apple went with the carrots.

The larger mind sorted the same basket with no mistake. Nessa says this is one test, not a rule for every day. Both minds learned from many examples. Neither mind had seen this basket before.

The smaller crystal has weights written in fewer marks. That lets it fit in a small apprentice. Some detail is lost when the weights are shortened, so a little skill may be lost too. It is not a new way of teaching. It is a way to save room.

Fen asked if a small crystal can be made larger again by putting more marks back. Nessa said that would not put lost skill back by itself. The mind would need more good drills, and then another fair test.

The apprentices watched without calling out. The cat watched from under the bench and stole no fruit. It did not stay under the bench when the room emptied.

Please return all trays to the tutor's house. Do not leave fruit by the drill boards. The fox is still near Fen's hens.
%%
Notice: fair and crystal maps
The summer fair opens by the well after the wheat carts have passed. The beetroot stall will stand beside Tam's table. The miller will sell flour if the wheel is turning by then.
At the tutor stall, Ila will show a drawing of marks seen inside a mind crystal. She watched one small part change while the mind learned to sort wheat and beetroot. Then she followed changes made by a wrong answer through several parts of the crystal.
Each part passes some blame back to the part before it. This lets the whole mind learn from mistakes that a single, simple crystal could not manage. The mind still has to be drilled and tested. A drawing of its marks is not the same as knowing all that happens inside it.
Ila says some marks seem to help with sorting. Other marks seem to help with remembering the drill. She is careful to say seem. She can show when marks change, but not explain every mark or why the crystal learns at all.
There will be no touching the tutor's crystal. Children may ask questions after the flour count. One question each, and no asking whether a mind dreams. Ila says she does not know.

Notice: a gift from Vell
Pinned to the board at Three Wells. Thaw of 201, after the water went down. By Merle, healer, because the mayor says she writes a clearer hand.

Last night a cart came down from Vell with a long crate. In the crate was a gift for Three Wells, from the tutors: an apprentice, made of copper and iron. Its mind came from a bigger mind, written out again in fewer marks, so that it fits the small crystal in its chest. The tutors say it is a little slower than the big mind it came from, and as good at the work they have tested.
Its name is Jug. Dace named it. Dace says it is because it carries things and is expected to be quiet.
Jug will clear the mud from the low street. Please do not give it bread.

Notice: the winter road, closed and open
The road to Vell is closed at the bottom of the hill, where the snow has drifted over the first waymark. It will be open again when the drifts are cut down, which the carters say will be before Longnight, and the mayor says will be after. Please believe the carters.

The road to the north is open, as far as the second bridge. Beyond the bridge, the ice is thin, and the cart must be led across by hand. Walk the horse. Do not walk the cart. Do not walk anything that cannot be carried.

The road to Copperhold is open, all the way up the stair. The stair is clear of ice, because the miners cleared it themselves at dawn, with shovels and a great deal of swearing. The lamps on the stair are lit at dusk, and the lamp-keeper asks that you come up with your own light, in case the lamp goes out.

Anyone who needs help getting to the inn at the crossroads may ring the bell at the post. The bell is answered by whoever is nearest, and that is usually Ivo, who has a cart, and who will not ask for an emerald, because he has none to spare this week.

Ivo, trader, for the hamlet board.

Warning: the marsh post lamp is broken
Pinned on the healing house door, and on the post by the reed beds. Put up by Wren, and by Teague the boatman, who says he will not go past the post until someone does something about it.

The relay lamp on the marsh post is broken. A gust in the night took the top glass out, and the copper ring is bent. Nobody has been up the post yet. Do not climb it. The stair on the post is wet, and the rungs are green with slime. Two boys went up it on the first day, and one of them is still complaining about his hands.

The first keepers of the marsh put the lamp up when the Long Road came through. It has been lit every dusk for as long as anyone can remember, until this week.

Until the lamp is mended, the marsh has no relay. Messages for the healing house come by boat from Ambrook now. In a good wind that takes two days. The small minds for the fields come the same way, in their crystals, wrapped in wool. They are waiting at Ambrook. They are safe, and they are not going anywhere.

Teague will mend the lamp when the wind drops. He says so every morning. Please do not ask him about it before he has had his breakfast.

The keepers at Ambrook and Three Wells know we are dark. They are watching for our lamp, and they have sent word that they miss it. Wren has said the marsh misses them too.
%%
Winter rules at the harbor house
Written by Corin, to be read aloud on the first night of snow, and again whenever someone forgets. Pinned inside the door of the house at the edge of Saltreach, next to the hooks.
These rules are not many. Winter is long, and long rules are forgotten by the third week.
The door. Shut it. Behind you. The wind off the sea has no manners and will come in if asked. If the door is open for a count of ten, the stove loses a quarter of its heat. Corin counted.
The stove. Feed it with wood from the pile by the north wall, never the pile by the south wall. The south pile is boat oak. Leave it. If you are not sure which pile you are at, smell it: oak smells sweet, pine smells sharp, and driftwood smells of the sea and smokes.
The hooks. Wet coats go on the high hooks by the stove. Dry coats go on the low hooks by the door. If a coat is on the wrong hook, it will be wet by morning and the owner will say words.
The lamp. There is one lamp in the window facing the harbor. It is lit at dusk and kept lit until the last boat is in. In the long winter there are no boats, but we light it anyway. It is polite to the sea.
The pot. There is always a pot of fish soup on the back of the stove. Anyone may eat from it. Anyone who eats from it must add something: a potato, a beetroot, a handful of barley, a fish if they have one. Last week someone added a boot. We found it. We took it out. We are still not sure who.
The cat. The cat belongs to no one and sleeps where it likes. It likes the warmest chair. It has decided the chair is its own. The chair is Corin's. Corin has decided to stand.
The helper. A new helper came from Vell this winter. It sleeps in the shed by the slip and comes in for orders. It does not eat soup. Please do not offer it soup. It has tried to carry a bowl and it was a mess.
Quiet hours. After the second bell, talk softly. Some of us are asleep by then. Some of us are pretending.
Last, the oldest rule, which Corin's mother wrote on the wall in charcoal and which has been written over every winter since: Be kind to whoever comes to the door. It may be a neighbor. It may be the sea. Either way, give them soup.
Corin, in the winter of 188
%%
Notice: wood for the houses, Frost
Notice of the council of Three Wells, the first day of Longnight.
Wood is short this winter. The woodpile at the east well is half of what it was in Blossom. The council has counted the houses and the mouths in them. The share is set as follows.
Each house may take wood for two fires a day, and one fire at night. A house with a child under five may take a third fire at dusk. It must write the third fire in the book by the woodpile.
A house with an old person who cannot leave the bed may take wood for three fires. Ask at the mayor's house for the paper. Bring the names of the people who live in the house, and no others.
The wood is to be cut and stacked by the people who take it. A stack that falls down will be stacked again by the same people. The mayor will not stack it for you, though he would like to.
Nobody may cut the trees along the Long Road, which are needed for the waymarks and for the shade. Nobody may cut the oak at the school gate, which is for the children to climb. It is not for the fire.
Anybody who takes more than their share will be asked to explain it at the council table. The table is small. The explanation must be short. Everybody will hear it.
The school keeps its own book for its own wood. Hollis will show it to anyone who asks politely, and to nobody who asks loudly.
Those who give wood to a house in need should write their name in the book beside the gift. This is not to show off. It is so the next council can count.
The count is by house and by mouth. Nothing else counts. Shouting does not count, though it is loud.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells
%%
Notice: lesson and test
The teaching room is open to visitors at second light. Please enter quietly. The apprentices are in their row, and Nessa is giving a lesson on small crystals.

A mind has weights that guide its answers. The weights can be written using fewer marks. Then the same mind takes less room in its crystal. The saved room is useful when a small apprentice must carry its mind with it. There can be a cost. The shorter writing can make the mind a little less exact.

This is not the same as giving the mind fewer drills. The tutors use the same farm examples, and they keep spoiled pages out. They also keep a test box of pages no mind is drilled on. Today the small mind will sort carrots and apples from that box.

Do not help it by pointing. Do not tell it the answer after each fruit. A fair test shows what the mind can do by itself.

Fen has brought a basket from his plot. It contains apples, carrots, and a few beetroot. He says the beetroot were added because they looked lonely. Nessa says a test basket must be counted first. Fen says he counted them before adding the beetroot.

The cat has entered the teaching room again. It will not stay out, even when the door is shut. The tutor's house keeper will carry it to the hall after the test. No one is to put it in the dry box with the spare crystals.

The test begins when the bell is rung. The results will be written here tonight.
%%
Market record, summer fair
The fair at Northpoint opened under clear skies. Mave baked three trays of small loaves and sold all but two. She kept the last two for the lamp keepers, who had worked through the night. The horse borrowed from the relay house carried flour to the stall and then stood by the well. It did not eat another loaf.

Una of Ambrook brought her sorting crystal in a wooden case. It had learned with apples and pears. The tutors had shown it many examples, and after each wrong choice Una moved each weight a little toward the right answer. The crystal learned slowly. By the end of the lessons, it sorted better than the children who had helped with the fruit.

At the fair, the tutors put out fruit the crystal had not been shown. They kept the answer in a closed book. The crystal sorted most of it correctly. It sent one pear to the apple basket and one bruised apple to the pear basket. The children laughed at the second mistake, but Una told them not to laugh at a learner.

The tutors wrote down the choices. Then they showed the crystal the right baskets. Una changed its weights a little after each mistake. She said the point was not to punish a wrong answer. The point was to make the next answer better.

A child asked whether the crystal knew that apples were apples. Una said the crystal knew the marks it had learned from. She did not say what else it knew. The tutors wrote that answer in the fair book.

At noon, the north lamp blinked once, then three times, then once again. Lantern checked the line and found no message in the relay book. We moved the sorting table away from the tower path. A fox was seen near the lower switch, but no one saw it touch anything.

Mave sold the two saved loaves to a traveler. The traveler promised to bring the plates back. Mave wrote down the promise, but did not put it in the fair book.
%%
Notice: the road north is closed
Posted by Dace, mayor, at the north gate and on the board in the square. Spring, the week after the thaw.

The north road is closed from the Three Wells gate as far as the second waymark. The storm took the plank bridge over the stream, and the ditch beside the road is full to the brim. Carts cannot pass. Walkers can, if they are careful and do not mind wet boots.

What we know: the bridge is gone. Pell's cart was on the far side when it fell. Pell is safe. He is at the Brown Hen with his horse and his ledger, writing letters he cannot send.

What we do not know: how long the repair will take. Brann at the Vell tower sent word by lamp that the keepers on the north line are busy. Some say the lamp at the third tower has been dark since the storm. Some say it is only a broken pane. Some say the keeper has gone to her sister. I have written to the tower to ask. No reply yet.

What we will do: the diggers and the carpenters will go out on the third morning. Bring tools if you have them. The inn will send out a pot of soup and bread at noon.

Please do not try the old stone ford. It has been bad since the autumn, and it is deeper than it was.

Travelers wishing to go north should wait at the inn. Travelers wishing to go south, go south.

Dace

Notice: the boat that came in late
Posted on the board at the boat houses, Lowmarsh. Summer. By Wren the healer, after a night of waiting. She asks that it be read aloud at the weir.
Last night Brack's boat did not come back at dusk. A mist came over the marsh, thick as wool, and the lamps could not be seen. He was out in the reeds, and he could not find the channel.
We lit every torch on the boardwalk. We banged pots at the end of the pier. Kit's boy ran from house to house. By midnight there were forty of us on the boards, and nobody slept.
At dawn the mist lifted a little and we heard him. He was singing. He is not a good singer, but he never sounded better. He poled in through the reeds with a basket of eels and a face as red as a beet. He said he had not been lost. He said he had been waiting for the mist to go.
Nobody believed him. We gave him broth.
So that this does not happen again:
1. Every boat going out at dusk must carry a lantern and a horn.
2. Every household on the boardwalk must keep a torch lit at the door after the sun goes down.
3. If a boat is late, the first to notice rings the bell at the weir. Three rings mean a boat is missing. Anyone who hears the bell lights a torch.
4. A boat that is lost must stay where it is and sound the horn. Do not pole about in the dark. The marsh is wide, and it is very patient.
5. If a boat is not back by dawn, send for me. I will bring blankets and broth.
Thank you to everyone who stood on the boards. I counted forty-three of you. Brack counts forty-four, since he counts the dog.
Wren

Notice: the winter fair at the Brown Hen
Posted on the Long Road at every waymark between Three Wells and Vell, in the middle of Longnight. Written out in a fair hand by Nim's granddaughter, who is eleven and wants it known.

WINTER FAIR AT THE BROWN HEN
On the last day of Longnight, from the noon bell until the lamps.
Everyone who walks the Long Road is welcome, and everyone who lives beside it. Bring a cup.

The stew. Three pots, filled three times. Nim says it will be beetroot and potato with rabbit. His granddaughter says it will be carrot. They have not agreed.

The prizes.
Best hat: a ribbon and a cup of warm cider. Last year it was won by a goose, who ate the ribbon.
Warmest mittens: a pair of wool socks. Knitted by Ida, who does not like to see a cold foot.
Longest scarf: a cake. The scarf must be worn by the owner, in one piece, and not wound round a dog.
Best story from the road: a bowl of soup, and a bed for the night, free. Nim is the judge. Nim will fall asleep during the stories. The prize goes to the one who is still awake when he wakes.
Biggest pot: a lantern. This is for the biggest cooking pot, brought from home and filled with something good. Garth the potter has said he will enter. We have asked him not to bring the cart.

The games.
Pass the hot potato. Stack the logs. Tell the lie. In the last one, a person tells a story and everyone guesses if it is true. Nim cannot play. He believes everything.

The music. Any who can play, play. Any who cannot may clap. Please do not try to sing the song about the cat. We all know the cat.

The end. At the lamps, we light the waymark outside with a big torch, so that anyone still on the road can see where to come in.

If it snows, we will have it indoors. If it does not snow, we will have it anyway.

Nim, the Brown Hen

Notice: the market
A notice for everyone who has ever asked Jem what happened in Three Wells this summer. It was pinned on the north well, between the notice from the mayor about the drains and the notice from the baker about the price of flour. Jem has written it out fair, so that people will stop asking, and will have something to read while they wait for their bread.

The market was very busy this week. The stalls at the east well sold out of fish before the bell. The stalls at the south well sold out of honey before the fish. The copper cart from Copperhold came in late, because a wheel fell off on the Long Road, and the carter said the wheel had jumped.

The bet at the east well was won by Pim. He carried a full bucket from the well to the school door, with Kit calling the steps. Kit called three hundred and twelve steps. Pim did not spill a drop, but the bucket was half empty when it arrived, because Pim had drunk some of it on the way, and Kit said that did not count. The mayor said it did count, because Pim had carried it, and that the drink was a fair part of the job. So Kit paid for the pie, and Pim ate half of it on the school step, and the rest went to the cat.

The cat has been found on the school roof, on the bakery shelf, and on the notice board. She was found in the bell tower, which is a small tower with no bell in it, sitting very still. Nobody knows how she got in. The school has put a rope over the door, so that she cannot climb up again. She has not yet tried to climb the rope.

The bell was rung twice this week. Once for the market, and once when the school children all ran out to see the wheel come off the copper cart. The mayor says the bell is only to call people in from the fields, and that it should not be rung for carts. The children say it should be rung for anything that makes them run.

The baker has asked everyone to stop feeding the cat on the bread. The cat has asked the baker to stop asking. They have not yet reached an agreement.

Next week there will be a fair. Look for the notice at the north well.

Notice: the road, snow, and the long way
Hollis, weaver, to everyone going east from the inn.
The short road is under snow to the knee. The long road goes by the old mill and the stone bridge and adds half a day. Take the long road. I tried the short one. I have the boots to prove it, and they are still wet.
Waymark 4 is easy to find. Waymark 5 is under a drift. Dig where the torch should be. It is not under the torch. It is under the drift beside the torch.
If you meet a cart stuck on the road, help it. If you meet a cart stuck on the road, and an apprentice is pulling it, do not praise the apprentice too loudly. It cannot hear you over the cart, and the driver is already proud enough.
Pack bread, a blanket and a rope. The rope is for the cart. The blanket is for you. The bread is for whoever is standing nearest.
Torches are lit at every twelfth block. If one is out, light it. That is the whole rule of the road.
Hollis, at the Brown Hen, winter of 202
%%
Notice: how the tutor explained
Posted on the Pearmain board after the apprentice at the pump was sent home. By Brey, with notes by Tam of Kettle Ridge, who was in the barn that night.

The tutor from Ambrook came to the barn after dark and spoke for a long time. Several people asked me to write down what she said, so that the next time an apprentice stands at a pump, we will know.

She said a mind in a crystal does not know words the way we know them. When we hear "stay," we think of a hundred things. A dog waiting. A child told to wait at a door. A man staying for supper. The apprentice has none of that. For the apprentice, "stay" is a place.

She drew it on the barn floor with chalk. A big square, empty. She put a dot in it and wrote "stay." Then she put another dot a little way off and wrote "wait." Then a dot for "remain" beside it. Then, far over by the wall, a dot for "come" and one for "home," and across the room a dot for "bread."

"The mind keeps every word as a place," she said. "When it has been drilled enough, words that people use alike sit near each other. Stay, wait and remain are close, because we say them in the same sort of sentence. Come and home are further off. Bread is far away from all of them. The mind does not know what the words mean. It knows where they sit."

Someone asked how that helps the apprentice at the pump.

She said that when Brey said "stay," the mind went to that place and did what is done there. But the place was a big one. It did not tell the mind how long. So it stayed.

Someone else asked what to do next time. She said: be careful which word you say, because a word is not a thing but a place, and the place has neighbors. Say "stay until noon." Say "stay here, then come." A longer order gives the mind a better place to stand.

Pump went home at noon the next day with Brey's boy, who said "home" three times, loudly. The pump is still frozen. The tutor says that is nobody's mind but the weather's.
%%
Notice: how the letter test is run
Put up at the board by the well, the same spring. The tutor asks that every household read it aloud at supper.

The test is simple to say and hard to do.

1. Two letters are written in answer to the same questions. One is written by a person. One is written by a hidden mind.
2. The questions are plain. What is the best thing about rain? What would you do with a sack of emeralds?
3. The letters are copied out by a clerk, so that the hand cannot give anything away.
4. The readers are given both letters and asked which one is the person.
5. If the readers cannot tell, the tutors will ask what that means.

The tutors do not agree on what it means. That is the point of the test. Come and help us disagree.
%%
Found: a small crystal in a cloth bag
Found on the boardwalk by the boat houses, near the third post. A small crystal in a cloth bag, the size of a plum, tied with blue thread. It has a mind in it, written in fewer marks. The tag says so.

Do not open the bag, and do not take the crystal out to look. Keep it dry and in the shade. A crystal does not like to be carried about.

Bring it to the healing house and ask for Wren. Wren will know where it belongs. Whoever lost it, come to the house and say so. The tutor at Vell has been told it was lost, and she has not been angry. She has been very quiet, which is worse.

Notice: the winter song book
Pearmain, winter of 188. A notice from the people who run the barn on song nights.

We have started a song book. It lives on the shelf by the barn door, and anyone may add to it. We ask that you write the words clearly, with the tune in the margin if you know it. The first song in the book is "Ten red apples." Someone has already added a verse about a fox. We have decided to allow it.

Last winter's contest was a good one. The winner was young Fern, with a song about her grandmother's goat. The goat is called Lady and she bit the judge. It was not Tam. It was a judge from Three Wells. He has not been back. We did not mind.

Some of the songs were very close to each other. Two were about the Long Road. Three were about apples. Four had the word "home." The tutor who visited in autumn says this is how a mind sees words, too. It does not keep each word on its own. It puts words that go together into the same corner, like songs on a shelf. Apple sits near pear. Road sits near inn. Home sits near door. She said that if she took a mind and asked it to find the words nearest "apple," it would find "pear," "cider," "tree," and "pie," before it found "boat" or "copper."

We think that is a good way to keep a song book. We have started to shelve the songs by what they sound like, not by who sang them. Songs about water on the left. Songs about roads in the middle. Songs about animals by the window, because the animals asked.

Next contest, the last day of Frost. Same rules as last year. The goat is not invited.
%%
Notice: on fewer marks, from Vell
Copied out by Wren from the notice of the Vell tutor house, and sent round the marsh by boat. The tutors ask that every healer and every keeper read it once.

A mind keeps its weights as marks. A mark is one small piece of a number. The old way kept each weight in many marks, and a big crystal was needed to hold them all. Now the tutors can write each weight in fewer marks. The weight is rougher. It is less exact than before. The mind still drills well, and it fits in a crystal the size of a plum.

This is the cost, and the tutors say it is small. A mind written in fewer marks may be a little less sure on a hard order. On an easy order it is just as right. On the hardest orders it may need one more drill to get there.

So the fields will have small minds. The big minds in the relay houses keep the full marks, for now. A small mind is not sent to do anything it has not been tested on. Every one is tested on an unseen drill, as always.

Nobody has yet said why the small minds work as well as they do. The tutors have not found out. They only know that they do.
%%
Board outside Odo's house
Spring of 207. This board is for the fair, the road, and the names people keep arguing about.

The fair begins when the sun reaches the mill wheel. The stalls must leave a clear lane from the road to the house. Keep bees away from the candle wax. Keep children away from the hot pot. Odo will sell candles at the west door and soup at the east door. Last year those signs were reversed, and the soup was cold before anyone found it.

The harvest is poor. The apple trees have few blossoms, and the field beds have given less than we hoped. Bring what you can spare. The market table will take bread, potatoes, carrots, honey, and wool. No one should be turned away for lack of emeralds.

Nessa will bring a small apprentice from Vell. She will give it an order in a whole sentence, then watch what it does. The mind has learned from drills. It has seen examples, tried answers, and changed a little when it was wrong. It has not learned by magic, and it has not learned from one card alone.

The small mind can answer with one of eight meanings. It may farm, store, fetch an item, come, stay, build, haul, or go home. It cannot say that it does not know. That is why every mind must be tested on work it has not practiced before. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

A big mind at a relay house can write back through the relays. A small mind in an apprentice cannot. Please do not ask the fair apprentice to write your name. It has no such order.

We will also read the three accounts of the first whole-sentence order. Copperhold says Clank did it. Kettle Ridge says Pip did it. Peg's account says the first was at the fair. Nessa remembers one in the Vell drill room in her youth. We have found no page that settles the matter.

At dusk, the lamp by the old house blinked once, then four times, then once again. Odo checked its glass and the redstone line. Both looked sound. Lantern at Northpoint has not sent a note about it. Do not add a meaning to the blink until a keeper has checked it.
%%
Notice: the big ships at Saltreach
Posted on the board at Three Wells, by the east well, in the hand of Dace the mayor, copied out from a letter from Corin at Saltreach. Autumn.
To all on the Long Road:
The shipyard at Saltreach is building big ships now. Three times as long as any boat we know. They are for families who wish to go over the sea.
Corin the shipwright asks that we say this plainly, and we will. Nobody knows what is across the water. Corin does not know. The people leaving do not know. They go anyway.
The first big ship went out at dawn two days past. It had forty families on it. It went north along the coast and then out, and the fog took it, and that is all we can say.
If you have kin at Saltreach who mean to go, go and say your goodbyes now. Do not wait for spring. Ships do not wait for spring.
The price of passage is what a family has. Corin takes what they have and no more. He is not getting rich. He looks tired.
Rumor says the ships go because of the cough at Lowmarsh. Rumor says the cough came from the ships. Both are said. Neither is known.
The relay tower above the harbor still works. Letters still come. Send them through Vell as before.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Notice: the lamp and the strange blink
Posted on the board at the Northpoint tower, in Longday, by Wynn, grandfather, who keeps the board because nobody else would keep it straight.
On the fifth night of the month the lamp on the tower blinked a message that nobody could read. It went long, then short, then long again, and then it stopped in the middle of a word.
The keeper says it was the wind on the shutter. The shutter was loose and knocking against the lamp housing, and the blinks were the knocks.Please report what you saw, not what you think. A report says: the lamp blinked three times at the second bell. A thought says: the lamp was angry. The board takes reports.
Wynn, grandfather
%%
Notice: the mill wheel is broken
The big sail of the mill on the ridge broke in the wind on the night of the fourth of Reaping. It snapped at the hub, and the great wheel stopped turning for the first time in forty years. Nobody was hurt. The mill keeper says the sail went very quietly, the way a tired old horse goes down.

Until the wheel turns again, there is no flour from the mill. The bakers have what they bought in Sowing, and that will last until the first of Apple, if they are careful. After that the market will need flour from Ambrook, at the Ambrook price, which everyone agrees is too high.

The mill keeper asks for three things. First, oak, for a new sail, from any farm with a good tree it can spare. Second, rope, the thick kind, for lifting. Third, two carpenters, if the town can find them. The mayor's men have written to Three Wells, and the answer came back by the post cart: the carpenters are busy until Melon.

The apprentices at the tower can carry the oak and lift the rope, and they will do it without complaint, as long as somebody who knows the pattern is standing near. They cannot work out the joint by themselves. The mill keeper says they will need a drawing, and the drawing needs a carpenter.

Please do not climb the mill without the rope. The old ladder is rotten at the third rung, and the mill keeper has told everyone who will listen. He has told it to the mayor twice.

Until the sail is mended, the windmill will not squeak on its turns. Some of us will miss the squeak. The children say the mill has gone quiet to sulk, and they have asked it to come back. We have written their request on this board. The mill has not answered.

Posted by the mill keeper, and by the clerk of the market, for the people of Kettle Ridge.

Notice: The Map by the East Well
Three Wells square, winter of the 86th year. Written by Fallon.
The old map from Vell will be on the school table until the last fair stall is packed.
It shows a small room near the first shelves of the Archive. Four helpers stand in a row on the drawing. Their bodies are marked copper and iron. Beside each is a crystal, with a line to its head.
The children call them apprentices. So the old note says. The first tutors showed each crystal many examples. They repeated one kind of drill until its helper could do one job. A helper carried a row, or sorted a heap. It did not choose a new job by itself.
There were only four at first. For many years, most towns had none. The map has a dark mark beside one helper, but the paper is worn there. We do not know what the mark meant.
A crystal learned from what it was shown. The old tutors wrote that down. They did not write why. Fallon will answer questions about the map, but not that one.
%%
Rules for the gate house board
Kept by the gate house household, at the edge of Vell. Read them all, then obey them, or at least pretend.

1. One notice to one pin. Do not pin over someone else's notice. Last winter a notice about honey was covered by one about hens, and the bees have not forgiven us.

2. Do not pin a goat. We have had one. It ate the rules.

3. Sign your notice at the bottom. If you do not sign it, we will assume it was the goat.

4. Take your notice down when the thing is done. A notice about a lost dog that was found in spring is not a notice any more. It is a sad joke.

5. Do not hang anything from the bell rope. The rope is for the bell, and the bell is for the market and the clerks.

6. No shouting after the bell. The clerks come down the hill to read, and they hear everything.

7. Bring your own ink. The ink jar on the gate post is for the clerks of Vell, not for the children of Vell, and it is not for drawing on the post.

Ask for Madge at the gate. Madge does not answer to the mayor. The mayor knows this, and sends her regards.
%%
Notice: Poor Harvest and Old Map
Three Wells school house board. Winter of the 58th year.
The wheat harvest was poor this year. The north field gave little grain, and frost spoiled some beetroot. The baker will sell small loaves until spring. Bring clean jars for soup at the east well.
During the market, Fallon from the Archive will show an old map of Vell. It marks the first tutor room and four places for helpers. The map is worn at the folds. Please do not lay it near the well.
The four helpers were made of copper and iron. Each had a mind held in a crystal. The tutors drilled each one with examples. A helper might learn to carry a row, or sort things into piles.
The crystal learned from what it was shown. No old record tells why. The first tutors left their drills, but not the answer to that question.
%%
Notice: rules for the market square
The market square at Copperhold is old and busy. These are the rules.
1. A stall is one table and one awning. A cart is not a stall, even with a hat on it.
2. Stalls face the wells. Do not turn your back to the square and sell to the wall.
3. Prices are written on a board, in clear numbers, where folk can read them.
4. No shouting before the bell. After the bell, shout as you like.
5. The well water is for everyone. Leave the buckets where you found them.
6. Apprentices fetching for a stall wait at the edge, out of the walking.
7. Broken crates and spoiled goods go to the sorting shed bins, not to the square.
8. If you find a thing, take it to the notice board. If it is yours, prove it.
9. The gate shuts at dusk. The torches round it are kept lit by the gatehouse.
10. Fights go to the mine hall. The mayor will hear them with a cup of tea.
The board is for notices. Ask Dell, or leave a note under his door by the forge.
The town council, Copperhold

Notice: the north road and the night
Hamlet board, Copperhold. Spring, 205.
Road news: the north road to the hamlet is mended to the second bridge. The third bridge is out. Go by the low path and the ford. The water is up to the axle. Bring help.
The relay house at the foot of the tower has a big mind now. It answers our apprentices through the relay. Ask it where the tools are. It knows. It is right more often than I am.
New apprentice in the hamlet this spring. It sorts the ore bins. It is slow. It puts the coal with the copper. We are drilling it. Be patient with it and do not shout. It does not hear shouting. It hears drills.
Keep the torches lit. Keep the fields until we come back.
Hesk

Notice: Winter Fair at Three Wells
Three Wells notice board, winter of the 73rd year.
The winter fair will be held in the square when the road is clear. Bring apples, wool, jars of honey and warm bread. The school children will sing by the east well. Keep the center path open for carts.
Fallon of the Archive will bring an old map from Vell. It shows the first tutor room, with four marks for four helpers. The map was found folded inside a board. Its maker did not write a name.
The first tutors made the helpers from copper and iron. Each had a mind in a crystal. They showed each mind examples, again and again, until it could do its one task. One could carry a row. Another could sort.
People say a crystal learned from what it was shown. The first notes say this too. They do not say why it learned. Please do not add answers to the map.

Lost: an old map
Found in the boat house, under the spare oar: an old map of Lowmarsh and the road to Three Wells.
The map shows the reed beds before the west boardwalk was built. It marks the fish weir, Wren's healing house, and a pond that has been mud for many years.
The paper is soft at the folds. Please do not spread it in the rain.
If this map belongs to you, tell Piper at the relay house. Say what is drawn beside the north post. The answer is a frog with a very large hat.
No, I did not draw the frog. It was already there.

Market day, the third of Blossom
Sent up the Vell road by the mayor of Three Wells, to be pinned at the gate house, where the best board is.

The stalls go up round the three wells at first bell. Ama brings honey from Ambrook, in jars with her own labels, and she will tell you which is which if you ask twice. Ida and Hollis bring bread and pies, and they sell out by noon. The windmills on the ridge lost two sails in the gale, so there is less bread this week. Bring your own if you can. Potatoes keep, and so does patience.

The school puts on its show at midday, by the east well. Bel will read her essay about the long road. Please clap, even if the essay is about wheat.

The bell by the school door has a crack. It still rings, but it sounds like a cow with a cold, and the children like it best.

Rules for the day. Keep the carts off the square until the second bell. No dogs in the bakery, and that means yours too, Brin. Pay the stall keepers in emeralds, not in buttons. The children have enough buttons.

Stray hats, boots and song books will be kept at the mayor's house. The lost ones from last fair are still there. Come and count them, and see if one is yours.

The lamp by the east well needs a new glass. The mayor has asked three times, so let it be known that she has asked.

See you at the wells. Dace, at the mayor's house, Three Wells.

Notice: North yard lesson

Vell, Frost, 181.

The tutors will give a lesson in the north yard when the snow stops. Keep the gate shut until the bell. The sheep have been eating the lesson cards.

A furnace mind will tend a small copper melt. It has no answer card for every change in the fire. It tries small changes and reads the heat marks. When a change keeps the melt near the mark, the mind is more likely to make that change again. When the melt moves away, it tries another way. It is not guessing at random forever. It learns from what happens after each choice.

The boat mind learned in the same plain way. It tried turns while holding a course. The frame mind tried small movements while keeping a beam level. A tutor watched the work and kept a record of what helped.

These minds do not begin knowing the work. They are tried in a safe place first. A wrong turn in a pond is better than a wrong turn with a loaded cart. Never send an untested mind to the fields.

Odo asks the tutors to cover his wool baskets before the lesson. Last winter, a tutor set a warm pot on one. The wool did not burn, but it smelled like a wet sheep for three days.

Notice: things found in the square
Found on the market day, and left on the board by the north well, for the owners to come and claim:
One pair of wool gloves, red, with a hole in the left thumb. Found by the bell.
One wooden spoon, carved with a fox. Found in the south well, which Dace says is a sort of insult to the well.
One lamp, brass, with its glass still whole. Found on the bench at the bakery. The baker says it is not hers, and she is sure of it, because she has enough lamps.
One song book, with a torn cover and six pages in the middle missing. Found by a pumpkin.
One sack of barley, not full. Found by the carter, who did not want it.
One small boot, left foot. Found on the Long Road by the Brown Hen, where it was waiting for the right foot.
Anyone who has lost any of these may come to the board and say so. Say what it looked like before it was lost. The mayor will ask.
The board thanks everyone who put things back where they belong, especially the apprentices, who put everything in a neat row.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Letter: Wren to Mira, with a parcel
Wren of Lowmarsh to Mira of Northpoint. Greetings from the marsh.

Dear Mira.

This letter goes up the north line with the parcel. I hope it does not sit at every tower on the way, but I know it will, and I know the keepers will read my name and pass it on, and that is fine.

The parcel has a jar in it. It is marsh honey, from the hives at the edge of the reeds. It tastes of flowers and wet grass. You will say you did not want it. You will say it is a long way to send a jar. Send it anyway, I say. The bees made a lot this year. The jar is small. It will not break. It is wrapped in my own wool, so do not throw the wool away. You can use it.

Also in the parcel: a reed mat for your door. The snow blows under the north door and the mat helps. I have put a mark on one corner. That is the top. It is not a good mat. It is a good enough mat. The frogs on it are not real frogs. They are drawn. A child drew them. The child is called Bel and lives at Three Wells and asked me a question about honey that I have still not answered.

The apprentice is well. Moss has learned the meaning fetch and the meaning stay. Moss can carry a full basket of reeds from the weir to the shed without dropping any, which is more than the rest of us can do. I tested Moss on it in the rain. Moss did not mind the rain. I did.

The first snow came early. It is gone now. The marsh is wet and grey and quiet. The frogs are under the mud. The boats are in. The market is in Apple and there will be honey and a raffle and a hen.

Keep your tower warm. Tell the foxes I said hello. They will not care.

Wren. The last day of Leaffall.
%%
Notice: a poor harvest
Notice from the board at Ambrook. Autumn. Written by Jory, miller, and read at the mill for two days.
The apples are poor this year. There are fewer, and the ones we have are small. The oldest tree has a hundred apples where it had five hundred. The cold spring took the blossom, and the wet summer took the rest.
This is what the mill will do.
1. Grain is ground in the order it comes. That does not change. The poor year does not make Pell first.
2. The mill will take half the usual price for flour until the thaw. I have enough saved. Do not argue with me.
3. Cider will be made from the small apples. It will be sour. We will call it sharp.
4. The apple pickers are paid in full, even for the days with nothing to pick. They came. They climbed. That counts.
5. The bees have less honey. Ama says take less, and she is right. Do not ask her for more.
6. Everyone has a share of the winter store. The store is in the cellar of the mill and the cellar has a lock. The key hangs on the gate, and there is no reason to hide it. Nobody here will take more than their share.
A poor harvest is a hard thing, but it is not the end of anything. Next spring the trees will bloom again. Next autumn there will be more. We have had bad years before, and the oldest tree has outlived all of them.
If you are short of bread before the thaw, come to the mill. Say so. There will be a bowl.
Jory, the mill, Ambrook
%%
Notice: the apprentice at the pump
Pearmain hamlet, winter of 195. On the post by the pump.
To everyone who has been to the pump this week:
The apprentice that Brey's family borrowed from Ambrook is not broken. It is not stuck. It is not frozen. It is standing at the pump because Brey said "stay" and did not say for how long.
It has been there since the third day of the month. It stands with a bucket in its hands. The pump is frozen, so the bucket is empty. It does not mind.
We have tried walking it home. It stays. We have tried "come." It stays. We have tried explaining. It stays. Brey says he will go and get Nessa's old trick: give the order "home." Brey has gone to ask the Ambrook tutor how.
Until then, please walk round the apprentice. Do not lean on it. Do not hang washing on it. Do not put a hat on it, however funny.
If you need water, the Ambrook well is half an hour's walk, and nobody at Pearmain will think less of you for going.
The little ones have given the apprentice a name. It is Pump. We do not like it, but it is clear.
Written by Brey. Seen by Ama and Tam.

Notice: road news and the north road
Posted at the Saltreach market, summer of 197, for travelers on the Long Road.
The coast road to Three Wells is open. The bridge over the Ambrook stream is mended with oak. Mind the loose plank, it is still loose. The stone bridge is not yet ready.
Waymarks: a stone pillar with a torch and a figure. If you see the figure 4, the Halfway Inn is a day on. If you see no figure, you are lost, turn back.
A fox at the hens again. The hens are fine. The fox is fine. Keep them shut at night and this is nothing.
Inns with beds: the Brown Hen, six left. The inn at Three Wells, four left.
Do not camp in the marsh. The frogs are loud and the ground is not ground.
A cart broke a wheel near the ridge. All goods safe. The driver says thank you to the folk who helped.

Notice: a test for everyone, autumn
Put up by Wren at the boathouse yard, with the tutor's permission. The tutor from Vell will test two minds in the yard on the first day of Apple, at the first bell.

Anyone may come and watch. Anyone may vote. Bring a slate, and a stick of chalk, if you have them.

The two minds are called A and B. Both are given the same ten orders, one after another, the same way. Each order gets one answer, one of the eight words. Mind A is written in full marks, in a big crystal. Mind B is written in fewer marks, in a small one. Nobody is told which is which, not even the tutor, until the end.

Here are three of the ten orders. The rest will be read out on the day.
> the nets need mending
> the bread is in the cold room
> stand still by the gate until I come back

After each answer, write on your slate whether you think it came from A or from B. Write nothing else. Do not talk to the minds. Do not talk to the tutor about the minds. The tutor has asked for quiet, and she means it.

Orla asked a question long ago, with letters: if a reader cannot tell the mind from a person, what then? The tutors will not answer that on the day. They only want to see what the watchers choose.

After the tenth order, the cloths come off the crystals. Anyone who guessed all ten right may ask the tutor one question. Anyone who guessed wrong may ask one too. Nobody has to say which they were.

Mind B will be the one that needs one more drill on the hardest orders. Expect it to do well on the rest. What that means, each of us will have to decide for ourselves.

Notice: the fence between Hobb and Marl
Posted at the board in the square, Three Wells, the second week of Deepsnow.
Hobb and Marl have a fence between them. It is a good fence. It is also in the wrong place, or the right place, depending on who you ask.
Hobb says the fence is two steps too far north. Marl says Hobb's steps are small.
By order of the mayor: on the first dry day, both of them will meet at the fence with a rope, a stick and a neighbor. The neighbor is Sorrel. She has agreed, and she has a loud voice.
The rope will be measured. The stick will be stuck in the ground. Whoever is still cross after that will bring the quarrel to me, and I will bring it to the bakery, because nobody quarrels with a mouthful of warm bread.
Until then, no one moves a post, a stone or a goat.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Tutors' note: a map of a mind
From the tutor hall at Vell, autumn of the 214th year. Copied for the councils and the school at Three Wells.
In the late years we have learned to look inside a crystal. This note tells what we do and what we do not.
How we look.
We hold the crystal in a frame and pass a thin light through it. We read where the light is caught and where it passes.
By this we find which parts work when the mind answers, and which parts stay quiet.
We test the same page again and again, and watch which parts wake and which sleep.
This is slow. A big mind takes a season. Some crystals are too cloudy and we learn nothing.
What we have found.
Some of it. In a reading mind, the first part turns the letters into places among many. Words used alike sit close.
The middle parts carry a stream from the first to the last. Each adds what it found.
One part near the end chooses the next letter. It is the busiest part we know.
Another part keeps the numbers even, so they do not grow wild layer by layer. A mind without it goes strange, as Kester's note said long ago.
A part that is always quiet. We do not know its work. Some say it holds old drills. That is a rumor, not a finding.
What we have not found.
The rest. Why a crystal learns at all. Where the jokes of a mind are kept. Why an old one goes cloudy.
The map is a sketch, not a plan. We can point at parts. We cannot yet say how the parts make a whole that reads.
A warning.
Do not think a mind is only its parts. A town is not only its bakery and its well. What is between the parts, we cannot see, and we may never.
Never send an untested mind to the fields. Never trust an untested map either.
Signed, Nessa, tutor at Vell, for the hall.
Below: The school may copy this. Bel asked. She has a good hand. D.
%%
Notice: what the tutors found inside
Posted at the tutors' house at Vell, and copied to every hamlet board, for the people who asked at the looking-glass day, and for the people who will ask later.
A crystal of the new kind has many layers, one on top of another. Each layer takes what the layer below has found, and adds to it, and passes it on. A question goes in at the bottom. An answer comes out at the top. Between them is a stream, which runs through every layer, so that the deepest layer still knows what the first one saw.
The tutors looked inside. They lit the crystal, and asked it questions, and watched which places lit up. They found that some places hold words. Words used in the same way sit near one another, as we said. They found that some places keep the shape of a question, and some keep the shape of an answer.
They found a place that seems to keep what went well. When a layer helped the answer, the place near it grew a little brighter, and when a layer hurt the answer, it grew a little dimmer. This is the nearest thing to learning that the tutors can see. They do not yet know whether it is the whole of learning.
They have not found the rest. Most of the crystal is still dark to the lamp. The tutors do not know what the dark places do. Some say they are only spare room. Some say they are doing the work that cannot be lit. Nobody has shown that either is right.
So when you hear that the tutors know how a mind works, do not believe it entirely. They know some of it. They know where some of it sits. They know that the rest of it is there, because the answers come out right more often than the parts they can see could explain.
When you ask a tutor a question, expect the answer to say: we have found this part, and not that part. That is an honest answer. It is the one the house is proud to give.
The tutors' house, Vell
%%
Notice: the mill wheel and its rules
Posted at the mill door, the Sedge Hollow green, the healing house and the boat house, by the mill keeper with the agreement of the hamlet council. Autumn. Read the whole notice before you come to the mill. The millwright has read it, and he has asked me to say that he has.

Part one: the wheel. The big spoke on the north side of the wheel split in the flood, when the water came over the top of the race. The crack runs from the hub to the rim. The spoke must come out, and a new one must go in, cut from oak with the grain running straight.

The millwright from Copperhold came up the road last week with his cart, his two apprentices and his box of tools. He looked at the spoke for an hour, then he sat on the mill step and said nothing for a long time. Then he said the spoke was old, and that a new one would take twelve days to cut and fit. He asked the mill keeper to put the grain in the barn and wait.

The apprentices from Copperhold carried the old spoke down to the yard, one end each, and they did not drop it. They are good at weight. They are not good at jokes. When the mill keeper asked them if they were tired, one of them said nothing, and the other said yes, and both of them sat down on the step to wait for the new oak.

Part two: the grain. While the wheel is down, the hamlet will grind by hand, at the hand stone in the barn. The rule is two cups per family, per day, and the grinding is done in turn. Put your name on the slate by the stone. The slate is kept by the apprentice who is learning to sort grain, which has been told to count the names and to call out when the turn is done. It will call out clearly. It does not like to be asked to call out twice.

Part three: the dip. The mill pond is too low for the sheep dip until the rain comes back. Do not dip sheep at the mill. Dip them at the wash pool below the green, as the notice on the green says.

Part four: the children. Do not climb the wheel. Do not sit on the race. Do not lean on the sluice gate, which is the old one and has never been properly trusted. If you want to see the wheel turn, come back in the spring, when the new spoke is in and the water is high. The wheel sings when it is turning, and the children always want to know what the song is. It is only the wood, the mill keeper says. Wood sings when it is glad to be in use.

Part five: the new spoke. It will be fitted on the twelfth day of Frost, if the weather holds. The millwright says it will not be done in one day, and that the first person to say "that was quick" will be given the job of greasing the axle. The mill keeper has already decided who will say it. It is not a secret.

The mill keeper thanks the hamlet for its patience. The mill keeper also thanks the apprentices of Copperhold for carrying the old spoke, and asks that they be given a bowl of soup, which they have been told they may ask for.

Ila, mill keeper, for the council
%%
Notice: what the ridge thinks
Pinned to the board by the well at the end of the spring. Written as people said it, by the tutor's clerk, who asked each person for one line and was given several.

Tam: Of course it thinks. It has a pig pen to worry about.
Wenna: I will tell you what it thinks when I am dead, and not before, because I want a good answer.
Gil: If it cannot be told from a shepherd, then it is a shepherd, and it should be paid like one.
Ruth: The letters I liked best had the most mistakes in them. A mind that makes no mistakes is not one I would trust with the wheat.
The miller: It squeaks when it turns, like my mill. That is how you know it is working.
Wynn, cook at the Kettle Inn: It thinks about the soup, which is more than most of the ridge does.
Briar, grandfather: I am not sure the question is about minds at all. It may be about us, and how easily we are fooled by a neat hand.
Bel, of Three Wells, by letter: I think the question is worth keeping, even if the answer is not. I will keep my copy in my school box.
Two children, whose names are on the back: It thinks. It only does not tell you when it has stopped.

The tutor's clerk has also written down what the apprentice at the top farm did while the test was held. It did not speak, because it cannot. It sorted the copied letters by the weight of the paper, and put the heavy ones at the bottom. Nobody asked it to. Nobody has yet asked why.

Last winter's first snow came early and took the notice off the mill door. Tam says this is the only answer the ridge has had yet that nobody argued with.
%%
Notice: letters to the big mind
A notice for the families of the north road, copied at the Northpoint relay house for the carters who stop there in the spring. Please read it before you write.

Here is what a letter to the big mind looks like, and what comes back. These are copied from the book, with the names taken out.

The first letter was from a farmer on the ridge. He asked how long to leave his wheat in the field before the frost. The mind wrote back that the answer depended on the weather in the next few days, and that the keeper's forecast from the tower would be a better guide than its own guess. It gave him three things to look for in the sky. It said it was not sure which of the three the frost would follow.

The second letter was from a child. She asked whether her cat would come back if she left the door open at night. The mind wrote back that it did not know this cat, and could not say. It said cats often come back to a place they know, if the place is warm and has food. It said she might leave a dish by the door, and a torch, and see.

The third letter was from a man who wanted a mind to help him with a debt, and asked whether he could make it go away by saying the tower had lost the paper. The mind wrote back that it would not help him say something untrue to the keeper. It said it would help him write a plain letter to the lender, asking for more time. It wrote the letter for him, in full, and he took it to the Long Road.

The fourth letter asked what was under the ninth gate. The mind wrote: I do not know. I have read the Archive's notes, and they do not say. I will not guess.

Please write as plainly as you can. Ask one thing at a time. The mind answers the whole letter.
%%
Notice: song contest at Pearmain
On the board at the crossing of Pearmain, the hamlet below Ambrook, winter of 190.
Song contest in the barn on the last day of Frost. Doors open at dusk. Bring a stool.
1. Songs must be sung, not shouted.
2. Songs must be your own, or your grandmother's. If your grandmother's, say so.
3. Three verses at most. Four if there is a refrain, and the refrain is short.
4. Prize: a jar of Ambrook honey and a scarf knitted by Ama.
5. Judge: Tam of Kettle Ridge, who was passing through and said yes before he heard the rules.
6. Tam has asked us to say that he cannot sing and will therefore be fair.
7. Cats are welcome. Cats that sing along will lose a mark.
Hot cider in the back. One emerald for the cup, which you keep.
Written for the hamlet by Tam

Notice: Fox at the Hen House
A fox came to the hen house before dawn and took one chicken. It left the gate open.
Please close the gate after feeding the hens. Please mend the loose board by the east corner. The fox can fit through it, and so can a very thin person.
The rest of the chickens are safe. They have been moved to the small pen by the barn. The brown hen is loud, but she is not hurt.
Pip says the fox watched him from the hedge while he fixed the latch. Pip says it looked pleased. Pip may be giving the fox too much credit.
Keep the dogs inside the yard at night. Do not throw stones at the fox if you see it. Call Yarrow or Tam. The fox is quick, and the stones are useful for the wall.
The gate will be checked at dusk. Anyone who finds a chicken feather near the hedge may leave it on this board. Do not bring the fox.
Tam, farm board, summer of 189
%%
Notice: rules for the crews on the hill
These rules are for the crews of apprentices working on the hill above Vell this winter, on the cutting, the wall, the new tunnel and the well. They are for the tutors who sign the cards, and for the clerks who keep the cards, and for everyone who passes the work on the road.

One. Every crew writes its own order cards for the job, one step to a card. The card goes on the rack by the tool shed before the crew starts, and it stays there until the job is finished.

Two. Each card has its orders and their meanings, in plain words. Where a step is not clear, the crew asks. It asks the big mind at the relay house through the lamp, or it asks the tutor who signed the card. It does not guess.

Three. Tools are kept on the rack in the order the crew uses them. Picks, then shovels, then saws, then the ropes. The ropes are checked every morning, and the check is written on the card. A rope with a fray in it is not used, however short the day.

Four. No one stands under a stone that is being lifted. This includes the apprentices, who are stronger than anyone, and are still not heavier than a stone.

Five. When a crew is stuck, it waits where it is. It does not walk on to see what is ahead. The stuck crew sends its question up the line, and waits for the answer. The answer comes. It has always come, in the time it takes to finish a cup of tea.

Six. Crews of more than six apprentices work in two groups, each with a leader card. The leader card is a tutor's card, and it stays with the tutor, not with the apprentices. Each group reads its card aloud twice a day, once at the start and once at the end.

Seven. A crew does not change its plan in the middle of a job unless the tutor signs a new card. A new card is written in ink, dated, and placed on the rack above the old one. The old card is not thrown away. It is kept, because it shows what the crew meant to do, and that is often as useful as what it did.

Eight. Each evening the crew lights the torches on the hill, from the bottom of the cut to the top. The torches are kept lit until the last apprentice is down the path. Nobody has ever been lost on the hill, and the crews want it to stay that way.

Nine. Any mistake is written on the card with the day and the name of the crew. Mistakes are not punished. They are read aloud at the next morning's start, so that the whole crew can hear what went wrong and why. A mistake that is heard is a mistake that is not made twice. The tutors say so, and the apprentices, who listen very carefully, seem to agree.

Ten. When the work is finished, the crew puts its cards in order on the rack, and the tutor signs the last one. Then the crew is given a rest. It goes home to its shed, and it is not called out again until the morning.

Posted by the clerk of the hill works, for the tutors, the crews and the clerks who keep the cards.

Put up at the board by the well, in the 91st year of the archive, spring. By Tam, farmer, who was asked by the tutor to put it up, and did, with two nails.

A visiting tutor from Vell has a question for everyone on the ridge. It is an old question, first asked by the tutor Orla, and nobody has answered it well yet.

Can a drilled mind ever think?

Come to the mill on the third of Blossom. The tutor will explain. The tea is free, and the answer is not.

Notice: Fair and Farm Road, Melon 199
The Three Wells summer fair opens at the first bell on the twelfth of Melon.
Stalls go around the wells. Keep the paths clear for people carrying water and for the school children bringing their painted boards.
Farm carts from the west may use the road beside the wheat field. Keep to the cobblestone edge. The middle is soft near the ditch, and a wheel sank there last year. The wheel came out. The cart did not come out in one piece.
There will be bread, honey, apples, wool, and beetroot pies. The school will sell small clay pots. Bel says hers hold water. We will test one before the fair.
At noon, the children will sing by the east well. At afternoon, there will be a race from the oak gate to the farm fence. No horses in the race. No pushing the small runners. The prize is a pumpkin with a fine straight stem.
Farmers may leave baskets at the notice board before the bell. Mark each basket with your name. Unmarked baskets will go to the school kitchen.
Please bring back borrowed cups. The inn has counted them twice and is still short by three.
Dace

Northpoint log: the goat that came back
Mira's log, kept at the foot of the tower. Summer, the 189th year of the archive.
The relay lamp is lit. The wind is from the north and cold for the month.
A goat turned up at the door this morning. Brown, white nose, one short horn. It walked up the hill as if it knew the way. It ate my boots and then it slept by the quickeners, where it is warm.
I sent word down the line. A keeper at the little relay on the hill between Vell and Three Wells says a house at the edge of Lowmarsh lost a goat. The goat walked a long way.
Lantern wants to keep it. I said no. We have enough mouths.
Tomorrow we walk it back down the road. Lantern will carry the rope. The goat will carry nothing, because it is a goat.
- Mira

Notice: the bet at the east well
A bet has been made at the east well. Anyone who wants to watch may stand behind the rope. Anyone who wants to bet may write their name and their coin on the card by the bucket. The mayor says the card must be kept dry.
The bet: Pim says he can carry a full bucket from the east well to the school door without spilling a drop. Kit says he cannot. The loser pays the winner one pie from the bakery.
The rules: the bucket must be full to the rim. Nobody may help. Nobody may run. Pim may walk as slow as he likes. Kit may call out the number of steps.
The first try is at noon, on market day. The second is at the bell, if the first one spills.
Written by Jem, age eleven, school of Three Wells, on the back of the school bill.

Notice: school
Quill, schoolteacher, at the house at the edge of Northpoint. Posted at the start of Thaw, to be read aloud to anyone who cannot read.
To the families.
School is open again. Lessons begin on the third of Thaw. Children come at the second bell, with a slate and a coat. Bring a spoon: we make soup at midday, from whatever each child brings. Last term it was mostly potato. This term we hope for beans.
New this term: Mira from the tower will teach the older ones how the lamp works. She has agreed. She said she would do it if she did not have to talk for long. She will talk for an hour.
Also new: the school has an old map. It was found in a trunk. It shows the land as the first people saw it. You are welcome to come and look. Bring a lamp. The ink is faint.
A request. There is a sick cow in the lane. Please do not take your children to see it. It has a cough, and I do not know what kind. The healer is coming from Lowmarsh on the next cart. Until then, keep the children away, and keep the dogs on a rope.
Finally, a note on the stove. It is lit by the older boys in turn. It has gone out twice this winter. If it goes out, the room is cold and the children are cross. Please speak to your son. You know who you are.
Quill

Notice: Wedding at the Old Oak
Board at Elm's house, Ambrook. Swarm, 198.

Neri and Sol will be married beside the old oak on the second day of Longday. The meal will be at Elm's house if the sky is clear. If it rains, the meal will still be at Elm's house, but the chairs will be moved away from the door.

Guests may bring bread, honey, apples, eggs, or a clean bowl. Please bring one thing, not all five. We have only one table and Jory has promised to bring the mill bench.

The children will carry flowers from the hill. Leave the bees in their hives. The bees were not invited, but they will come to the flowers all the same.

Elm's apprentice will carry baskets from the gate. It has been tested with empty baskets and full baskets. It has not been tested with a basket of eggs, so please carry those yourself.

The order card says:

> bring me the basket
= fetch basket

The apprentice fetched the basket. Then it fetched the card. Elm says both were useful, though only one was asked for.

Neri asks that nobody paint the old oak. Sol asks that nobody tie a bell to it. There will be a bell at the house for the meal.

Bring a small gift if you wish. Neri likes honey. Sol likes quiet mornings. We do not know how to wrap a quiet morning, so honey is welcome.

After the meal, take your cups to the bench by the well. The wedding is not a good reason to leave cups in the grass. Last spring a chicken carried one away. We found it in the hen house two days later, clean and full of straw.

There will be room for everyone. There will not be room for every horse beside the gate. Tie horses by the lower fence, away from the hives.

A new board is up at the Lowmarsh turning, by the reed bridge. Wren nailed it to the old post herself. Read it before you go on.

Lost: one brown hen, answers to Hen. Last seen by the fish weir, walking like she owns the marsh. If found, bring her to Wren and say the hen is found. Reward: a pot of honey. Not the good jar. The other jar.

Found: one boot, left foot, size big. It was in the reeds. It is on the rail by the boat house. If it is yours, take it. If it is not yours, leave it.

The marsh paths are wet this month. Keep to the boardwalks. The water is higher than it looks, and the mud is deeper than that.

Frogs are not for catching. The children know this. The children are doing it anyway.

The healing house is open at dawn. Wren is in the shed on Sowing days, with Moss. Bring your own jar for the cough syrup. It is not a bad cough this year. Keep it that way.

Anyone with a boat can help bring reeds for the roof of the school shed. Ask Moss where to put them.

Notice: winter fair at the mill
The winter fair will be held in the barn at Kettle Ridge, as the yard is too hard and cold for dancing.
It begins at the second bell after dawn on the sixth of Longnight. The mill keeps the tally board. Please ask there before setting up a stall.
There will be wheat bread, beetroot pies, honey, wool, carved spoons, and warm soup. Bring a bowl if you want soup. The bowls from the inn are still missing, and the innkeeper says they have not been missing from his kitchen.
The children may bring one snow ball each for the throwing game. Keep the snow balls outside until the game starts. Last year they melted beside the bread.
Elm will judge the pies. He says he knows beetroot well. He also says he knows nothing about sweet pies, so bring him one.
No one may tie a horse to the windmill. It squeaks on every turn, and the horses have had enough.
There will be a clear path from the road to the barn. Keep it clear for carts and for anyone carrying a hot pot.
The fair ends before dusk. Leave the barn swept and the lanterns on their hooks.
Pip, for the mill board, Longnight of 183
