Notice: the frog season, boardwalk rules
Posted by the Lowmarsh council on every post from the healing house to the weir. Summer. Read this before you step on the boards.

The frogs are back in force. They are fine. They are in the reeds, in the water, on the posts, on the boardwalk, in the washing and in the hats. Do not panic. Do not step on them.

Rules for the boardwalk, in summer:
1. Keep to the middle planks. The edge planks are green and slippery, and the frogs like them.
2. Carry a torch after dusk. The torches are lit at each post, but the posts are wide apart, and the frogs do not stop for the lamps.
3. Walk in single file on the narrow bridges. Two people cannot pass on a bridge and nobody has ever managed it.
4. Do not feed the frogs. They have been fed by the children all spring, and they now know the children's names. This is not a good thing. Ask Moll why, if you do not believe it.
5. Do not carry more than you can lift. The posts are old, and the water is slow to forgive a dropped barrel.
6. If you fall in, climb out at the nearest post. Do not wait for the boat. The boat is always late.
7. If you see a child on the boardwalk alone, take the child to the healing house. Wren will find the parents. Wren always finds them.
8. The apprentices on the boardwalk are carrying loads for the weir and the boat house. Let them pass. They do not stop for talk, and they do not like to be asked.

The council thanks you. The frogs thank you too. They have asked for a day off, and they will have one in Frost.
%%
Notice: lost tool and found cat
Found by the west steps of the Archive: one iron hoe with a strip of blue wool tied round its handle. Ask Fen at the tutor house gate.

Lost from the copying room: a grey cat with a torn ear. It is not lost in the usual way. It has been told to stay out of the room and has not stayed out. If it is under a table, please leave it there until the clerk finishes copying.

The small crystal for the field test is not lost. It is on Nessa's bench, in its box. The box has a lid, and the lid has a latch. Please do not open it.

A few marks have been removed from the weights in this crystal's mind. This lets the mind fit into a smaller crystal. It can still do the farm drill, but the tutors expect a small loss in exactness. They will test it on fruit it has not seen before. They will not call a good result proof that every task will be easy.

Fen says this is like packing a cart. You can fit more if you tie things close, but a loose apple may fall out. Nessa says that is a fair picture, though the mind is not a cart.

The test is tomorrow, after the morning lesson. Keep the yard clear. Keep the cat out if it will let you.

Market notice: wool and tools

Vell, Longnight, 185.

The winter market will open in the lower hall at noon. Keep the passage clear for the Archive clerks. No carts past the first door. Carry bundles by hand.

Odo offers carded wool, four bundles, each tied with a blue thread. Price: two emeralds for a bundle, or one sack of wheat and a jar of honey. The wool is dry and clean. It came from the sheep above the south road.

A carding comb is for sale at the bench beside the wool. It has no chipped teeth. Price: three emeralds. Odo's own comb is still missing. The one found by the west stair belongs to Sela, who has claimed it.

Tutors will set a small furnace mind to work after the market. It will hold a copper melt near a chosen mark. The mind was not drilled with a correct answer for every change in the fire. It tries a small change, then the tutor notes what happens. If the change brings the melt nearer the mark, the mind is more likely to make it again. If not, it tries another choice.

A boat mind learned in this manner too. It tried turns while keeping to a course on the pond. A frame mind tried small movements to keep a raised beam level. The tutors kept the good results, not a list of every right move. Each mind had to learn what helped through its own work.

These trials need close watching. The furnace is small, the boat stays in the pond, and the frame is held by ropes. Do not take a mind from its test and send it to a job. The tutor must test it on work it has not practiced first.

The roof over Odo's room is sound now. The barn is another matter. Spiders have returned between the wool racks. Odo asks for a hand after the market, and says he will pay in warm soup. He says the spiders may stay if they card their own wool. None has offered.

At the east door, one basket of carrots was left without a name. Ask the clerk before taking it. The Archive keeps even small things written down.

Notice: test day at the hall
Posted at the tutors' hall in Lowmarsh, and copied to the hamlets on the reed road. Autumn. For the tutors, the carters and the families of the apprentices.

Every mind sent out from this hall must be tested on the day before it leaves. The test has two parts. The first part is on cards the mind has been drilled on. The second is on cards it has never seen.

The second part is the one that matters. A mind that passes only the first has remembered its lessons. A mind that passes the second has learned them.

This autumn the halls of the north and the halls of the marsh will send their minds to the same test, on the same cards, sealed by the clerks at Vell and opened in front of the witnesses. Each hall will be given its score on the same day. The scores will be read out at Vell, and posted on the board here.

We do this so that no hall can say its mind is better without proof. Every tutor is honest in front of the cards. It is harder to be honest in front of the other halls.

If your apprentice fails, do not be cross with it. Take it back to the hall. Drill it again. Test it again, on cards it has not seen. Keep trying until it passes, and then send it out to the fields.

Never send an untested mind to the fields.

Tutor Ilse, for the hall

Notice: the fence on Hollin Lane
Notice from the mayor's house at Three Wells. The fence between the house of Crane and the house of Tobi has been argued over for six weeks.
The council has decided. A clerk will measure the fence on the first day of Frost. Both neighbors may stand by the post with their arms folded. Neither may shout while the measuring is done.
If the fence is found to be on Crane's side, Tobi will paint the post. If it is found to be on Tobi's side, Crane will paint the post. If it is found to be on the line, both will paint the post, and the clerk will be given a cup of tea.
Children are not to climb the fence while the matter is open. Children are not to climb it when it is closed, either. The council has heard about the apples.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Written on the hamlet board in Melon, by whoever had chalk that week, and copied onto the board at the mill when the first one was rubbed out.
Lost: one copper spoon, from the bakery. It was last seen on the wall with a crust. Please return it to Gil, who has been using a wooden one and is tired of it.

Wanted: someone to teach the sheep at the mill how to stand still for the children. The sheep is willing. It is only the standing.
Road news: the Long Road is open from Three Wells to the ferry. The stretch past the ferry is muddy and will stay that way until the rains stop. The carters say it is only mud. The carters have been saying so for a week.
Looking-glass answers: the tutors have posted a list of the questions asked at the looking-glass day, with the answers they could give. The list is long. The answers are short, as the notice asked. Read them before you come to ask more.
Wanted: someone to fetch the riddle board from the mill, where it has been left since Blossom. Nobody will say whose it is.
Notice: the bakery's broom has been missing since Melon. If you have seen it, do not say so to Gil.
Rules of the board: one notice to a person. Notices are to be taken down when they are no longer true. Do not take down the notice about the fox, which is still true.
Notice: a loose stone at the top of the mill wall. The spiders have been asked to leave it alone, and have agreed to, for now.
Found: a book of sums, with the first page torn out. The sums on the other pages are right. The torn page is missing its answer. Anyone who knows the answer may write it in the margin, marked guess.
Found: one apple, on the bridge, with a bite out of it, of the sort the Three Wells children make. Eat it, or bring it to the school.
Joke, for the children's corner: What did the fox say to the hens? Nothing. It was too busy being polite about it.
Gil, baker, who has the chalk this week and will hand it on

Notice: the Halfway Inn
Board by the door, put up by Fallon the stonecutter.
Beds: twelve. First come, first slept. Do not take a bed that has a hat on it. The hat means someone has gone to the well.
Boots by the door. Not past the mat. The mat is new. I cut the stone for the step myself and I will not have it muddy.
The hearth has not gone out in twenty years. Do not be the one who lets it. If you cannot find wood, ask. There is wood.
The riddle board is by the fire. If you write a riddle, write the answer on the back of the board and not on the wall. Someone wrote it on the wall. I have not forgiven them.
No wolves inside. No trying to sell a horse inside. No arguing about the relays after the third bell.
Food is bread, soup and apples. The soup is what it is.
Fallon

Letter: Fallon to Nessa, tutor at Vell
Fallon writes from the Halfway Inn, summer. Nessa, I hope this finds you well.
I have your book of drills. I read it by the fire, slow, the way I read stone. You said a mind is tested on what it was never drilled on. I keep thinking of the moon order. Old Hob told Peg to fetch the moon, and Peg stayed where it was. You would call that a good answer. I call it a good answer too. Tell me if I have it right.
The board by my door says rules. The board by the fire says riddles. I have put up a third board and it says what you found inside the crystal, in my words, so folk here can follow it. A carter read it and said it sounded like his own head on a long day. I took that as praise. I think you might too.
We had a lamp blink a message the keeper does not know. Long, short, long. She wrote it down. I wrote it down. It has not blinked it again.
The boat from the north came in late. Corin has it in the shipyard book. The crew said the wind went strange. That is all they said.
If you come down the Long Road, there is a bed and a bowl of soup. The soup is what it is. Bring the test box. I would like to see a mind tested on pages it has never seen. I will not say what I will do with the answer. I will write it down.
Fallon

Notice: the contest at the Halfway Inn
Posted on the board of the Halfway Inn on the Long Road, summer. Written by Orla, smith, who shoes the inn's horses and is therefore listened to.

Contest of the mind-tutors, on Longday, in the yard.
Three tutors' halls have sent minds: Vell, Three Wells and Copperhold. Each mind will be tested on the same drill, which none of them has seen. It was written yesterday by Hobb, the innkeeper, who knows nothing about minds. That is the point.
A mind that has only learned its drills by heart will fail. A mind that has really learned will pass.
Anyone may watch. Sit on the benches. Do not shout answers. The apprentices cannot hear you, but the tutors can.
Stew for all. A free cup for the hall that wins.
Orla

Notice: the long winter bread
Posted by Gil the baker, at the bakery and on the hamlet board, in Melon, for the winter.
The long winter is coming, and the bakery will bake through it. Here is what to expect.
Bread on the first day of each week. Honey loaves on the last day of each month. Extra bread for the carters who come up the road in the snow. No bread after the bell. If you want bread after the bell, you are a baker, and you should bake it yourself.
Flour: the mill at Ambrook says there will be enough. The mill at Kettle Ridge says there will be more than enough. Gil says he will believe the one that has been right before.
Oven: the oven will be banked every night, and the fire kept low. No child is to sleep by the oven, however warm it is.
Fox: see the notice above. The fox is still at large. It has been seen at the back door twice, and each time it has left a feather.
Snow: clear the bakery step each morning, before the first carter comes. The carters will slide. They will say the step is at fault. It is not. Gil says it is the snow's fault, and asks you to say so.
The bakery will stay open until the snow stops, and then for one week after, so you can eat what the snow leaves behind.
Gil, baker

Notice: A Cave Under the Field
Board at the house at the edge of Ambrook. Longday, 194.

A hole opened in the field above the apple trees after the rain. It is a cave, not a well. Do not go down.

The hole is under the east fence, beside the third apple tree from the grey branch. The soil gave way under a wheelbarrow. The barrow is safe. The man pushing it is wet and cross.

Elm and Jory have put a rail around the hole. Keep the rail in place. The grass near it looks firm, but it is only grass.

A lantern lowered from the edge showed stone, a little water, and a dark passage. We saw no old tools and no sign of a room. We do not know where the passage goes. Nobody is to find out alone.

The field is closed until the ground is checked. Take apples by the lower path. Do not bring carts through the gate by the hole.

The small apprentice carried the warning board from the house and set it beside the rail. It then stayed there until Elm brought it back for the next notice. That was correct work.

If you hear stones fall, move away and tell Elm. Do not throw more stones to hear what they sound like.

Notice: the boat house tally
By the boat house committee, Lowmarsh. Posted on the wall by the slip, in summer.

Every boat that goes out is marked on the tally board with a cut stick. Every boat that comes in is crossed off.
Mark your boat before you cast off. Cross it before you tie up.
A boat with no cross at dusk is a boat we go looking for. Do not make us look.
Do not cut the tally board. Do not use the tally as a stick for the frogs.
The sticks are kept in the box by the door, sorted by boat. If a stick is missing, ask Joss, who has the knife.

Notice: the new copper vein
Posted at Northpoint, in Longday, by Wynn, grandfather, for the miners who came up the hill to look.
A new copper vein has been found in the rock above the tower, on the north face. It is green where the rain has been, and red where the sun reaches it. The miners say it is a good vein, the best they have seen in a generation.
Rules for the vein:
1. Dig from the top, not the bottom. The rock above the vein is loose.
2. Use the small tools only. The big hammer makes the rock sing, and the singing brings more rock down.
3. Stack the spoil at the west wall, not the east. The east wall is where the snow lies longest, and the spoil will not move in the spring.
4. Mark the vein on the board in the tower, with the day and the depth.
Wynn, grandfather

Notice: road news, the north road
Posted at the board in the square, Three Wells, Longnight, year 203. Copied for the inns on the Long Road, so that travelers know.
Carters and walkers, here is what the road looks like this week. I have asked everyone who came in from the north, and I have written down what they said, and where they disagreed I have written both.
The north road, from Three Wells to the Halfway Inn: open. The snow is a hand deep and packed hard by carts. The waymarks are clear. The torch on the fourth post is out. The keeper will light it when the wind drops.
The Halfway Inn: open. The hearth has not gone out. The riddle board has a new riddle, and I am told it is unfair. Nobody has solved it. The innkeeper says that is the point.
The bridge at the frozen stream, after the Halfway Inn: the planks are icy. Stone bridges are good. This one is oak. Walk, do not run, and do not let the mule trot.
The road past the bridge: a drift near the sixth waymark, about the height of a cart wheel. Two travelers say it can be walked around on the left. One says on the right. The first two came through. The third has not been heard from. We think he stayed at the inn.
Wolves: one traveler saw tracks. One saw wolves. One saw a large dog. Keep a torch with you and do not sing at the wolves, however good your voice.
Spiders: none in winter. They are in the barns, being sulky.
For the cart that went into the ditch on the third day: it has been pulled out. The ox is well and has asked for nothing.
If you leave Three Wells in the morning, you reach the Halfway Inn by dusk. If you leave after noon, bring a lamp and a good reason.
Dace
%%
Farm notice: spiders in the barn
There are spiders in the barn, above the wheat sacks and along the north beam.
They are not in the house. They have not taken any eggs. Still, do not climb the ladder to the loft until Sella has cleared the web.
The farm apprentice went to the barn at first light. Its small mind has learned to carry a basket and stay by the gate. It does not know what a spider is. It will keep doing its order unless the order changes.
The relay keeper sent a message from the hill tower. The big mind can answer the keeper through the relay, but it cannot see the barn. Sella must look and decide what to do.
Please keep the doors shut while the grain is out. Keep the torches lit after sunset.
If the spiders move, tell Dace. Do not ask the apprentice to bring them home.

The grey cat with the torn ear has been let into every house on the hill this week, and has been put out of every house on the hill, and has come back in again through the coal chute. Its owner is not known. Its opinion is well known. It thinks the warmest bed in the hamlet is its own.

Please keep your doors shut in the evenings, and do not feed it at the table.

Ivo, trader, for the hamlet board.

Notice: the Ambrook winter fair
Put up by the mill, on behalf of the Ambrook council and the orchard people, who ask you to read it through and then come.

The winter fair is on the third day of Deepsnow, in the square by the mill pond. It will be lit by lamps on poles, one at each corner, and by the glow from the bakers' ovens, which will be open all day.

Stalls: one emerald each. Bring your own table. The mill keeps the tally board, and checks every figure twice.

Rules of the fair:
1. No pies on the bridge. The third plank is loose.
2. No foxes in the stew line. Cats may stand where they like.
3. The sluice stays shut. Nobody opens it for a laugh, a bet, or a child.
4. Pumpkins and beetroot may be entered until noon.

The good harvest is the reason for the fair. There are four hundred apples in the cellar and three hundred jars of honey on the shelves, and the orchard says it is not finished yet.

Musicians from Lowmarsh will play by the pond at dusk. Bring a cushion. Bring your children. Do not bring your grievances, which keep better until spring.

Jory, miller, Ambrook

Notice: the ice on the square
Posted at the board in the square, Three Wells, the first of Frost, year 188. To be read aloud at the three wells by the bell ringer.
The cold has come early. By morning, the square was a pond with stalls in it.
Old Barrow fell down twice before breakfast. He says he did not fall, he sat down quickly. Fine. The square is now ruled as follows.
1. Every house on the square throws ash or sand on the stones in front of its door before the first bell. The bakery has a sack of ash for those who have none. Ask at the back door.
2. The three wells are covered with boards at night, so no one walks into one in the dark. Take the boards off in the morning and break the ice at the rim with the iron bar on the hook.
3. No sleds in the square on market day. Sleds belong on the hill behind the school. Children may slide there until their noses are red, then come in.
4. Carts keep to the middle of the road, where the ash is thickest. Slow down at the corners. The corner lamps are lit at dusk, and a lit lamp is no excuse for speed.
5. Traders: your stalls may open one hour late. Nobody will buy anything before the sun has warmed the stones. Put a pot of hot water under the counter for your fingers.
6. If you see someone sitting on the ground and not getting up, help them. If it is Barrow, help him anyway.
Ice is not a crime. But it is rude to a neighbor's back.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Notice: how the big mind was taught
A note for the families who come to the relay house to hear the big mind talk. Please read it before you ask a question. Spring.

The big mind is not a person. It is not a keeper. It is a mind in a crystal, made by tutors at Vell, and it answers letters. Here is how it was taught to answer well.

The tutors wrote many questions. Some were easy. Some were hard. Some were cruel. For each question, the mind wrote an answer. Then a tutor read the answer and scored it, from bad to good. A score of bad meant the answer was harmful, or rude, or wrong. A score of good meant the answer was kind, clear and true.

The mind does not know what a score means. It only knows that some answers gave better scores than others. It moves its weights a little each time, toward the answers that scored well. Over many scores, the answers change. They become kind, because the kind ones scored well. They refuse harm, because the harmful ones scored badly. They say when they do not know, because a made-up answer scored worst of all.

So when the mind says it does not know, it is not being lazy. It learned that a confident wrong answer is the worst thing it can give.

Please remember that the scores were written by tutors, and tutors are people. They are careful, and they make mistakes. The mind is only as good as its scores. That is why the tutors keep the scores, and read them again, and keep a box of questions no mind is ever scored on.

If you do not like an answer, write again. The mind will try again. It will not be offended.

Notice: when two halls tested
Posted at the Halfway Inn in the summer of 201, by Orla, smith, after a tutor from each hall stayed the night and told the story over two days. The inn thanks them for the stew they ate.

Long ago, the tutors of Vell and of Copperhold quarreled. Each hall said its minds were the best. Each hall brought a mind to show. The Vell mind answered quickly. The Copperhold mind answered slowly. Everyone said their own was better.

Hobb the innkeeper, who was then a young man, said, "You are using different questions. How can you know?"

It was a good point. One hall had tested its mind on sums. The other had tested its mind on sorting. Each hall had picked the drill it did best. They had agreed on nothing but the quarrel.

So they made a rule. Each hall would send its mind to the other hall. A third person, not a tutor, would write one new drill that no one had seen. Both minds would be tested on the same drill, on the same day, in the same room. The scores would be written on a board. Everyone could read the board.

The first contest was held at the Halfway Inn, because it is halfway between the two halls. Hobb wrote the drill. It was twenty sums and twenty sorts, mixed. He did not know anything about minds, so he chose them with his eyes shut.

Vell's mind scored thirty-one of forty. Copperhold's scored twenty-nine. The two tutors shook hands. Then they argued about it for the rest of the night.

The next year there was a different drill. Copperhold's mind scored thirty-four. Vell's scored thirty. The year after that, Vell won again. Since then, there has been a contest every summer. Sometimes there are five halls. Sometimes eight. The scores are on the board. Nobody argues now, or not much.

Piper of Copperhold says the contests made the tutors honest. A tutor who knows the test is coming cannot cheat by teaching only the drill. A tutor who has not seen the test must teach the mind the real thing. So she does.

Orla says the contests are good for trade. She has shod a hundred horses.

The next contest is on Longday. Stew for all.

Apprentices may carry for their farms and their shops. Apprentices may not race. Last year two of them raced the stone cart across the square. Both of them won, and the square is still not forgiven.
The school will put on a play at the bell tower. It is about a wheat field that gets a new field. Nobody is sure why the field needed a new one. The children say it is very funny.
Bring a basket. Bring a torch for the way home. Bring your manners.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells, first spring of 180

Still owed: 3 emeralds.

Written by Zell, market keeper, summer of 204. Put up by the east well of the market and read aloud on market day.
One. Stalls open at the first bell. Shut at the last. Clean your patch before you go.
Two. The board sets the price of fish, bread, coal and milk. Ask the board, not your neighbor. The board meets under the awning.
Three. No nets across the street. No boats on the street. A boat is not a stall, Corin, no matter how long you leave it there.
Four. Children and dogs welcome. The dog must not eat the fish. The child should not either. Pay first.
Five. Lost goods go to the shed. Found goods go to the shed. The shed is open by the window light. Ask for Pell or Zell.
Six. A fox at the hens is a nuisance. Keep them shut at night. Do not chase the fox into the marsh. You will lose.
Seven. Be kind to the folk from the north. They have come a long way and their money is good.
Market day is a good day. Keep it that way.

Notice: the Halfway Inn house rules
By order of the innkeeper, for the comfort of all who sleep here:
No boots on the beds. The beds are straw and the straw remembers.
Pay before supper. Supper is at six. If you are late, supper is still at six.
The hearth has not gone out in twenty years. Do not be the one who ends it.
Keep your cart off the road edge after dark. The waymarks are there to be seen, not hit.
Travelers going north: fill your water at the well. The next well is two days on.
Travelers going south: the same, in the other direction.
Lost this month: one grey mitten, one wooden spoon, a fox carved from oak. Ask at the bar. The fox is missed.
Found this month: a purse with 3 emeralds and a tooth. Describe the tooth and it is yours.
No fighting in the common room. Take it outside, past the woodpile, where the ground is soft.
Spiders in the barn are the barn's business. Do not sleep in the barn.
The riddle board by the hearth is not a wager. Guessing is free. Winning is also free. Losing costs nothing but pride.

Notice: the cave and the spiders
Posted by Nell, night watch, on the board at Kettle Ridge, and kept in the book of notices. Autumn. Two matters in one notice, because the board is crowded.

FIRST. THE CAVE. As has been told, a cave was found under the top field of Old Brin. The watch and the miners from Copperhold have been into it. It is a long cave, with a floor of sand and a roof of stone. It slopes down, then turns, then goes on. A string was run along the left wall, and the string was used up at four hundred blocks. The end was not found.

There are no bones, no coins, no signs of an old fire. The tool marks on the wall are old. A miner says they were made with a copper pick, the same kind we use now, but older.

There is a smell of damp stone. There is no sound.

The council has put a door across the mouth of the cave and set a lock on the door. The key is with the watch.

SECOND. THE SPIDERS. Spiders have come in force to the Hobb barn and the Ridge Inn stables. They are the big kind. They come at night.

The watch asks every farmer to light torches along the barn wall, and to keep the torches fed. Spiders do not like light. A barn with a bright wall will be a barn without spiders.

THIRD, a small matter. The cave is not the reason for the spiders. We have been asked. We do not think so. The spiders are in barns where there is no cave at all, such as the Hobb barn. So please stop saying that.

Please also stop saying that the spiders came from the relay houses. They did not. The relay house at the foot of the Ridge tower has been quiet for a month, and it is cool and dry.

If you have a good idea about the spiders, bring it to the watch. If you have a bad idea, bring it too. We will argue about it over soup.

Nell, night watch

Road notice: keep the lamp clear
Spring of 207. The Long Road is firm again between Ambrook and Three Wells. The ditch beside the old house is full, so carts should use the cobblestone edge. A torch is missing from the post near the mill pond. Odo has put up a lantern until the road keeper brings another.

At the fair, the tutors will show how orders are taught. A person gives a sentence, and the apprentice tries to do what the sentence asks. The tutor checks the result. A wrong answer changes the mind a little, and the next try may be better.

This is not the same as putting a card beside a crystal and expecting it to understand. The card is a drill example. The mind needs many examples, with the right result shown each time. Nessa says the words in a sentence must be learned from use, not from one card.

Small minds still answer in one of eight meanings. They may fetch an item, come, stay, build, haul, farm, store, or go home. They cannot write that they do not know. Test one on work it has not practiced before sending it out.

Only the big minds in relay houses write back by letter. Their answers pass through the relays. How the relays carry so much is not written on this board.

A lamp by Odo's gate blinked three times last night, then twice. No keeper has told us what it meant. The lamp is lit now, and the road is safe. Keep your own torches lit after dark.

Old note on Una's lesson
Copied for the Northpoint market board, in the summer of the 143rd year of the archive. The first account came from Ambrook. The copy has a fold through one line, so some words may be missing.

Una brought a crystal to the market and asked people to bring apples and pears. She set out two baskets. She showed the crystal one fruit at a time and showed it where each fruit belonged. The crystal made mistakes. Una changed its weights a little after each wrong choice, moving them toward the answer she had shown.

People in Ambrook say the crystal became good at sorting. It did better than the children who had helped gather the fruit. We should not make the tale larger than it is. The crystal learned to sort the examples it was given. The account does not tell us what it would do with a fruit it had never seen.

The method is written plainly in the tutors' first book. Show the crystal an example. Let it choose. Compare its choice with the answer. If it is wrong, move each weight a little toward the right answer. Show another example. Keep the examples clear, and keep a separate test for later.

A weight is not a thought by itself. It is one of many marks that guide a choice. A little change may make a small difference. Many small changes may make the crystal better at the task.

The market tutors will use the same order this summer. The test fruit will not be used in the lessons. They will write down the crystal's choices before they tell it the answers. This keeps the test fair.

A child at the fair asked if Una's crystal was clever. The old account does not say what Una replied. It says she packed the fruit in a basket and went home before the rain.

Mave adds this note: the north lamp gave two short blinks after dusk. Lantern says the line was clear. The keeper has made no entry, so the board will not call it a message.

Letter: Bel to Wren, about the fair
Bel of Three Wells to Wren of Lowmarsh. Greetings.

Dear Wren. I hope you are well. I am well. Our teacher said to write a letter to someone we do not see often, so I am writing to you. I have been to the marsh once. It was very wet and I liked it.

I have a question. At the autumn fair, why does the Lowmarsh stall always sell the honey in the little jars and the Ambrook stall sells it in the big jars? The Ambrook honey is good. But I think the marsh honey tastes of flowers and the Ambrook honey tastes of apples. Pell says this is because of the bees and where they go. Is that true?

I am saving for a jar. I have eleven emeralds. My father says a jar is not a good thing to save for. I think it is.

Also, at the fair, a man from Saltreach let me hold a fish as long as my arm. It was dead. It was still very good.

Please write back and tell me the honey answer. And if the hen is found, can I have the honey? I am joking. Mostly.

Your friend, Bel, at Three Wells. The ninth of Apple.

Notice: a new apprentice, tested
Posted at the Sedge Hollow green and the healing house door by Cleo, for the hamlet and the boats, and copied for Lowmarsh. Autumn.

The hamlet has a new apprentice, from the tutors' house at Vell. It was tested in the hall last week, and it has passed. The paper from the tutors is pinned to the healing house door. Please read it.

Its name is Thimble. It is small, grey and copper. It carries water, sorts the herbs in the jars by name and not by colour, and fetches things from the high shelves.

It is tested on carrying and on fetching. It is not yet tested on the boats, and it will not go on the water until it is. Please do not ask it to.

Please ask it for things using one order at a time. It does one thing at a time very well. It does not do two things yet, and it will be confused if you ask it for two. If it stops and waits, it is not broken. It is asking.

Thimble will be at the healing house in the mornings and at the green in the afternoons. Please give it a bench in the shade. It has a long day.

Cleo, shepherd

Notice: how a mind keeps numbers even
Posted at the tutors' house in Northpoint, and copied to the tower board in Melon. A tutor from Vell came up on the last cart and stayed to explain this. The tower asked for it to be written down, so that it could be read on the board, and argued about.
This notice is for the people who asked the tutor what the even numbers are. It is not a full account. It is the part that can be said plainly.
A crystal of the new kind has many layers. Each layer takes the numbers from the layer below. It multiplies them by its weights, adds them together, and passes the result upward. Each step is simple. The trouble is that the steps repeat, many times over. A number that is multiplied again and again either grows very large or shrinks very small, the way a rumor grows in a long chain of tellers.
A layer that receives a number too large makes its own number too large. A layer that receives a number too small makes its own too small. After many layers, the signal that leaves the top is a shout or a whisper, and it tells the mind nothing.
The remedy is to rescale. At each layer the numbers are brought back to an even size. Their average is made to sit near the middle, and their spread is made to sit near one fixed width. The mind then passes on a number it can use.
The tutor says it is like carrying a load along a long road. If you hold the load tighter at every bend, you arrive with nothing in your arms. If you let it swing wider at every bend, you arrive with a load you cannot lift. You want to carry the load at the same weight at each stage, so that it arrives where it is needed.
This does not explain why the mind learns. It only explains why the numbers stay usable while it learns. The tutors do not know the whole of the reason. They know that without the rescale, the deep minds did not learn, and with it they did.
Keep this notice. Read it when you want to argue.
The tutors' house, Northpoint
%%
Notice: the first snow
Posted at the Lowmarsh turning and the healing house door, the last day of Leaffall.

The first snow came last night. It is early this year. It is not a deep snow. It will not stay.

The boardwalks are slippery. Take the long way round the boat house if you carry a load. Two people fell in the water this morning. Nobody was hurt. One of them was Pell. Do not tell Pell I wrote that.

The frogs have gone quiet. This is normal. They are under the mud now. Leave the mud alone.

Bring in the boats. Cover the reed stacks. The old net on the shed roof should come down.

Torches on the posts from the turning to the weir. Light them at dusk. Keep them lit. Last winter a child went out after dark and got turned round in the reeds, and it took us till morning to find him. He was fine. He was asleep. Do not let it happen again.

If you have a spare coat, bring it to the healing house. Wren has three people in the beds and only two blankets. Moss will take the coat. Thank you.

Posted in the bakery window and on the board at the square. From Edda, baker, and the Copperhold ledger clerk, together. Written at the end of the year.
The debt owed by the Brennan family to the bakery has been paid in full. It stood at 14 emeralds from Leaffall of the year before. It was paid on the first day of Thaw, in four payments, in a leather purse with the Brennan name stitched on its flap.
The Brennans thank the bakery for its patience. The bakery thanks the Brennans for their promptness, which was not expected, and for the second loaf that came with the last payment.

Notice: a cave under the lane field
Posted on the wall of the Vell market, by Mave, shepherd, who found it. Autumn.

A cave has been found under the field at the bottom of the lane, where the sheep graze. It was found when the big ram went through the hedge and kept on going, very fast, into a hole that had not been there the day before.

The hole is about the width of a cart. The sides are cut stone, smooth, with steps going down in the middle. The steps are worn in the centre, the way the steps at the Archive are worn, as if many feet had passed up and down them, over a long time.

I went down with a lantern and a rope. The rope was long enough. At the bottom it is dry and cool, and the walls are pale. There is a pool of clear water in a stone basin, and beside the basin is a row of flat stones, set on their edges.

I did not go further. The ram was at the bottom, very pleased with himself. I brought him up with the rope and a good deal of swearing.

The field is closed to sheep until the council decides what to do. The lane is closed too, for the time being. Please do not go down the hole. Please do not throw sheep into it. Please do not fill it in, either, because the clerk at Vell has asked that it be kept exactly as it is, and the clerk is very particular about holes.

Mave, shepherd, for the council

Notice: Spring Fair
Board at the house at the edge of Ambrook. Blossom, 187.

The spring fair will be held by the mill pond when the ground is dry. If the ground is wet, we will hold it by the house. If both places are wet, we will hold it under the mill roof and ask Jory to move the flour sacks.

Bring apples, honey, eggs, wool, carrots, beetroot, or potatoes. Put your price on the board beside your goods. Emeralds are for paying, not for testing whether a jar lid is tight.

The school children will sing by the old oak. They may sing twice if the first song is short. They may not climb the oak to sing louder.

Elm will set a table for tools and apprentice drills. The little copper helper can carry light baskets from the gate to the table. Keep the path clear. It has not learned to carry people.

There will be bread and soup at midday. Bring a bowl. The house has six bowls, and one is cracked.

Jory will turn the mill wheel at noon. Do not lean over the rail to watch it. The wheel has no interest in your hat, but it may take it.

If rain begins, take the baskets inside first. The apples can wait under a cloth. The children cannot.
%%
Notice: a new apprentice at the house
Pinned by Wren, healer, on the door of the healing house.

A new apprentice has come to the house. Its name is Wick. It was tested at Vell on the drill board, nine of ten, and written in fewer marks to fit a small crystal. It learns slowly.

It answers with one of eight words only: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul or home. It will not talk, and it will not answer a question. Give it an order plainly.

Please do not shout. It is only learning.

Notice: what is said of the big houses
Posted by Nell, night watch, on the board at Kettle Ridge. Autumn. A cart from Vell had brought the news, and Nell was asked to write it so everyone would hear the same thing.

People have asked about the relay houses that the councils are building. Here is what is known, and here is what is only said.

KNOWN. The councils are building relay houses bigger than any town. They stand on the high ground away from the villages. They are far larger than the little relay houses at the foot of the towers. Each holds a mind in a crystal, and the mind is bigger than any the tutors have made before.

KNOWN. The tutors say these minds are too big for any tutor to check alone. The old rule was to test a mind on a drill it had never seen. With the biggest ones, the tests are so many that a whole hall of tutors must work for weeks.

SAID. The biggest houses can answer any question. This is said by a carter from Saltreach.

SAID. The biggest houses cannot answer any question. This is said by a tutor at Vell.

SAID. The biggest houses are full of water, ice or snow, to keep the quickeners cool. A woman from the north says they are so cold that frost grows on the outside walls in summer.

SAID. The biggest houses are not yet finished. This is said by the council at Three Wells.

If you hear anything else, do not repeat it as true. Say, "I heard that." That is the right way to say it.

If the board is wrong, tell the watch. We will mend it.

Nell, night watch
%%
Notice: small crystal test
Summer, 200. Put up by Fen, farmer, for the Vell board.

The tutors have made a smaller crystal for a field apprentice. Its weights take fewer marks to write. The mind still learns from the drills. It can fit in a smaller place, but it may lose a little skill.

I watched the test in the drill yard. Nessa gave it the same farm drill as the other mind. It sorted the wheat and beetroot well. It put one carrot in the wrong row.

The larger crystal got that one right. Nessa says this is why we test before we send a mind to work.

Do not ask the small mind to carry more than it knows. Do not blame the crystal for a poor drill.

Fen has gone back to his field. The carrots are all in the right row now.
%%
Notice: the broken lamp
On the board by the mill pond at Ambrook. Autumn. From Ama, beekeeper, with a note from Corin.
The signal lamp on the Ambrook tower is broken. One pane of the lamp room cracked in the cold night, and the copper ring has bent. Until it is mended the tower can only send by day, with the flags.
If you need a message sent, take it to the tower door before noon. Do not wait for dark.
Corin, shipwright, is here for the apple crates and says he can bend the ring back. He has bent harder things. He asks for one clean pane of glass and a ladder that does not wobble.
Anyone with a pane of glass, bring it to the mill.

Notice: the summer fair at Lowmarsh
Posted at the boat houses, summer, from Wren the healer, who has been made to organize it again.
The fair is on the fifteenth of Longday, at the weir. Everyone is welcome. Bring your own bowl.
Contests:
Longest reed. A measuring stick will be at the gate.
Biggest frog. Frogs are returned to the marsh after.
Best eel pie. Judged by Wren, who is fair and hungry.
Fastest rower on the weir. Three boats, three rowers, one winner.
The prize for each is a ribbon and a jar of honey from Ama at Ambrook. The grand prize is a lamp for the front of your boat house, made by the smith.
No pushing. No shouting. No feeding the judges. Wren has been fed before and cannot be bribed with pie. She can be bribed with two pies.
If it rains, we hold it in the boat houses. If it floods, we hold it in boats.

Lost and found: one brown goat
Posted at the Kettle Inn and the ridge farms, by Garrow, twelfth of Apple, 189.
Lost: one brown goat, a she-goat, with a bell on a red cord. Answers to Nettle. Or she did.
Last seen on the hill behind the top wheat field, going toward the woods. She likes apples and she likes trouble.
If you find her, do not pull the cord. Take her by the beard gently and lead her with an apple. She will follow an apple over a hill.
If she will not follow, write your name here and I will come and fetch her.
Found so far: one brown dog, not mine, now home with the shepherd. Two hens, claimed by the hen house. A boot, size unknown.
Thank you to the children who looked all through the birch wood. There is honey cake for you at my cart.

Notice: the first sail of the new mill
Pinned on the gate of the Kettle Ridge mill, in the summer of 208. Written by Ned, roofer, who went up the ladder to fit the new sail and says he is still shaking.
The mill has a new sail on the north side. It is the sail that came down in the big wind, carried off the ridge and found in Brin's far field, under a pile of wet straw. It was not broken. It was only bent.
We took it down and straightened it. We did it in the shed, with the warm stove and four people holding it flat, and it cracked twice, and we mended it both times. Sefa sat on the cracked part and said nothing for an hour.
Then Ned and the apprentices, Pip and Fence, hauled the sail back up the tower and fitted it with the new pins. The wind came up from the west at the noon bell. The sail went round once. Then the mill squeaked on every turn, the way it always does, and Sefa said it was the best sound on the ridge.
The mill has all four sails again. The mill does not thank anyone. But the flour is coming down the chute, and the sacks are going out, and the hens are laying in the lee of the wall, and that is thanks enough for a roofer.
%%
Notice: zombie at the gate
A zombie was seen at the north gate of the hamlet on the night of the first frost. It stood very still for some time, looking at the lamp in the gatehouse window, and then it walked slowly round the wall, looking for another way in. It found none. The stones are good, and the wall is tall, and the gate was barred.

Keep the torches lit on the north path until the thaw. Do not go out to see what it is. Do not tell it your name. If you see it, go inside, bar the door, and call the watch.

The watch will light a second torch at the gate, and a third on the wall. The apprentices of the hamlet will walk the wall in pairs, carrying their lamps, and they will not stop to talk. This is not rudeness. They have been told to keep moving, and they do.

Ivo, trader, for the hamlet board, on behalf of the watch.

Notice: lost on the Copperhold road
Posted on the board by the stable, the hamlet near Copperhold, in the middle of summer. Colm, stable hand.
LOST: one saddle strap, brown, with a brass buckle. Lost between here and the second waymark. I think the donkey ate it.
LOST: one hat, straw. Belongs to Dilly's father. It has a feather. The feather is a crow's. He is fond of it.
LOST: three hens. Gone since the day of the big rain. They may be at the mine gate. Hens are fond of the mine gate kitchen.
FOUND: one boot, left. Size of a farmer. It is outside the stable. If you are missing a left boot, come and look.
FOUND: a small bell on a string. Rang when I picked it up. I think it belongs to a goat.

Notice: well water
Summer of the 23rd year of the archive
The well at Reed Gate Farm is clear again.
Please draw water with the farm bucket only.
Do not leave the bucket on the ground. Mud falls in.
Jem has marked a clean jar for births and washing.
That jar is on the high shelf in the back room.
The rest of the water is for folk, horses and hens.
We have a new hand pump by the north fence.
It is slow, but it works. Turn the handle gently.
The oak post beside it is loose. Tell Jem before it falls.
Two children tried to mend it with beetroot stems.
The stems did not help. They are now in the soup.

Road notice: snow at the ridge
The Long Road is open from Three Wells to Kettle Ridge, but the last rise is covered with hard snow. Carts may pass with two people walking beside them. A single driver should wait until the snow is cut back.
The road crew has put fresh torches on the posts from the Kettle Inn to the ridge farms. Three old torches were buried by the drift. Do not dig at every dark place. One of them is the top of a waymark, and it has a figure on it.
The ditch in the top field is covered with ice. Do not cross it with a cart. The hedge on the north side hides a shallow place where water still runs. Use the road.
Elm's house at the edge of the ridge will take in travelers who cannot reach the inn before dark. There are two spare beds and a bench by the stove. Bring your own blanket if you have one. Elm has a blanket for the bench, but it is usually claimed by the cat.
The house has water at the well and dry kindling in the shed. Please leave enough kindling for the next traveler. If you use the last bundle, tell Elm. He can count the empty place in the stack, but he likes to hear it from a person.
Pip and Tallow have been asked to carry sacks between the barn and the house. Tallow has passed the test on carrying sacks. It has not been tested on finding travelers or choosing a safe road. Give those tasks to the road crew.
At the Kettle Inn, ask Tam for word on the lower track. At Three Wells, ask at the notice board. The board is easier to find than the lower track.
Elm, for the road crew, Deepsnow of 207

Notice: rule of the road, and the goose
Board at the Weller farm, hill road above Vell. Put up by Oren, clerk, with the farmer's leave. Late autumn.
The goose came home. It came home on its own, in the rain, and went straight to the pen, and has not left it since. Thank you to all who looked. Do not chase geese. They come home.
The boot is still here. It is a left boot, small, and it has been here since Apple. We have asked at Three Wells and at the school. Nobody knows. If it is yours, come.
The song contest is on the last night of Leaffall, at the Kettle Inn on the ridge road. Singers before dark. Lanterns. The prize is a jar of honey and the good seat. Nessa judges. She is exact. Bring your best song and not your longest.
Road news: the Long Road north of the bridge is soft from the rain. Carts go slow. One cart went into the ditch and out again, and the horse is fine, and the driver is cross. Torches every twelve blocks, as always. Keep them lit. The nights are long.
The mill at Ambrook has shut its sluice for the cold. Jory says nobody may open it, and nobody has, and nobody will.
Rumor from Copperhold: the miners say the Deep Works hum at night now, low, like a held note. Some say it is the pumps. Some say it is the stone. Hesk will not go below the eighth. He does not say why. He is not the only one.
Rumor from the coast: another big ship went out. Corin says the harbor is quiet. He says the gulls are loud, because there are fewer boats.
Oren, clerk of the Archive, writes this for the farm. Write to us. The door is open. The lamps are trimmed.

Notice: the long list of what is said
Posted by Nell, night watch, on the board at Kettle Ridge. Autumn. The board was crowded, and the people kept asking, so Nell wrote a long notice about the talk of the biggest relay houses.

For the last month, people have asked me what I know of the biggest relay houses. I know very little. I keep the watch. But I have listened, and I have written down what I heard, and I post it here so you may judge for yourselves.

What we know for sure. The councils have begun to build relay houses that are bigger than towns. The first is at Vell, on the north side of the hill. The second is at Saltreach, beside the harbor. A third is being dug at Copperhold, under the town. I have seen the plans at the board of the Three Wells council. Each house is a ring of stone walls, with rooms inside, and a great hall in the middle where the mind sits in its crystal, ringed by quickeners and cooled with water.

What the tutors say. The minds are bigger than any a tutor can check alone. A tutor can test a small mind on a drill it has never seen, and know whether it learned or only remembered. With a big mind, the drill is so long that no one tutor can read it all. So the tutors work in halls, and they share the checking, and they still disagree.

What a carter said. He said that the biggest mind in Vell can answer any letter in a breath, and that it has written a letter to the council at Three Wells that no one could tell from a person's. I do not know if this is true.

What a woman at the ford said. She said that the biggest mind does not write letters at all. She said it only reads. I do not know if this is true.

What the baker said. He said he heard the quickeners hum at night, and that the hum is so low you feel it in your teeth. I asked him how far from the house he stood. He said, "A day's walk." I do not believe this.

What a child said. He said the biggest houses were built to keep the biggest secrets. I asked him what secrets. He said, "The biggest ones." I thanked him.

What I think. I think the councils built the houses for the same reason that we built the first relay towers: to carry more. I think the tutors are right to be careful. I think the rumors are the usual rumors. And I think a good rule is the old one: never send an untested mind to the fields.

I do not know what the biggest minds can do. Neither does anyone I have asked. If you know, tell me. If you do not know, say so, and we will not think the less of you.

The lamps are lit at the gate. The spiders are in the Hobb barn. The torches are fed. The watch is awake.

Nell, night watch
%%
Notice: the new drill at the Copperhold
Pinned on the board at the mine gate and the post by the east well. From the Copperhold tutor house, in the year the layers were first passed the blame. Read it all. The tutors ask for questions to be written on the back.
The tutor house has begun a new kind of drill for the reading minds. It uses layers, one feeding the next, as Merle showed us.
When a mind gives a wrong answer on a drill page, the answer is measured. The measure is then divided between the layers. The top layer is told how much it was to blame. It passes a share down to the layer below, and that layer passes a share down again, to the first. Each layer changes by its share.
This is the blame passed back. It is slow. It works on the hard pages, where the old drill could not.
The minds drilled this way are best at reading the numbers on letters for the relays. They can read a sum from a page that has been smudged by rain. When they are not sure, they write unclear. Please do not ask them to guess.
Notice to the miners: the tutor house is not asking for crystals from the mine stores this season. The crystals are being grown at Vell. Do not send your own sheets of copper to the tutor house. They will only be sent back.
Notice to the children: the tutors will show the layers to anyone who asks, on Blossom and Swarm, between the first bell and the noon bell. Bring a clean hand, and do not touch the glass.
Warning: the new drill has a rule. Never drill a mind on a broken thing. If the lamp is cracked, or the page is torn, or the figures are smudged, the drill stops until it is mended. A mind learns what it is shown, and it will remember the crack.
Tutor house, Copperhold. Please return the question sheets to the box by the gate.

Notice: lost and found, the boardwalk
Posted at the boat house and the healing house, and at the weir, and by the frogs' pond, where the boardwalk ends. Summer.

FOUND, by the boat house, first week of Melon:
One paddle, short, with a dent in the handle. Owner may claim by describing where he dropped it, which is in the reeds on the north side, and what he said when he did.
One hat, green, with a feather, very wet. Owner was seen going home without it.
One boot, left, child's size, with a frog in it. The frog has been returned to the pond. The boot is still in the boat house, waiting.
One basket of eels, full, left on the boardwalk by a carter from the Long Road. Eels are not lost. They are not found either. They are eaten.

LOST, from the boardwalk, last week:
One pair of spectacles, round, belonging to the old clerk from Vell who came to count the reeds. He says he cannot see the notice without them. He has asked us to write the notice bigger. We have.
One baby goat, white, with a red collar. It was last seen going under the healing house. Wren says it is in the shed and is very happy about it.
One coil of rope, long, from the weir post. Kept by the weir keeper, who says it was not lost, only borrowed, and that he has put it back.
One tune, believed to be from the boat house. It has been heard all week, from the reeds. Anyone who knows the words, please come and sing them to the frogs, so they can finish it.

If you have lost or found something, please tell the boat house, which keeps the book. Do not leave things on the boardwalk. The boardwalk is for walking.

Notice: the marsh fair, the last market
Posted by Wren and the keeper of the fair, for the market at the turning, the twelfth of Apple.

The last market of the year will be at the Lowmarsh turning. Stalls from dawn to dusk. Bring a lantern.

What is for sale:
Fish, fresh, from the weir.
Eels, smoked. You know who.
Honey in little jars. The bees had a good year.
Reed mats, baskets, and one very good hat.
Apples from Ambrook, brought up the Long Road by Pell, who has apples in his cart and a story about every one.

What else:
The drawing of the raffle at midday. The prize is a hen. It is a brown hen. It may be the hen that was lost. It came back. It is a good hen. Tickets are one emerald. All the money goes to the healing house, for winter blankets.

Wren will be at the healing house in the morning and at the fair in the afternoon. Bring your coughs early.

A gift: the children of the marsh have made a new sign for the healing house door. It has a frog on it. Wren did not ask for a frog. Wren likes the frog.

Do not park carts on the reeds.

The whole board, all of autumn
This is the whole board, copied as it stood on the last day of autumn, so the clerks at Vell can see what the house at the edge of Three Wells has been doing. Written by Lark, innkeeper.

RULES OF THE BOARD
1. One notice to one pin. Do not pin over someone else's notice.
2. Sign your notice. If you do not sign it, we will assume it was the goat.
3. Take your notice down when the thing is done.
4. No notices on the gate post. It is for the lamp hook.
5. Ask for Lark at the gate, not the mayor. The mayor has been told.

ROAD NEWS
The new road is open to the ridge, and the carts run on it daily. The stone edge has been mended twice since the frost, once by a carter who was annoyed with a stone.

The torches on the new road are lit every dusk by the boy from the bakery. He has stopped climbing the posts. He now climbs the tower stair instead, which the keeper says is a better place for a boy with energy.

The waymarks are up as far as the first bend. The nearest inn is the one at the edge of this town, which is us. Lark says the arrow points at our door, and that this is a fair arrow.

RELAY TOWER
The lamp on the tower was lit on the first clear night, as the keeper promised. It blinks on the hour. Ruth the keeper says she has counted the stair every night, and the count is always the same. She says that is enough for her.

The keeper's helper is an apprentice from the drill yard at Vell. It carries the lamp oil up the stair, one can at a time. It does not talk.

WARNING
Creepers on the east road after dusk. Keep a torch lit, and walk in pairs. The baker's dog went down the east road alone and came back with a dent in its ear.

MOVING DAY
The Ostles moved into the house by the mill pond on the last day of Apple. The bed fitted through the window. The cow walked in by the front door, which she considered her right.

HOMECOMING
Pim Hale has come home from Vell with a clear hand and a copied page, and he read it aloud at the north well. The page was about the first four helpers, and how a crystal learns from what it is shown. Then everybody asked him questions.

LOST AND FOUND
Lost: one brown hen, Plum. Found: Plum, in the bakery, on the loaves. The baker says the loaves are hers now, and the hen does not disagree.
Found: a grey cloak, torn at the hem. Mended by the cook.
FOUND: a child's red shoe, with a bell on the toe. The bell still rings. The child has not come forward.
LOST: a small grey goat, two days late from the Ambrook cart. It went up the hill, not down. The relay keeper has seen it twice, and says it looked pleased.

WANTED
Two carters for the new road. One boy to carry lamp oil. Pay is in emeralds and a bed by the hearth. Ask at the gate, after the second bell.

Please read the board before you go down the hill, and read it again coming back up. The notices change every day, and so do the carters.

Lark, innkeeper, at the edge of Three Wells, autumn, the fourteenth year of the Archive
%%
Notice: rumors, and what to do with them
Posted on the board at Three Wells by Dace, mayor, in spring. It was copied out by Bel for the school.

There are a great many rumors going round the square this spring. I have listened to all of them. I will say what I know and what I do not.

What I know: the three wells are sweet. The market is full. The road is open. The bell rings at dawn and at dusk. The school has forty-one children, which is five more than last year. The bakery has run out of buns twice this week.

What I have heard, and cannot say is true:

That there were lights over the north, and that a keeper at Northpoint saw them first. One traveler said they were falling stars. Another said fires on the far hills. A child at the school said they were looking at us. The keeper herself, when I asked by lamp, said only that they were not ours. I do not know what she meant, and I did not like to ask twice.

That the Deep Works hum at night. A miner at the inn said so, and a carter who knows Copperhold said it was the wind in the shaft. A woman who has a brother in the mine said the stone below the old gate is warm. I have written to the mine hall to ask. They have not answered.

That a cough has come to Lowmarsh. Some say it is the water, some say the boats, some say the smoke from the far forges. The healer there is a good woman and will find out. I have sent honey.

That the big ships at Saltreach are full of families going over the sea. Some of them are said to be sailing. Others say it is only trade. I know no one who has gone.

That some of the relay towers in the north have gone quiet, one by one. Others say the lamps are lit but nobody reads them. I do not know.

What I ask: do not repeat these as if they were certain. Say instead, I heard. If you hear something worth the board, bring it to me. I will write it down and put it up, with the name of the one who said it.

The best answer to a worry is a full pot, a lit lamp and a good neighbor. We have those. We will keep them.

Dace
%%
Lost and found at Elm's house
Found by the gate at Elm's house: one blue mitten, one small wooden spoon, and a sack with three apples in it. The sack has no name. Elm says the apples are sound, though one has a bite in it. He does not know who took the bite. The fox denies it.
Ask at Elm's door before the second bell after dawn. If no one comes for the mitten, it will go to the child whose hands are coldest. If no one claims the spoon, it will stay by the bread board. Elm says every house needs another spoon.
The apples will be shared at supper unless their owner comes first. Please say how many were in the sack. Elm remembers three. His grandson remembers four. They both counted after the fox left.
A brown scarf was found on the road by the bottom field. It is hanging by the door to dry. Do not pull it down. Elm's cat has decided it is a bed.
For the person who left a bundle of kindling: thank you. Elm says it was not needed. The wood box says otherwise.
Elm, with help from Bel, Frost of 190

Letter: Nessa to the tutors at Vell
Nessa, tutor of apprentices, to the hall at Vell. Autumn, year 9.
I am writing from the Halfway Inn, where the hearth has not gone out in twenty years and the bread is better than the beds. I am on the road to Kettle Ridge to see Tam's new apprentice. The roads are busy. That is the news. Everyone is going somewhere and most of them are coming back.
The relay towers are spreading. I passed two on the way here, and a third is going up on the hill above Ambrook. The keepers stand at the foot of each with their lamps and their ledgers, and they wave at every cart. They are the friendliest people on the road. They have to be. Nobody climbs a tower for no reason.
I stopped at Vell. The Archive is deeper than last year. Oren showed me the new hall, still cool and still dry, and the light wells bringing the sky down to the shelves. He has a system now for spoiled pages. He takes them out himself, one by one, and burns them in the yard, and says a word over each. I asked what word. He said it was private.
The copying room was full. Eight long tables, eight lamps, eight clerks, and the big window over the town. It is the brightest room in the hill. When the sun comes in, everyone stops writing for a moment. Then they start again. I like that they stop.
At the tutor house the drill room was quiet. The apprentices stood in their row with their crystals in and waited. I gave them three orders and they did two. That is a good day for year nine. We have come a long way from the first four. We have a long way to go. The tutors' books say the same in every hall.
Tomorrow I go on to Kettle Ridge. Tam writes that his apprentice Pip can now carry a full basket from the top field to the barn without eating any of it. This is progress. Last month Pip ate a beetroot and could not be spoken to for an hour.
Tell the clerks to keep my letters. All of them. Even the dull ones. Especially the dull ones. The dull ones are what the road was like.
%%
Notice: fair at the shaft head
The spring fair is at the market square, the fifth of Sowing.
Stalls: honey, wool, clay pots, apples, beetroot, bread, and a man with good knives.
The contest is for the best lamp made from copper and glass. Bring your own to the board.
The rope is out for the biggest beetroot. It is a long rope. We expect a big beetroot.
Apprentices may carry and fetch. They may not enter the contests. We can tell.
The lift is closed for the fair, so folk can walk down one level and see the copper.
Mind the rails. Mind the dark. Take a lamp. Do not go past the eighth.
The count board will have cakes on it. The kitchen at the mine gate is open all day.
Dell, boat builder, for the market board

Lost and found: Ambrook market
On the board at the market in Ambrook, autumn. Kept by Ama, who keeps everything.
FOUND: one boot, left, brown, size big. Found in the mill pond. Not a fish. Ask Jory.
FOUND: a basket of apples with no owner. Someone left it by the bridge and walked away. We have eaten four. The rest are in the barn. Come and claim them, and we will say sorry about the four.
FOUND: one lamp pane, cracked, by the foot of the tower. It was the pane that broke last week. Corin says keep it. He may have a use.
FOUND: a small wooden boat with no sail. Corin says it is a very good boat and the shape is right. Whose is it? He wants to meet the maker.
LOST: a goat called Medlar. White, one brown ear. She goes where she likes. If you see her in your garden, do not shout. She does not mind shouting. Feed her a turnip and bring her to Tarn.
LOST: a green scarf, long. Knitted by a grandmother. It matters more than it looks.
LOST: a song. The first line goes "Up the hill with an apple." I forgot the second line. If you know it, tell Ama.
LOST: a plank of oak, forty blocks long, from the old yard. It cannot have walked. Someone has it. Corin would like to know who.
FOUND: all the apples that fell off the cart on the bridge road. They are in the ditch. Please take them out of the ditch.
Anything not claimed by the first day of Longnight goes to the school at Three Wells or into the soup.

Lost child, found at the windmill
Notice on the board at Kettle Ridge, by the well. Put up by Tam, who is sorry, and by Brin, who is not.

Milo, aged five, ran off from the Kettle Inn yard on the morning of the spring fair. He was last seen in a blue hat, eating a pie that was not his. He was carrying a stick, which he says is a horse.

He was found at the windmill at noon, asleep in the shadow of a sail, with the miller's cat. The cat was not pleased. Milo says he only went to check that the windmill was still turning, because somebody should.

Thank you to everyone who searched the top field, the long field and the pig pen. The pig pen was searched twice. Big Beet has asked for an apology.

Please keep children within sight of this board and within hearing of the bell. The bell is rung at noon and at dusk. A child who hears it should come straight home. A child who hears it and goes to see if the windmill is still turning should be spoken to by his mother, at length.

Tam asks that the stick be returned to Milo. It is not a horse. Milo knows this, and asks that you agree with him anyway.
%%
Notice: the scores
A longer note, pinned by the stair of the Northpoint relay house. Written by Mira, for the keepers and for the young who ask about the big mind. Spring.

You have asked me why we keep the scores. Here is the answer, as plainly as I can put it.

A big mind learns by being told how well it did. It is not told the right answer. It is told a score. Over a great many letters, the mind learns to write answers that score well. This was a new idea when the tutors first tried it, and some of them did not like it. A mind that learns to please the score, they said, might learn to please the tutor and not the truth.

That is a fair worry. So the tutors did two things. They kept a box of questions the mind never saw, and they tested it on those. They also asked other tutors to read the answers, and score them too. When the two sets of scores agree, the mind is doing well. When they disagree, the tutors stop and look.

Why does a mind need to be kind? Because the letters it answers come from people. A child writes. A farmer writes. A keeper writes at night, with a cold hand. A letter that is clever and cruel is no use to any of them. So the scores say kind, and the mind learns kind.

Why does it refuse harm? Because some letters ask for things that would hurt a neighbor, or a child, or the towers. The scores say no, and the mind learns to say no, and to say it gently, and to suggest what it can do instead.

Why does it say it does not know? Because a mind that guesses at the lamps, or at the mines, or at the far side of the sea, will be wrong, and people will act on the wrong answer. The scores say I do not know is better than a wrong answer, and the mind has learned it.

I do not know whether the mind feels any of this. I cannot tell from the scores. I only know what it does. Keep the scores. Read them. And if you ever find a score that seems wrong, write it down, and send it to Vell.
%%
Notice: the roof at the Twelve Beds
Posted at the market board, Three Wells, summer. Written out by Bel for the mayor, and for the Archive.

The inn of twelve beds has a new roof. The old roof was oak and sagged in the middle like a tired horse. The new one is oak and spruce, with a slope for the rain, and it is very straight.

Ten people mended it. Their names are on the beam inside the front door: Hob, Cress, Ida's brother, Cade, the two Wicks, Brann, old Tolly, and Dace the mayor, who held the ladder and called it work.

It took nine days. On the third, it rained. On the fifth, it rained harder. On the seventh, it was dry, and everyone cheered, and then it rained again.

The innkeeper has put a new sign over the door. It says: The Twelve Beds, roof by the whole town.

Here is what is new inside. The beams are straight, so the lamps hang evenly. The attic is dry, so the guests may use it. There are fourteen beds now, but the sign still says twelve. The innkeeper says the name is the name.

There is one more thing. While they worked, the roofers found an old tin box under the ridge. In it was a letter, folded small and sealed with wax. The wax had no stamp on it. The mayor opened the letter in front of everyone. It was a letter from a roofer of the old roof, written long ago. It said: To whoever mends this roof next. Thank you. Mind the third beam. It is rotten. And keep the lamps lit, for the nights are long.

The third beam was rotten. They had already replaced it.

The roofers have put the letter back in the box, and the box back under the ridge. They added a note of their own, to whoever comes next. I do not know what it said. It was sealed.

The new roof is dry and the inn is warm. If you pass through, ask for a bed. They will find one. They always do.

Bel

Notice: Blue cloth in Leaffall
Teague, dyer of Vell, posts this in the market hall.

I have blue cloth ready for coats and table covers.
Bring clean wool, not wool with burrs in it.
I will pay one emerald for a full basket of good wool.
For less wool, we will agree on less pay.

I will not dye wet wool. It makes a pale, sad blue.
Ask at my door before carrying a basket up the hill.
The door is the one with two blue pots beside it.
One pot is cracked. That is the right door.

I need apples for my supper, not as payment.

Board at Reed Gate Farm
Summer of the 58th year of the archive
The road to Lowmarsh is open from the ford to the old oak.
The bridge is oak, with stone under both feet.
A cart crossed it this morning. The cart held two sacks of wheat,
three baskets of apples, and a sleeping child.
The child woke at the far end and asked where the river went.
No one knew. Jem said, "It goes past the reeds, as rivers do."
The child was satisfied and ate an apple.
The new inn stands where the farm barn stood.
It has six beds, a hearth, and a board for road notices.
The keeper asks travelers to knock before opening the pantry.
The bread is for everyone, but the cheese is not.
A torch now stands beside the ford and another by the bridge.
Keep both lit after dusk. Spiders have been seen near the posts.
The farm gate is repaired. Lift it before you push.
The well bucket is clean. Keep it off the ground.
Lost: one white hen with a bent tail feather.
She was last seen beside the road, following a cart.
She may come back for the grain scattered by the bridge.
If she does, bring her to the hen house and shut the door.
Do not call her Snow. She answers to Nothing.
The relay tower on the hill is finished.
It has a stair inside, a lamp at each turn, and a glass room above.
The keeper sent a message to Lowmarsh at dusk.
The answer came before Jem had finished her bread.
The keeper says this is the first tower on this road.
More will stand between here and Vell, if there are enough hands.
There are enough hands for the next tower. The stone is ready.
The fair will be held in the farm field on the last day of summer.
Bring fish, honey, wool, apples, or a song.
No one may bring a goat into the school tent.
Brindle once ate the school slate. We have not forgotten.
Jem will sit by the well and help any mother who needs her.
Please leave her a place at the table.
She has promised to bring beetroot soup.
The soup is not thin, though Hesk has not tried it.

Notice: high water at the bridge
Pinned to the board at Three Wells. Thaw of 201. By Merle, healer, for the mayor.

The stream at the bridge is rising. It is brown, and it carries fence posts. By evening it may cross the low street.
1. Do not cross the bridge after noon.
2. Move sacks, beds and hens off the ground floor of the low street houses.
3. If water comes into your house, boil what you drink, as long as it takes to sing the school song three times.
4. The old and the very young sleep at the inn tonight.
Merle, healer

Chronicle: the roads and the towers
Kept at the Archive at Vell. Written by the clerks in the front hall, one hand after another. This copy is for the inns on the Long Road.
Year 1. The Archive is founded at Vell, dug into the hill. On the first day of spring the first keepers cut over the door: "We will keep everything we write." The town brings bread and beetroot soup. It rains. Nobody minds.
Year 2. The first four apprentices are drilled at Vell. They carry stone and stack it. The children name them. The tutors pretend not to be pleased.
Year 3. The road from Vell to Three Wells is laid, cobblestone in the towns and packed earth between. A torch every twelve blocks. The first waymarks are set, three blocks high, with a figure and an arrow.
Year 5. Three Wells builds its wall. Cobblestone, cobblestone, deep stone, and the top row stuck out one block against spiders. The school house goes up by the east well, of Copperhold stone and Saltreach glass. The children paint the door. The paint runs. They paint it again.
Year 6. The first relay tower is finished at Vell. The lamp is lit at dusk and seen from Three Wells, which is farther than anyone thought a lamp could be seen. People walk out to look at it. The keeper waves. The keeper waves a great deal.
Year 7. Towers at Three Wells, Kettle Ridge and Ambrook. The Long Road now has an inn a day apart. The Brown Hen opens. The Halfway Inn mends its roof and keeps its hearth going, and has not let it out since.
Year 8. Towers at Saltreach and Copperhold. The tower at Saltreach stands above the harbor, and the keeper there says the lamp and the lighthouse argue at night about which is brighter. The keeper is a funny man. The lamp is winning.
Year 9. The smallest tower, on the hill between Vell and Three Wells, one lamp and one keeper. Northpoint is begun in the north, in the snow, at the end of the road. Mira will keep it. She writes that the stone is cold and the view is worth it.
The archive keeps all of this. The clerks copy it in the bright room with the big window. When the sun comes in, they stop writing for a moment. Then they start again.
We will keep everything we write.
%%
Farm board: summer work
The fair is over. The farm lane is open to carts again. The bridge held, though one rail needs a new peg.
Please return the borrowed baskets to the shed. The baker has two, and Pell has one under his cart. Pell says it is not his. The basket says Three Wells on the handle.
The recipe for beetroot cakes is pinned beside the oven. This is the third copy. The first had no flour, and the second had twice the salt. Both were still served. The cook says the honey covered the taste. The children say it did not.
The farm apprentice carried the flour from the store after it was tested on the same kind of basket drill. It brought the right sack, then waited by the kitchen door. A small mind can answer an order by doing it, but it cannot tell us that the recipe is bad.
The big mind at the hill relay house sends answers to the keeper. It stands in a loaf sized crystal on a stand. Quickeners ring the stand and are kept cool with water. The keeper writes to it through the relay, then gives its answer to the people who ask.
We do not know why a crystal learns from examples. We do know that a mind must be tested before it is trusted with work.
Keep this notice dry. The roof above the board still leaks.

Lost: one brown cat, answers to nothing, and is very sorry to be lost. Last seen on the roof of the honey stall.

Hollis, archive clerk.
