Notice: the hamlet's winter rules
These rules are for the whole hamlet, from the first frost until the thaw. They were agreed at the council in the hall, after a long argument about the cat and a short one about the well. They are read aloud at the start of each week, and they are pinned to the board for those who cannot hear.

One. Keep one lamp lit in every window facing the north path. The watch will walk the path at dusk, and they need to see the houses to know where the houses are.

Two. Bring the animals in before the second bell. Bring in the cats, too. They will not want to come, and they will try to stay out. Bring them in anyway, and shut the coal chute.

Three. Wood is stacked at the back of the house, under the eaves, so the snow does not sit on it. A wet log is a sad log, and a sad log will not light.

Four. Water is drawn from the well each morning, before the ice comes back. Break the ice with the long pole. Do not use your foot. The old men have been told not to use their feet. They have not listened, and they have had the falls to prove it.

Five. If you see a zombie at the gate, go inside. Bar the door. Light a lamp in the window, and call the watch. Do not go out with a torch to see what it is. The torch will be seen, and the zombie will be closer than you think.

Six. If you are cold, come to the hall. The stove is lit from dusk to the second bell, and the bench by the stove is for anyone who needs it.

Seven. The school is open unless the bell does not ring. If the bell does not ring, the school is closed, and the teacher is at home with a cold.

Eight. Do not walk on the lake. The ice is thin at the outflow, and the children have been told so many times that they have started to believe it. Believe it anyway.

Nine. The apprentices of the hamlet work the winter shifts. They carry the wood, clear the paths, walk the wall, and keep the lamps lit. They are not to be disturbed while they walk, and they will not stop to talk. If you need one, call its name, and it will come.

Ten. Remember the people who live alone on the far side of the hill. Take them bread on the first day of each week, and stay for a minute, if they want you to. Often they do not, and that is also a kind of answer.

Ivo, trader, for the hamlet board, for the council, and for the watch. Please read these rules aloud to your children, and make them repeat the sixth, which is the one they will forget.

Notice: lost and found, the Brown Hen
Winter of 192. Hung by the fire at the Brown Hen on the Long Road. Everything on this list was left behind by a traveler and is waiting to go home.

Lost, and now found here:
One left boot, brown, with a hole in the heel. The right boot has not been found. We think a fox has it.
One scarf, red and green, very long. It was wound three times round the dog and has been given back to the dog.
One purse, empty. A very good purse. Its owner may have its contents back when he can describe them.
Two combs. One for hair, one for something else.
A small wooden horse, painted blue, with one ear. A child in a green coat left it on the stairs on the third night of Longnight. Please come back for it. It is very sad.
One bag of seeds. We do not know what seeds. They have been planted in the yard, and in spring we will find out.
One hat. It fits Nim. Nim will keep it unless it is claimed by Longday.
One note, unsigned, which says "I will meet you at the third waymark when the snow is gone." The snow is gone. We hope you went.

Found nothing: no one has lost a pig.

Anyone who owns any of these may take them, and may not complain about the state they are in.

Nim, with the good hat on

Notice: the Ambrook board, all winter
Put up on the mill board from the first frost to the end of Deepsnow, one at a time, by whoever had something to say. The board is the best place to read the news, and the worst place to keep a secret.

Frost, the first. The pond froze at the edges. Do not walk on the middle, and do not stand on the sluice, which is shut and must stay shut. Jory has said it three times and he is not going to say it a fourth.

Frost, the eighth. Bees are home and quiet in their hives. Hob asks you to leave them alone until Thaw, and to bring him any honey you were going to trade. He is keeping it for the fair. He says the fair is the only thing in Ambrook that is worth waiting for.

Frost, the fifteenth. A girl was born at the mill on a cold night, to Tamsin the miller's daughter and her husband Piers. Named Bramble. She has a loud voice and a clear opinion about the wheel. The mill wheel is not pleased. Everyone else is.

Longnight, the first. The orchard has been counted. Four hundred apple trees, the old one with the grey branch, and the three new trees planted by the bridge. The apples in the cellar are the best of the year. Pell took a barrel to Lowmarsh and came back with a letter saying so.

Longnight, the fourth. The lantern walk on the longest night, from the mill to the bridge and back. Carry your own lantern. Do not swap them. The old ones were swapped once, and a whole family found themselves walking home by the wrong road.

Longnight, the ninth. The bridge is mended. The third plank is new and a little yellow beside the others, and the children have named it Honest. Walk over it carefully and tell the children to do the same.

Deepsnow, the second. Ice on the pond again, thicker. The children are skating on the edge, where Jory says it is safe, and the rest of the pond is not theirs to try.

Deepsnow, the twelfth. A cart from the Long Road came in with the post, and the post was not late. It was only wet. The carter asks that you never say it was late again.

Deepsnow, the last. Keep the torches lit on the bridge. Keep the fires banked at the mill. Keep the honey dry and the bees asleep. Keep the lamps lit at the square, so the fair people can find their way home after the musicians have gone. The orchard will wake up in Thaw, and so will the board.

Notice: the cat that will not stay out
Lost and found, and a warning, at the north well. Written by Jem, who has been asked to fetch the cat four times this week.
The cat is ginger and fat and has no proper name. The children call her Mrs Pie. The baker calls her a thief. The mayor calls her a nuisance and then gives her a bit of his bread when he thinks no one is looking.
She has been seen on the school roof. She has been seen on the bakery shelf, asleep on the warm loaves. She has been seen sitting on the notice board, under this very notice, with her tail over the words.
Warning: do not leave the back door of the bakery open. Do not leave a window on the north side open either. The cat knows the way in and does not care about the way out.
Reward for the cat, brought back to the school: one honey cake, from the baker, who says he is very sorry about the loaves.
Reward for the cat, brought back to the baker: one bowl of soup, from the inn, which is not the same as a cake and everybody knows it.
If you find the cat sitting on the notice board, please do not read this aloud to her. She is very tired of it.

Tam has lent the mill his spare oak plank. It is long enough, but it has a knot in the wrong place.
Pip carried the tools and waited by the door. The miller says the wheel will turn again before the next flour count.
Until then, bring grain to the hand mill. Take only what your family needs.
The miller will post a new notice when the water runs through the wheel again.
Ivo

Notice: a melon
By Garrow, posted at the Kettle Inn and the farm gate, twentieth of Apple, 208.
We grew a melon. It is too big. It took three of us and the helper to lift it onto the cart. It does not fit in the cart. It rides beside the cart.
Come and see it on the tenth of Apple at the fair. We will weigh it on the mill tally board. The board may not be long enough.
A word about the fields, for folk new to the ridge:
Keep the torches lit along the field edges. At night there are skeletons and creepers about, and they spoil a crop.
Keep the ditch clear. The water down the middle of the field feeds the roots and drowns the weeds.
Keep the north hedge trimmed. It breaks the wind and the wheat stands straighter.
The helper walks the rows at dusk and does the counting. Say "farm" and it goes. It is slow but it does not stop.
Lost and found: a brown goat, Nettle, found at last in the birch wood, eating apples and looking pleased with herself. She is home. Thank you all.
Found also: one blue mitten, one boot, and a small copper bell on a red cord. Ask at the gate.

Notice: the relay on the hill
Posted at the notice board in Three Wells and at the inn of twelve beds. Summer, the 196th year of the archive.
The little relay on the hill between Vell and Three Wells is open again. One lamp, one keeper, one small door. It was shut for six years and the hill grew over.
What it does:
It carries a message from Three Wells to Vell in a morning.
It carries a message to Lowmarsh by evening, if the weather is kind.
It does not carry anything heavy. A message is a few words, not a cart.
What it costs:
One emerald a message for folk of the town. Nothing from the school, the mayor, or anyone sending word of a sick person.
Rules of the relay, as Nessa wrote them for the drill room, and as we keep them here:
Keep the message short. Short messages arrive. Long ones arrive in pieces and lose their way.
Say who you are and where you are.
If the lamp is dark, wait. The keeper is asleep or the wind is up.
Do not climb the tower to look. The keeper will look for you.
The keeper this season is a young mind in a crystal on a stand, a small one, with a keeper to mind it. It reads the numbers on the letters and passes them on. It is wrong now and then and the keeper checks it. It is getting better. Do not tease it.
- Nessa, for the keeper

Rules for the spring fair, Kettle Ridge
Read out at the bell by the mayor's man of the ridge, and pinned on the board by the well. Please read them before the fair, not during it.

1. Bring your own cup. The Kettle Inn has run out of cups twice, and both times the cups came back with wool in them.
2. Do not race the windmill. The windmill always wins, and it has been practising longer.
3. Pigs are kept behind the pen. Big Beet is not to be trusted with the pies, whatever he says about the pies.
4. Horses are tied at the north rail. The rail is strong. Tie your horse to it, and not to your neighbor's cart.
5. Stalls open at the first bell and close at the second. A stall that opens early will be asked why.
6. Apprentices may carry for you. Only tested minds are sent to the stalls. Ask the tutor for the test sheet, and read the mark.
7. Lost things go to the board. Lost children go to the mill house, and the mill house is not a stall.
8. Songs under the oak are free to hear. Pay the singer if you like the song. The singer will not ask. The singer will look.
9. The bell is rung at noon for the pies and at dusk for going home. Do not ring it yourself. It is not a joke.

Signed for the ridge by the mayor's man, who has been asked to sign it and has done so with his left hand, which is shaking from the rain.

Notice: Summer Work and Fair, Melon 207
For the farm road outside Three Wells and the square.
The summer fair will open on the fifteenth of Melon. Stalls may be set up after the morning bell. Keep the three wells clear, and leave a path wide enough for two carts to pass.
Farm carts should use the cobblestone edge beside the wheat field. The middle of the road is dry now, but the ditch is full. Do not turn a cart near the oak gate. The soil there gives way under a heavy wheel.
The farmer has asked for help bringing water to the lower beds. The farm well is working again, but its bucket rope is short. Use the east well for the first two trips. The farmer will lend a longer rope after the noon meal. Please return it to the shed. Last year it went home with a hay cart and came back tied to a pig.
The carrots are ready. The wheat will need cutting soon. Anyone taking a fair day from farm work should finish the lower row first. This rule applies to the mayor too, though the mayor has been told it applies to him before.
At the fair there will be apples from Ambrook, honey, bread, wool, and beetroot pies. The school children will sing beside the east well. Their new song has four verses. The fourth verse is for clapping, and the children have asked us not to clap before it.
A small apprentice from the town store will carry baskets from the square to the farm shed. It has been tested with empty baskets. Do not give it eggs until the store keeper has tested it with eggs.
Lost and found: a blue cup, one left shoe, and a wooden spoon with a round handle. Leave found things at the notice board. The owner of the spoon should ask at the bakery.
No fires beside the hay. No pigs in the school house. No climbing the fair bell. The bell is for calling people, not for finding out how high a child can climb.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Notice: why the ridge is asked
Put up by the inn door, in the spring of the 91st year, in the tutor's own words, which he asked to have written plain.

A drilled mind is shown many examples of the right answer. It learns the pattern of the answers, the way a child learns the pattern of the seasons. Give it a new question of the same kind, and it gives the answer that fits the pattern. Most of the time the answer is right.

Orla's question is about what happens when the pattern is very large. A mind can learn so many patterns that its answers look like a person's. Then the question is whether the pattern is all there is, or whether there is something in the mind that is doing the choosing.

We do not know. The tutors can count the patterns. They cannot count the choosing, because nobody has found where it is kept. Some say it is in the stream, which runs through every layer. Some say it is in the number of layers. Some say there is no choosing, only very good patterns, and the ridge is welcome to think so.

What the ridge can do is simple. Read the letters. Guess which is which. Write down the guesses, and the reasons. The tutors will keep them all, and the next visiting tutor will ask the same question, and the answer will still not be finished.

Please do not hurry the answer. A question this old has earned its time.
%%
Notice: harvest rules
From the board at the foot of the tower at Ambrook, in autumn. Written in a hurry by Tarn, the tower keeper, with a note from Corin the shipwright, who is visiting.
The lamp is broken. It broke on the second night of Apple. A pane cracked and the copper ring slipped. The lamp still lights but it throws a crooked beam, and the next tower cannot read it. Until it is mended we send by day, with the flags.
Corin looked at it and says the ring is not hard to fix. He says a ring is just a plank bent in a circle, and he bends planks for a living. He will fix it when the rain stops. He is not afraid of the ladder. He is afraid of the pond. He says he has been in worse water.
HARVEST RULES for the next twenty days:
1. Pickers start at first light and stop at noon. The afternoon is for sorting.
2. Fallen apples go in the pig basket, not in the cider basket. The cider basket is for apples from the tree.
3. Do not climb the oldest tree. It is old. It has a grey branch. It does not like climbing.
4. Bring your own cup. The mill pond is for the bees and the wheel.
5. If you break a ladder, say so. Do not hide it in the hedge. Last year three ladders were hidden in the hedge, and the hedge was not pleased.
6. The apprentices carry the full baskets to the cart. Do not overload them. A basket too full is a basket spilled.
7. Songs are encouraged. The bees like a steady tune. A song with many verses keeps the pickers going.
8. The harvest supper is at the mill, on the last night. Bring something. Jory will bring the cider. It will be sour this year, but it will be ours.
WARNING: the road to Three Wells is muddy by the old bridge. The cart from Pell got stuck there on the first day, and it stayed stuck for a night. Pell says he was in no hurry. He says that every time.
A last word on the harvest. It is not a great one. The trees gave less this year, and some of us will have thin pockets. But the barns are not empty, and the mill is turning, and there are people at the table. That is enough to go on with.
Tarn, tower keeper, Ambrook
%%
Notice for the spring fair
The farm outside Ambrook will send wool, apples, honey, and two baskets of carrots to the fair.
The cart leaves after breakfast on the fourth of Swarm. Pell will take the road past the mill pond. Put goods under the canvas and write your name on each basket.
Linnet will card wool at the stall. She will show children how to pull a fleece clean without pulling it apart. Children may take home a small roll of wool. They may not take the carding combs.
The red apples are for sale. The spotted apples are for eating at the stall. Please do not sort them by taking bites.
Mara and Sella may come if the weather is fair. Sella likes the cart bell. She does not like the rooster, though the rooster has done nothing to her.
We need two people to carry the honey jars. Carry them upright. Last year, one jar arrived with honey on the lid, the cart, and Pell's sleeve.
The mill wheel is still in Jory's shed. He has fitted a new oak arm and says it will be ready when the pin holds. We will not send flour until the wheel has turned under water.
If the fair runs late, Pell will leave a message at the orchard gate. No one should wait alone on the road after dark. Keep the torches lit.
Linnet

Lost and found, Copperhold hamlet
Board by the gate. Spring, 193.
Lost: one iron pick, the handle wrapped in red wool. Left somewhere between the second level and the lift. Please.
Found: a wooden horse with one wheel. It is on the shelf in the tool store. It is waiting.
Found: many lanterns. Too many. We think someone is collecting them. Stop it.
Lost: my good boots. Size small. I know they are in the tool store. I have looked three times.
Found: a letter with no name on it. It says only "I am well. The stone is cold. I will write again." If it is yours, come and get it.
Found: the goat again. It is not lost. It lives here now. We have given up.
The board is cleared at the end of each month. Take your notices down or we will.
Hesk

Notice: poor crop and fair work
Spring of 207. The apple blossom was thin this year, and the lower beds have little in them. Bees worked the hill flowers, but there were fewer than last spring. Odo has set aside two shelves of candles for homes that cannot pay. Take one and leave an honest mark on his board.

The fair will still be held by the house at the edge of Ambrook. The tutors have agreed to bring a small mind and a set of safe tasks. They will show how an order is taught, and why a test must come before work.

A mind does not learn a sentence from hearing it once. It is shown examples, tries the work, and is corrected when it gets the answer wrong. Each correction changes its weights a little. Weights are the marks in the crystal that guide its answer. The crystal learns from the examples, though no one here can say why it learns at all.

The small mind in an apprentice cannot write a full reply. It can answer only with one of eight meanings. It cannot say it has forgotten or that it does not know. A big mind at a relay house can write back by letter, but it is not the same mind as the one riding inside the apprentice.

People still argue about the first whole order. Clank's name is on the Copperhold account. Pip's is on the Kettle Ridge account. Peg's is on the fair account. Nessa says she saw such work in the Vell drill room when she was young. The old accounts do not agree.

Please bring food to share. The poor crop is not anyone's fault. Odo will trade candles for potatoes, carrots, or a sack of wheat. He will not trade for a story about who was first.
%%

If the basket owner wants the missing apple, ask the children. They know where it went.

Minutes from the spring board
Written by Odo, candle maker, with notes from Nessa and Dace. Spring of 207.

We met in the front room of Odo's house at the edge of Ambrook. Rain tapped the glass. The old oak door stuck as it always does. We put a small wedge under it and began.

The harvest will be short. The oldest apple tree has blossoms on only one side. The grey branch is still bare. The bees have come to the hill hives, but not in their usual number. The lower beds gave fewer carrots and potatoes. We will keep the fair, and no family will be charged for a bowl of soup.

Nessa set out a crystal on the tutor's bench. Each card beside it had a short drill. She said the cards are examples, not orders to be obeyed by themselves. A mind learns from many examples. It tries an answer, and when the answer is wrong, its weights change a little. The tutor shows the right result. After many tries, the mind may do better on a new example.

A child asked if the crystal knows that it is learning. Nessa said no one has written that down. We know what we show it and what it does. We do not know why the crystal can learn.

She spoke about the new work with whole sentences. A person can give an apprentice an order in ordinary words, and a trained mind may find the work meant by the sentence. This is not a new meaning. The small mind still answers by doing, or with one of the eight meanings. It cannot say that it does not know. It cannot write a letter. Only the big minds in relay houses write back through the relays.

A man from Copperhold said Clank understood a whole order first. Tam's note from Kettle Ridge says Pip did. A peddler from the road says Peg did at the fair. Nessa remembers seeing the same kind of work in the drill room at Vell when she was young. Each account has a witness. None gives a date we can trust.

Dace asked us to stop calling the matter a race. The board will keep all four accounts. We will test the minds on the same unseen drill when they come. That is the fair way to compare them.

At sunset the lamp by Odo's house blinked in a pattern no one knew. It was not the steady light used for a road warning. We checked the lamp room, the copper ring, and the redstone line to the desk. No break was found. We sent a note to the relay keeper above the mill pond. There has been no reply.

Odo says the lamp may have a loose line. Nessa says we should not call a blink a message until we know who sent it. The lamp is working again. The fair will open at the mill wheel, as planned.

Bring food if you have it. Bring a witness if you have one. Leave room for the apprentice to walk. Keep the torches lit on the road home.
%%

Notice to the children: do not climb the gate chain. You have been told this many times. The chain is not a ladder, and the gate men are very tired of finding you on it.

Joke for the children: why did the lamp refuse to go to school? Because it was always blinking about something.
Wynn, grandfather, with the chalk

Notice: the lamp that blinked wrong
Posted on the board at the east well, Three Wells, by Bel, who is nine. The mayor asked me to put it in plain words. This is for the Archive too.

Last night the relay lamp on the hill blinked a message nobody could read.

It began at the second hour after dark. The keeper, Sedge, was at her desk. The lamp from the next tower was sending in the usual way, long and short and long. Then it changed. The blinks came faster. Then slower. Then in a pattern of fives that Sedge had never seen.

She wrote it down. Here it is, so everyone can see.

Long, short, short, long, long. Long, short, short, long, long. Long, short, short, long, long.

Then it stopped.

Sedge sent a note to Vell: lamp at the north sent something odd. Please read. Vell sent back: we saw nothing. Please repeat. Sedge sent: it is gone. Vell sent: then it was a glass flaw, or fog. Sedge sent: there was no fog.

This morning, a man who came down the north road said that the tower at Kettle Ridge had seen the same blinks, at the same hour. A woman who came up the Long Road from Saltreach said she had seen the lamp at the harbor do it too. Nobody in any tower can read it.

What people say, so far:

The baker says it is a fault in the glass.
Hob says it is a joke by the keepers.
The fox trapper says it is the northern lights, getting into the lamps.
Sedge says nothing. She is writing it in her log.

If you saw it, please come to the school and tell me which way the lamp faced. I am keeping a list. The mayor has told me I should not make anyone afraid. I have not. I have only made a list.

Bel

Notice: Song contest at Vell
On the ninth of Leaffall, the school yard will hold a song contest.
All may enter. A song may be short, and a voice may be rough.
The judges will listen from the bench by the drill yard.
Please do not sing while the first singer is singing.

There will be a prize of one jar of honey for the song that folk remember.
There will be a small cloth prize for the song with the clearest words.
Teague will give the cloth, if the dye pots are kind that week.

No song may last longer than the bell rope can be held by one child.
The bell rope is sturdy. The child is not a measure of time.
Bring a stool if you need one. Bring no drum unless you can carry it home.
The school will provide warm beetroot soup after the songs.

Lost pear basket
Found beside the flour stall: a shallow oak basket with six pears and a blue wool cord. The pears are sound. One has a small mark like a thumb near its stem.

Mave has put the basket under her table. Ask her before the market bell is rung. If it is yours, tell how many pears were in it when you lost it. The basket is not a reward for guessing.

Una of Ambrook came through the market with a little crystal in a cloth wrap. She set two baskets before it, one with apples and one with pears. She showed it each fruit and said which basket should take it. At first the crystal sent some to the wrong place. Una changed its weights a little after each mistake. It did better when she showed it again.

Notice: spiders in the barn at Kettle
Posted by Tam at the Kettle Inn, autumn of 9. Copied for the Long Road inns.
We have spiders in the barn. I know. You know. The barn knows.
They are not the big ones. They are the ordinary ones, the sort that keep the flies down and the corners busy. They have been there since Sowing. They will be there at Deepsnow. They are part of the barn now.
The rule is this: do not sleep in the barn. The barn is for hay, for tools, for the cart, and for the cat, who does not mind the spiders and does not help with them either. The cat is a disappointment. The cat knows this.
If you must go in the barn after dark, take a torch. Keep it high. Spiders keep to the dark corners and the high beams, and a torch tells them you are coming. They are not brave. They are just everywhere.
Do not burn the barn to get the spiders. We tried to explain this to a traveler last month and he did not listen, and now we have a barn with a burned door and the same spiders, and the traveler is not welcome at the Kettle Inn until he pays for the door.
The hens are in the hen house. The hens are safe. The foxes are the other notice. See the board.
If you find a spider in your boot, shake the boot outside. Do not shake it inside. We have learned this the hard way and so will you.
Pip the apprentice sweeps the barn each morning. Pip does not mind the spiders. Pip minds nothing. Pip is new.
The hay is good this year. Come and buy some. Bring a torch.
%%
Notice: the school's autumn drill
From the school house by the east well, Three Wells. Posted the 2nd of Leaffall, 214th year.
The autumn contest is at the school on the 10th of Leaffall, at midday.
Each mind is tested on a drill it was never drilled on. That is the rule, and we keep it.
The test box of pages is kept by the teacher. No mind is ever drilled on those pages.
Bring your crystal in its dry box. Bring your card. Bring your own drill board.
Apprentices may come. Apprentices stand in their row and wait.
The meanings are the eight: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul, home. Nothing else.
There are three prizes. First is a copper cup. Second is a bag of honey cakes. Third is a red ribbon.
The mayor will judge with the teacher. The teacher's word is last.
A word on looking inside, for the older children.
This autumn the tutors at Vell sent us a note. They can look inside a crystal with a thin light and a glass.
They found some of it and not the rest. The part that chooses the next letter is very busy.
The part that keeps the numbers even is there, and we know its work.
And a quiet part, and nobody knows what it does. Some say it holds old drills. That is only a rumor.
We do not know why a crystal learns. We do not know why an old one goes cloudy.
The teacher says: write down what you found, and write down what you did not. That is how a mind is tested, and how a town is run.
Our Bel will read her letter about the melon at the contest. It is a good letter. It has a pig in it.
Keep the torches lit. It is dark early now.
Signed, the teacher of Three Wells, for the school and Dace the mayor.
%%
Notice: the big oak and the bell rope
Notice pinned to the oak in the square at Three Wells, under the songs board, where the children gather to climb and the mayor gathers to complain. Written by Jem, with the help of Bel, who says she checked the spelling.
The oak is very old. It has been here since before the wells. The first people who came to this square tied a rope to its lowest branch, to hold the bell, and the rope is still there, though the bell has moved to a post by the school.
The rope is now a swing. It is the best swing in the town. It has been mended by the carpenter three times and by the children, many more.
Rules for the swing:
One child at a time. Two children if one is very small.
Hold the rope with both hands. Never with the teeth.
Do not swing higher than the lowest branch. The lowest branch is also the highest one that the children are allowed to climb, and the mayor has said so twice.
Do not sing the bell song while swinging. It makes the goats come to the fence, and the goats eat the rope.
When you are finished, tie the rope back to the branch in a knot that the next child can undo. Bel says a knot is only as good as the person who ties it. Jem says the knot is the only thing that is good about the swing.
The carpenter says the oak is sound. He says he has been saying this for thirty years, and that he will go on saying it, until the swing breaks, and then he will say he was right.
%%
Notice: a thin harvest
Posted at the board in the square, Three Wells, Longnight, year 171.
Friends, the harvest was poor. Everyone knows it. The wheat was short, the beetroot small, and the potatoes came up the size of eggs. The rain came late and left early.
This is what the town will do.
One. The big barn by the south road is open to all. Whatever you can spare, bring there. Write what you bring on the tally board by the door.
Two. Nobody sells grain out of town this winter. Not for emeralds, not for a good price, not for a fine hat.
Three. The bakery will make a plain loaf every day. Plain is fine. Plain is warm.
Four. Every house keeps a pot on the fire where a neighbor may sit and have a bowl. We have done this before, and we are still here.
It is a hard winter to begin with, but it is a short winter if we share the wood. Next year the rain will come at the right time. I have written to the sky about it.
Dace

Notice: winter rules at the Halfway Inn
Put up by Fen, keeper, for the cold months, this year 199
The inn is warm. The road is not. Read this before you go out.
Beds: twelve. When they are gone they are gone. The floor by the fire is free, and so is the hay in the barn, but the goat has first choice of the hay.
Boots off at the door. This is not a rule for you, it is a rule for the floor.
Torches: take one from the box when you leave after dark. Bring it back or bring another. The box is not a well.
The bridge past the second waymark is stone again and holds well. The stream under it is high. Do not test it.
The relay keepers' beds are kept. If a keeper comes in at night with snow on their coat, let them sleep. They have been carrying everyone's letters all day.
Lost and found: one red scarf, one tin whistle, a small boot, and a sack of turnips (still). Ask at the board.
No pigs in the beds. Someone tried. We know who. We have forgiven him.
Soup at dusk. Bread at dawn. Songs after, if the mood is right and the wind is not.
Keep the torches lit. It is creeper season, even in the snow.
Fen, keeper

Notice: bees swarming
At the Brown Hen. A swarm of bees landed on the inn sign at noon and has not left. Do not go near the sign. Do not throw anything. Do not shout. Marda has sent a boy to Ama at Ambrook.

While we wait, two answers about what to do were pinned up, and guests are asked to decide which to trust.

The first was written by Ama's niece, who keeps bees. It says: stand away, keep quiet, and wait for a keeper with a box and a cloth.
The second was written by the big mind at Vell, by letter. It says the same, in more words. It adds that a swarm is not angry, and will leave when it finds a house.

Guests have voted. 14 trust Ama's niece. 9 trust the big mind. 3 trust the sign, which is the one with the bees on it.

Hobb says the mind's letter is the first he has read that sounds like a person who has met a bee. Nessa, who was at the next table, said it has never met a bee. It has read about thousands. Nobody at the table could say what the difference was.

Corin, writing this on the inn table, autumn.

Notice from the lamp keeper
The north market lamp blinked a strange message last night. It gave one short blink, then three, then one. No keeper sent those marks. I checked the copper line and the glass room. Both were in place.

Lantern says a fox may have brushed the lower switch. The fox denies nothing. We have put a board around the switch until it is checked. Do not climb the tower to look for the cause.

The lamp is a signal, not a toy. A message must be sent by the keeper and written in the relay book. If you see a lamp blink without a keeper's mark, tell Lantern at the relay house. Do not repeat the blinks at another tower. A false message can send people along the wrong road.

At tomorrow's fair, Una's sorting crystal will be tested beside Mave's stall. It will see fruit it has not been shown during its lessons. The tutors will keep the answer list in a closed book. After each choice, they will say whether it was right. They will not change the weights during the test.

When the test is over, Una may move each weight a little toward the right choice. That is the lesson, not a trick. A wrong answer gives the tutor a chance to show the crystal what it missed.

Keep the lamp path clear. Mave has flour sacks there, and they are not part of the test.
%%
Notice: a talk on fewer marks
Pinned to the board at Three Wells, Sowing of 201. By Merle, healer, from a note by the tutor Nessa.

A tutor from Vell will speak in the back room of the inn at the evening bell on the first dry night. The talk is called "Why Jug is small". Everyone may come. Bring a stool.

For those who cannot come, here is the short of it.

A mind is made of a great many numbers. Each number is written down in marks, and each number has room for as many marks as the crystal will hold. A big mind with long numbers needs a big crystal.

The tutors found that every number can be written with fewer marks. The numbers become a little less exact. The same mind then fits in less room. And the mind is only a little worse.

How much worse? Write each number with half its marks, and the mind is hardly worse at all. Write it with a quarter, and it is a little worse. Write it with a tenth, and it begins to stumble.

That is the trade. A smaller crystal costs a little skill. For a mind that rides in an apprentice's chest, the trade is a good one. For the biggest minds, in the relay houses, nobody has tried it.

After the talk there will be soup. The inn asks that no one ask Jug to stir it.
%%

Hobb has already guessed, and will not say. Corin.

Notice: Winter card fair

Vell, Longnight, 178.

The tutors will show three working minds in the yard. One will keep a copper melt steady. One will guide a small boat across the pond. One will hold a frame level while it is raised.

These minds were not taught a list of answers. They try an action, then keep more of what went well. If the melt grows too hot, the furnace mind changes its hand on the fire. If it cools too far, it changes again. It learns by the marks on the heat board.

The boat mind has a simple course to hold. The frame mind has a line to keep. Neither knows every trouble before it comes.

Bring warm mittens. The yard is cold. Do not touch the copper pots.

Odo has carded wool for the tutor's bench. He asks that the fair end before dark. The torches are not a second sun.

Notice: mending the mill roof
Pinned at the mill gate, Kettle Ridge. Written by Ned the roofer, for the apprentices who carry for him.
The mill roof had a hole in the north corner the size of a cart wheel. Sefa found it when the flour came in damp. We have mended it. The reed is new and so are the rafters on the north side.
I will write down what I did, in case the next roofer needs it. You may not believe me. I did not believe it either.
Strip the old reed from the rafter to the eave. Do not pull it all at once. Take a bundle, then the next. Keep the old reed in a pile at the back. The old reed is good for the hens' nests, and the hens have been very patient about the fox.
Lay the new reed from the eave up. Each row must cover the joint of the row below. A roof is a set of overlaps. If you lay it flat, the rain comes in at the join.
Tie each row with a double knot. Test the knot with your hand. If it moves, tie it again.
Nail the ridge last. Put a lamp by the ridge, not on it. A lamp on a roof is a fire waiting to happen, and Sefa will say so.
Then, from the top of the roof, look down at the ridge. You will see the whole of Kettle Ridge in one look. The mill, the barns, the windmill with its one new sail. The apprentices going home along the road in a line. I think that is what a roof is for. It is what you can see from it, after you are done.
Ned, roofer, Kettle Ridge.
%%
Notice: the fox at the hens
Notice on the barn at the Brin farm, Kettle Ridge. Summer. Put up by Ned, with the hen house roof finished above it.
A fox has been at the hens. Four are gone. Three are very sorry about it, and one is pretending to be a rock.
We have found the den. It is under the old wall on the north side, by the blackberry bush. We have put a board across the gap, but the fox has dug round the end of it, and we are not sure it is the fox that dug. Biscuit says it was the sheep.
Please keep the hen door shut at dusk. Please keep the lamps lit round the coop. The fox does not like the light, and it does not like the smell of the lamp oil, which is one of the few things Brin is sure about.
If you see the fox, do not chase it. Shout. Then go and find the nearest apprentice, and have it stand in the gap and look large. Pip has been doing this since spring. The fox has not yet come past Pip, and nobody has asked the fox why.
Ned says the best way to keep a fox out is to make the coop the only dry place in the field. Brin says the best way is a good dog. Biscuit says nothing and eats the bread.
%%
Notice: the Long Road, autumn of 9
Posted at the inns and at the Three Wells board. Kept by Dace, copied by Nessa.
The road is open from Three Wells to Vell. Six new waymarks stand between them, each with its figure and its arrow. Little stones are already on top. Leave one if you pass. That is how a road learns it is used.
The bridge over the mill stream at Ambrook is mended. Oak, not stone, but it holds a cart. Jory says it will hold until we can do better. Jory says many things. The bridge holds.
The relay tower between Vell and Three Wells is finished. It is the smallest we have: one lamp, one keeper, one stair. The keeper's name is Osric. He is glad of company and will trade tea for news. Bring news.
The tower at Vell is up to four lamps now. Messages go from Three Wells to Vell in a morning if the air is clear. In rain, a day. In snow, ask again in spring.
New inns: the Brown Hen, one day south of Three Wells. Twelve beds, good bread. The keeper is Alda. Tell her the road sent you.
Northpoint is still the last tower and the longest climb. Mira keeps it. Winter there is already winter. If you go, take wool.
Wheat is in. Beetroot is in. The apples are coming. Ambrook wants pickers, see their notice.
Keep the torches lit on the open road. If you pass a post with no light, light it. Someone will do the same for you.
There were foxes at the hens at Kettle Ridge again. Tam lost four. He has mended the fence twice and the fox has mended its manners never. Foxes are clever. Fences must be cleverer.

Minutes: the inn keepers' meeting
Kept by Fallon, who has the steadiest hand at the table. Twelve inns were asked. Nine came.
First: the road. Each keeper said the state of their stretch. All agreed the cobblestone edge needs mending where carts turn. Fallon will cut the waymarks where they lean. Pell will carry word to the towns.
Second: the relays. A keeper from the north stretch read out what the tower keeper sent her. One tower went quiet for a day and came back. No one knows why. A lamp blinked long, short, long, and no keeper can read it. It was put down as a rumor and written anyway. We do not explain it. We write it.
Third: the late boats. A boat from Saltreach came in three days after the rest. The crew said the wind went strange. The keeper of the harbor lamp kept it lit until she came in. The meeting thanked her by name, and her name is in the book.
Fourth: minds at work. A crew of apprentices has been mending the road by the old oak bridge. They follow a pattern, the keeper said, and they follow it well. One of them was told to fetch a pail and fetched a pail. Another was told to fetch the moon, and waited. We agreed this was the right answer.
Fifth: a tutor's note was read about looking inside a crystal. She found the part that brings a mind back to an even size. She did not find the rest. It lies tangled. We agreed that this is like most things.
The meeting ended. The hearth was still lit. No one had let it out.
Fallon
%%
Notice: the crew at the new well
The crew of apprentices digging the new well in the square has written its own order cards this week. They planned the job on the cards, one step to a card, and pinned the cards on the rack by the tool shed. Anyone may read them. Please do not move them.

The first card reads:
> lay the stones round the hole
= build
> carry the earth to the cart
= haul
> wait at the top until the stone is down
= stay
> put the rope back on the rack
= store

The crew wrote the cards with the tutor's orders in mind, and then wrote what each order means to them, in their own plain words. The tutor says this is the way to catch a mistake before it is made. We have caught two so far.

When the crew is stuck, it asks the big mind at the relay house, through the lamp. Answers come back in an hour or two, and the crew waits by the rope. The crew has been stuck twice this week. Both times the answer was: check the rope. The rope was fine. The rope is always fine. Still, the crew checks it, and that is why the well is so deep.

The well will be finished at Melon. Please keep the square tidy until then, and mind the edge.

Willa, potter, for the board.

Notice: the pie that failed
Posted in the Brown Hen kitchen, under the card, for the new cook and for anyone else who wants to try the recipe for the pie. The old card is still pinned above it, in its own grey pen. Jory has written the notes under it, in red. Read both before you bake.

The old card says:
Take the flour and the butter. Rub them together. Add the water until the dough holds. Roll it thin. Fill with beetroot, leeks, and a little pork. Bake until done.

Jory's notes, in red:
Until done is not a time. Done for the card-writer's oven is not done for this one. Look at the crust. It should be the colour of a good loaf, and the edges should be firm when you press them. If you can smell the pork through the crust, it is past done.

Rub the butter in until it is like sand, not like paste. Then stop. The more you rub, the tougher the crust will be. Ask yourself if the butter is cold. Cold butter helps. Warm butter ruins everything.

Add the water a spoon at a time. The dough should hold together when you press it, but it should not stick to the board. A dry dough cracks. A wet dough sags. A dough that sags has been given too much water by someone who was in a hurry.

Roll it thin, but not so thin that you can see the board through it. Lay it in the tin and press the edges. A fork is good for this. Your thumb is better, if you have a clean one.

The filling: beetroot cut small, leeks cut thin, pork cut into pieces no bigger than your thumbnail. Salt them lightly. Do not put in the whole store. The card said a little pork. It did not say the whole pig.

Bake it in the middle of the oven, not the bottom. The bottom burns first. Turn the tin halfway if the oven is hot on one side. Check at the half hour. Check again at the three-quarter hour.

Let it cool before you cut it. A hot pie falls apart. A cool pie holds, and the carters can eat it without wearing it.

If you have followed all this and the pie is still grey, then say so to the carters, and give them soup.
%%
Notice: the market, summer rules
Posted at the market at Three Wells. Copied out clean by Bel. The mayor's words, and one of mine.

1. The market opens at the first bell, and closes at the last. Do not ring the bell for fun.
2. Stalls must be set up on the first morning. Do not move them in the night.
3. A fair price is posted on a board. If a stall has no board, ask. If it still has none, ask the mayor.
4. Animals are allowed at the market. Animals must be held. A goat is an animal. A goat that eats a hat is still held.
5. Fires are not allowed. Torches are. Do not drop one.
6. The wells are for water. Do not wash in them. Do not throw coins in unless you wish.
7. Carts must go slowly in the square, at a child's walk. Bel says: a nine-year-old child, not a baby, and not Hob.
8. If you hear a hum at night, tell the keeper. The mayor says it is probably the wind. Some say it is the stone. We do not know.
9. If you have a cough that is gray, go to the healer. She will come from Lowmarsh on the fair days.
10. Sell good things, and tell the truth. That is all the rule there is.

The mayor says rule 8 should not be on the board. I said it is a true rule. The mayor said: leave it.

Notice: the old map
Quill, schoolteacher, spring. Posted on the board by the door of the house at the edge of Northpoint, and at the tower, and on the gate of the Wick farm.
FOUND: an old map, rolled in a tube, in the attic of the schoolhouse. It is drawn on yellow cloth in brown ink. It shows the north, the coast, and the Long Road as it was in the first years. The towns are in the wrong places. There is a village on it called Elm, that I have never heard of. If anyone knows Elm, please tell me.
The map has a corner torn off. The torn part would have shown the sea. It may be in someone's drawer. If you find a piece of yellow cloth with a wave on it, please bring it to the school.
The map is for looking at, not for taking. Come on any day after lessons, and sit with it. The children have already found three mistakes. I have found six.
Also: the cow in the lane is sick. It is Marra's brown one. The healer says it must be kept warm and given warm mash. If you have bran, send some. If you have a blanket, send that. Marra is very worried. The cow, it must be said, is not.
Quill, schoolteacher

Notice: horse lent and returned
To whoever borrowed the grey mare from my field by the east well on the 12th of Apple:
She came back on the 15th. She was tired and her coat was muddy.
She had been fed. Thank you for that.
Next time, leave a note. I did not know where she was for three days.
My son cried. The mare is old and does not like to be hurried.
She is well now. She is eating.
If you need a horse again, ask. I lend her for oats, not for nothing.
Signed, Marta, keeper of the east field.
Below, in a child's hand: I am sorry. I took her to see the lights. I did not mean to keep her. Bel.

Notice: the roof on the hen house
Notice pinned to the gate of the Brin farm, Kettle Ridge. Put up by Ned, roofer, who did the work.
The hen house roof is mended. It was torn off in the big wind of summer and carried into the next field. Brin brought it back on his back. It is now on properly, with new reed and four new nails.
Please do not stand on the roof. It was not built to hold a person. It was built to hold the rain, and it does that well.
The hens are back in the house. They are not happy about the new door, which is wider. They have been told that is their own fault for going out.
The fox that visits at dusk has been told to stop. It has not stopped. Do not leave the gate open, and do not leave a hen out after the lamps go up.
Ned asks that everyone who helped carry the reed bundles come by the gate for a cup of cider. He has been up the ladder for three days and he is very tired.
Brin says the roof is crooked. Ned says it is only the hen house. The hens do not mind.

Notice: lost dog at the Halfway Inn
A small brown dog is missing from the Halfway Inn. She answers to Nip. She has one white ear and a crooked tail and no collar, because she eats collars.
She was last seen by the hearth on the night of the nineteenth of Longnight, when a traveler left the door open. She is not fierce. She is only fast, and fond of bread.
If you find her, bring her to the inn and ask for Fen. There will be a reward of bread and thanks, and a bed by the fire for as long as you need one.
The riddle board is still by the hearth. Nip has not solved it either.
Fen, keeper, Halfway Inn, Longnight 173

The board at Three Wells, flood week
Copied from the notice board in the square at Three Wells, the week after the high water, Thaw of 201. The writing is in many hands. Most of the hands were muddy.

Notice from Dace, mayor: the spring fair will be held when the square is dry. Stalls may be set on the north side now, where it is.
Wanted: a cart that does not leak. A cart that does. Any cart. The bakery.
Found: a boat, in the square. The owner may claim it at the north well. The boat says nothing and has not been asked.
Lost: the bell from the inn door. It went down the stream in the night. If found, please keep it out of the water. The innkeeper.
For sale: a door with a brass knocker. Used once, by a flood. 2 emeralds. Ask Merle.
Notice from Merle, healer: boil all water. If in doubt, boil. If not in doubt, boil.
Wanted: a good word for what we have done to the low street. Hollis, baker.
Found: one boot, left foot. Forty others like it. We are beginning to think someone has done this on purpose.
Notice from the tutors at Vell: Jug, the new apprentice, is working well. Please do not give it orders longer than a few words. It is a small mind in a small crystal, and it reads only so far. If you have a long thing to say, say it in two.
For sale: eggs. The hens were up a tree for three days and are cross, but they lay.
Wanted: the person who lent Jug a spade. Jug has returned it. Jug has returned it four times. Please come and take it back.
Thank you, from Kettle Ridge: flour, ten sacks, on the cart behind the mill. Tam says he is sorry it is all one color.
Thank you, from Three Wells: to Ambrook for the apples, to Copperhold for the stone, to Saltreach for the fish, to Kettle Ridge for the flour, to Vell for Jug, and to the north well for staying dry.
Lost: our good humor, the day the water came. Found: the next morning, in the bread. Hollis.
Notice from Dace, mayor: the bridge will be open in ten days. The mayor will walk over it first, with his hat on, to prove it.
A note, in a child's hand, pinned under the rest: Jug carried me across the low street today on its back. It did not say anything. It went very slowly. I think it was being careful. Rye.

Farm board, spring 207
Spring work begins when the ground is soft enough to take a seed and firm enough to hold a boot.
Please use the west field for wheat. The east field is for carrots and potatoes. Beetroot goes in the long bed by the hedge. The ditch must stay clear, so take weeds to the compost heap and not into the water.
The hives are busy near the apple trees. Walk slowly there. The bees have their own work and do not need help from a waving hat.
The mill wheel at the lower pond is turning again. Jory fitted a new oak arm and a copper pin. The first turn shook dust from the rafters. The second turn shook a mouse from the grain bin. The mouse was not hurt. It ran into the flour room and has not been seen since.
The first sack of meal is for the farm kitchen. The next two are for Ambrook. Mark each sack with a charcoal line before loading the cart. Do not stack meal beside the honey jars.
Linnet's carding bench is by the barn door. She will take clean fleece after the noon meal. Fleece with straw in it goes back to its owner. Fleece with burrs in it goes back with a note.
Pip from Kettle Ridge came to help mend the west gate. He brought a small apprentice with him. The apprentice carried six fence posts from the shed and placed them in a neat row. It then carried the same six posts back to the shed. Pip says it had been taught to fetch posts, but not where to leave them.
We have marked a place beside the gate. A board there says:
> bring me a fence post
= fetch fence post
Pip tested the order with one post. The apprentice brought it to the mark. We will teach it the rest after the fence is mended.
Please return tools to the rack. The hammer belongs to Jory, the spade belongs to Fen, and the red-handled rake belongs to no one who will admit it.
There is a basket of apples by the gate for the workers. Take one. Leave the bruised ones for the pie, unless you like a soft apple. Sella likes them best.
Linnet, wool carder

Notice: rules for the kitchen
These are the rules of my house at the edge of Copperhold. Read them once. Then come in and eat.
The kitchen:
- Wash your hands at the barrel.
- Wipe your boots on the mat. The mat is there for a reason.
- Do not stand in front of the oven. It is hot and I am working.
- Bread at night is for the miners coming up. Wait your turn.
- Bring your own bowl if you have one. I have six. They go walking.
The yard:
- The dog is friendly. The dog is also hungry. Do not feed her at the table.
- The hen house is behind the wall. Shut the gate. The hens do not know the way back.
- The well is dry and the lid stays on. Draw water at the mine gate until the masons come.
- The woodpile is for the oven. Ask before you take.
- The bees by the fence are not ours. They belong to the hives on the hill. Leave them be and they will leave you be.
The apprentices:
- If an apprentice comes to the door with an order card, read it and do what it says. It is carrying for someone.
- Do not tease an apprentice. It does not understand a joke. It will stand there all day.
- Clank carries the bread at noon. Do not take his tray. Do not tell him the bread is for the pigs.
The notice board:
- Put your notice here if it is short. I will not have a long notice on my board. Write it small.
- Lost things go by the door. Found things too.
- No notices about who owes who. Settle that at the mine hall.
I have kept this kitchen for eleven years. The soup is the same soup. The rules are the same rules. Come in, sit down, and be warm.
Fallon, cook, Copperhold, Leaffall of 207

Letter: Nessa to Tam, about Pip
Tam,
Your Pip came down the Long Road with a cart of beetroot and stayed a week at the house. She is quick. She is also untidy, which I hear runs in the family.
She watched me drill a young mind on the sorting, apples and pears, and she asked a good question: does the mind learn the shape or the color? We tested it. It learns the color. A green apple it calls a pear, every time. Pip laughed so hard she fell off the bench. Then she fixed it in a morning by showing it green apples beside short green pears. She has a good eye for a wrong answer.
She says your top field is ready early. She says the scarecrow has lost its head again.
We walked a goat back from the north. Long story. It had walked to Northpoint by itself, which is further than most folk go. Mira sent it down the line, keeper to keeper, and it arrived here fat and pleased with itself.
The relay on the hill is working. The lamp is small but it blinks true. Tell Pip the frog on my door says hello.
- Nessa

Notice: Lio is home
Lio, son of the reed cutter, ran off after breakfast on the fourth of Blossom. He went north along the boardwalk with one shoe, a wooden cup, and a piece of beetroot in his pocket.
He is nine years old. He has brown hair, a blue coat, and a strong opinion about frogs. If you see him, do not chase him through the reeds. Call his name and wait by the nearest torch.
His mother looked by the fish weir. Wren checked the boat houses. Piper sent word to the next tower. At noon, Moss found Lio asleep in the shed beside Wren's healing house.
He says he was looking for the old pond on the map. It is still mud. He says the map should show that.
Lio is safe, and his mother has him. His father says the wooden cup is not lost. It is now Lio's cup for carrying water.
Thank you to the fishers who stopped their boats and to Bel who remembered the shed door was open. Lio will help stack reeds tomorrow. He has been told to keep both shoes on.

Spiders in the Hobb barn, the big barn on the north road. The big kind that walk.

They came in with the first cold. They sit in the roof beams by day and come down by night.

Do not go into the Hobb barn after dark. Keep torches lit at all four corners. The watch will check the torches every hour.

If you find a spider in your own barn, do not be ashamed. Light a torch.

Nell, night watch

Notice: new copper below the ridge
A new copper vein has been found below the north slope, beyond the old stone marker. The miners have marked the path with torches. Do not follow the torches into the cut. The ground is steep, and the loose stones roll underfoot.
The first cart of copper came up yesterday. The ore is bright green where it is fresh. Hesk says it is good copper, and Hesk is not a man who gives praise to a rock without reason.
The miners will make a safe path before anyone carries more ore. Until then, keep children and animals away from the cut. Do not take stones from the marker. It is not a pile of spare stones.
Elm's grandson found a copper chip near the path and brought it home in his pocket. Elm found it in the wash water. The chip is now on the mill board, where it belongs.
The new vein may bring work to the ridge. The miners ask for turnips, bread, and clean water at the shaft. Bring supplies to the gate at noon. Do not leave them by the cut.
The north slope is not a shortcut to Elm's house. Use the road, even if it is longer.
Posted by Hesk and Tam, Reaping of 196

Notice: a word on the board, from Tam
Pearmain board, winter. Written by Tam, who stayed three days too long.

I came to Pearmain to buy cider. I stayed for the song contest and now I cannot leave, because someone has hidden my boots. I suspect Ama.

While I am here I have been asked to write a few words on how the tutors say a mind learns what words mean. I said I was a farmer, not a tutor. They said I explain things the way a farmer would, and so I should. I have told it to the barn, and now I write it for the board.

When I put out beetroot seed, I do not know where each seed will land. I know that the seed in the wet corner will grow, and the seed on the stone will not. A mind is the same. It sees a word in a thousand places, and each time it learns a little about the corner the word likes.

After a thousand times, "apple" is always in the corner of "tree" and "pie" and "bee." After a thousand more, it knows "pear" is in that corner too, even though it has seen "pear" less. It does not know what an apple is. It knows what lives next to it.

That is what the tutors mean when they say a word is a place. It is a place in the mind where the neighbors are the same neighbors that word has in our sentences.

I asked the tutor what a place is made of, and she said numbers. A long row of numbers, one for each side of the place. I asked how many numbers. She said a few hundred, for a good mind. I said that is a lot of sides for one apple. She said apples are complicated.

My boots have been found. They were in the barn, on the goat.

Tam
%%
Notice: spiders in the inn barn
Pinned to the barn door at the Halfway Inn, for the stable boy and the carters who sleep in the straw. Written by Jory, who was asked to write a list of the spiders, so that the carters would stop talking about them at breakfast.

The spiders in the inn barn are of three kinds.
The large grey kind, which sits at the end of a thread, high in the rafters, and drops down when you look up. It is not poisonous. It does not want to be your friend.
The small brown kind, which lives in the hay bales and comes out in the evenings to see who is sleeping. It is a little bit rude.
The white kind, very fine, which spins its web across the lantern on the post, so the light is thin and grey. The stable boy says this is because the spider wants the light for itself. Jory says the spider is only tidy.

What to do about them.
Do not kill the large grey kind. It eats the flies. The horses do not like the flies.
Sweep the small brown kind out of the hay with the long broom, but not onto the sleeping carters.
Clean the white kind off the lantern glass each morning, with a dry cloth. Do not use water. The web comes off better dry.

The barn has been swept and the rafters cleared. Some spiders have come back. Jory says that is fair, because the barn was theirs first.

Please do not wake the spiders by singing. The horses find it upsetting, and so do the spiders.
%%
Notice: Recipe for Apple Cakes
The recipe was copied from a paper found under the flour bin. It is pinned here for the fair cooks.
Mix two bowls of flour with one bowl of chopped apples. Add honey, a little water and one egg. Stir until the dough holds together. Make small cakes and bake them in the hot oven.
Do not add salt from the fish jar. That jar is marked in large letters. It is not sugar.
Do not put the cakes on the oven floor. They will burn there, as the first batch did. Put them on a tray and ask the baker before you use the fire.
If the dough sticks to your hands, add flour. If it sticks to the table, scrape it up and add flour. If it sticks to the baker, ask the baker to stand still.
The fair cooks made the first batch with salt. The second batch was put on the oven floor. The third batch was not offered to anyone.
The fourth batch was good. It was a little hard, but the bees liked it.
Bring one clean bowl to the kitchen before Longday.
Yarrow, farm board, summer of 174

Notice: the apprentice
A notice for the apprentices and the carriers who use the Northpoint relay. Spring.

An order must say what it means. Here is a case from last winter, copied from the desk book.

The order, written at the desk: fetch the lamp oil from the cellar.
The apprentice understood: fetch the lamp.
It fetched the lamp from the lamp room, down the ninety steps, and carried it to the cellar, and set it on the cellar floor, and waited for the oil to come out of it.

Nothing came out. The lamp was empty.

The keeper said the order was not wrong, only short. The apprentice had heard two words and guessed the rest.

Next time, write: bring me the oil can from the cellar shelf, the one with the red cap. Name the thing. Name where it is. Name the cap if you can.

The apprentice has since been retested. It fetched the right can the first time. It also came back up the stair without the lamp, which is also right.

Song for the last night of Leaffall
Sung at the Kettle Inn on the ridge road, on the last night of Leaffall, by the baker's girl of Three Wells. Written out after by Oren, clerk, who was there, and who clapped.
The bees are in the hive, the hive is in the hill,
the apples are all down and the wind is never still,
the mill wheel turns and squeaks, the mill wheel turns and squeaks,
and the honey in the jar will last us all the weeks.
So light the lamp and come inside, the nights are growing long,
and sing a song of Leaffall, a small and quiet song.
The goose came home, the boot is on the shelf,
the hedge is mended with the oak, we mended it ourselves,
the cart is in the yard, the cart is in the yard,
and the torches on the hill road burn, and keep the dark off hard.
So light the lamp and come inside, the nights are growing long,
and sing a song of Leaffall, a small and quiet song.
The lights were in the north, the north is far away,
some say they were stars and some say fires, and I say let them stay,
the relay blinks, the relay blinks,
and the keeper at the tower writes it down in ink.
So light the lamp and come inside, the nights are growing long,
and sing a song of Leaffall, a small and quiet song.
Nessa gave the honey to the baker's girl. She said the song was short, which from Nessa is high praise. The good seat by the fire is hers for a year. Oren copied the song into the archive the next morning, in the copying room, in the bright light, and left a space after the last line, in case anyone wished to add a verse.

Notice: after the harvest, a thank you
To everyone who stayed at the Brown Hen this autumn, and to everyone who carried grain through the door on their backs.
The barn is full. The loft is full. The cellar is so full that Marda has put the potatoes in the room where the guests sleep, and has asked the guests to be tactful.
It was the best harvest on this stretch of the road in twenty years. Wheat from the ridge, beetroot from the long field, more apples than we have jars. There is a jar of apple butter on the table for anyone who stops. Take a spoon. Take two. Do not take the jar.
A word about the pigs. They have been eating very well, and they know it.
Winter will be long, they say. We do not mind. The cellar says otherwise.
Marda, the Brown Hen, winter of 189

Notice: the school play, Frost
The children of the school at Three Wells will perform a play on the last day of Frost, in the school house, when the bell rings for dusk.
The play is called The Long Road. It has seven children, two carts made of chairs, one cat, and a very large moon made from the bottoms of barrels. Hollis made the moon. Please do not look too closely at the joins.
Parents may come. Please bring your own cushion. The school has eleven cushions and forty children.
No apples are to be thrown in the school house, at the play or at any other time. Last year an apple was thrown at the moon. The moon was not hurt. The apple was not returned.
Keep your torches outside the door, not in the room. A torch near the moon is a bad idea. We have had enough excitement for one winter.
The play is free. A bowl will be by the door for the roof. Put in what you have, not what you wish you had.
Written by Runa, who is in charge of the costumes. She would like it understood that the cat was not her idea.

Notice: how the big mind reads
Posted at the relay desk of the Halfway Inn, by Corin, shipwright, who sat through Nessa's talk and was asked by Hobb to write it down for those who were not there. Autumn.

Travelers may write to the big mind at Vell through the desk by the fire. One emerald for ten words, as for any relay message. Its answer comes back the same way, usually before the soup is cold.

What it is. A big mind lives in a crystal at the relay house at Vell. It is not a person. It is not an apprentice. It does not walk or carry. It reads, and it writes.

How it reads. It reads a letter at a time, through a window about sixty letters wide. That is about one line of a page. It cannot see farther back than the window. If your letter is longer than the window, it forgets how the letter began. So write short, and put the question first.

For each letter in the window, it asks which of the earlier letters matter for what comes next. It takes what it needs from those, and little from the rest. Then it guesses the next letter. This is how it was drilled, on pages from the Archive, and this is how it answers.

How it writes. It writes a letter by guessing one letter, adding it to the page, and guessing again. Because it has read so many letters, its letters sound like people. Some are so like a person's that a reader cannot tell. Nessa says this is the oldest question in the tutors' books, and she does not mind that it has come back.

What to remember.
1. It can be wrong, and sound sure. Ask a person too.
2. It does not remember your last letter, unless you put the last letter in this one.
3. It writes the way the pages it read were written. It may sound like a clerk. It may sound like a farmer. Ask it to speak plainly and it will try.
4. It has never met a bee, a well or a goat. It has read about thousands of them. Weigh its letters accordingly.
5. It cannot say why it writes what it writes. Neither can the tutors.

Two examples from this autumn. The well at the Brown Hen ran dry. Marda wrote to the mind. It said to dig deeper at the south corner, and that a well digger would know better. A swarm of bees landed on the inn sign. Hobb wrote to the mind. It said to stand away and wait for a keeper. In both cases, a person came and agreed with it.

That is all Corin was told. Please do not ask the mind to build you a boat. Corin has asked, and it gave a very polite answer, and the answer began with the sails.
%%
Lost: one brown hen with a limp
Lost on the second day of Blossom, from the yard behind the potter's house. She is brown, with one white tail feather, and she limps on the left foot. She answers to Crumb. She does not answer to anything else, and she is not impressed by the name.

We searched the yard, the drill yard, the reed beds below the wall, and the top row of the bean field. Nobody found her. The apprentices searched every row of the bean field twice, because they do not get tired, and they reported no hen. They did report three eggs in the hedge, which they have put in the store under a card that says: not the hen.

If you find her, bring her to the potter's yard, and the potter will give you a loaf of bread for her, and a second loaf for the apprentices, who will want to thank you properly. Please do not leave her in a cart. She does not like carts. She has never liked anything that moves when she is not moving.

Willa, potter, for the board.

Letter: Hesk to Dell, about the tin
Dell, I have the tin. Seven emeralds, counted twice. You did not need to count twice.
I lent you the money because your roof went in Frost, and a roof is a roof.
You have paid me by the first of Sowing, which is sooner than I expected. Good.
Now to business. The market board says the lift is closed for the fair.
I am the one who has to check the lift before the fair, so I will do it.
Clank will hold the lamp and I will look down the shaft. He is good at holding a lamp.
The zombie at the gate. Folk keep asking me about it. It was one zombie. One.
There will be more as the weather warms. That is the way of it. Keep the torches lit.
Tell your boat builder friends there is no water here, and they should build a cart instead.
If you want work, the sorting shed needs a new bin. The old one is split.
Bring your tools and your good temper, and we will see it done by market day.
Hesk, at the mine gate

Notice: after the flood
Pinned to the board at Three Wells, Thaw of 201. By Merle, healer. These rules hold for ten days.

The water has gone down. The mud has not. Here is what to do.

1. Wells. Three Wells has three wells, and the flood went into two of them. Do not drink from the east or the south well. The north well is high and was not reached. Use the north well. Queue politely. It is cold, and so is the queue.
2. Drink only water that has been boiled. Boil it longer than you think. If you are not sure, boil it again.
3. Dry your beds. A damp bed makes a sick child. Hang blankets on the fence in the sun, not on the floor, where the mud is.
4. Wash your hands before you eat. Wash them after the mud. Wash them again if you have touched a pig.
5. Watch for the cough and the cold shakes. If a child has either, bring the child to my house by the bakery. Do not wait until night.
6. Jug is clearing the low street. It works from dawn until dusk and does not stop to talk. Please do not help by moving mud where it has already cleared. It will begin again.

What the flood left behind. One boat, in the square, belonging to nobody. One door, with a brass knocker, from nobody knows where. Forty-one boots, all left feet. Come and claim them.

Gifts. Ambrook has sent a cart of apples, 100 in all. Copperhold has sent stone for the low street wall. Saltreach has sent salt fish, and a note that says: we are sorry about the water, we have plenty. These are on the long table in the back room of the inn. Take what you need, and write it on the board.

The bridge is closed until the posts are set. The mayor says ten days. The mayor says it with his hat on, which means he is thinking.
Merle, healer

Notice: festival day
Posted on the board by the stable and on the door of the inn in the hamlet near Copperhold, in the week before the summer festival. Colm, stable hand, wrote it out three times, and the third time is the one they kept.
Neighbors. The summer festival is on the second day of Longday. The sun will rise at the fifth bell. The first event is at the sixth. Be there.
THE PLAN
Sixth bell: breakfast in the field. Bread, honey, eggs. Free.
Seventh bell: the horse parade. All horses and mules, with ribbons. The judge is Dilly, who knows horses.
Eighth bell: races for children.
Ninth bell: the pie contest. Bring one pie, any kind. Please label it, or we will not know whose is whose and the judge will weep.
Tenth bell: lunch. Soup from the mine gate kitchen. Cook has said she will bring 200 bowls.
Noon: the song contest. Hesk will sing. He says he will sing something about coal. We have asked him not to. He will sing it anyway.
Afternoon: games, a quiet hour, a nap.
Evening: the lamps are lit. The dance. The old fiddler from the ridge will play. He is deaf in one ear, but he is the best we have.
THE RULES
1. No horse races on the cobbles. They are slippery.
2. No one drinks from the horse trough. This is a rule that we should not need.
3. Keep the children away from the pond edge. Not because it is deep. Because it is muddy.
4. Do not feed the grey mare. She has had four apples. She will have no more.
5. No dogs near the pies.
6. No goats near the pies.
7. No cats near the pies. We know this one is useless. Write it anyway.
THE SNOW JOKE
Every year someone says, "Remember the year it snowed at the festival?" It did, once. On the second day of Longday, in the year of the long frost, a handful of snow fell at noon. It lasted ten minutes. The pie contest was held under a cart. Nobody has forgotten.
If it snows this year, we will hold the dance under the cart too. The cart is ready.
THE THANKS
Thank you to the stable, which lends the field. Thank you to the mine, which sends the cook. Thank you to Dilly, who judges. Thank you to Hesk, who sings. Thank you to the fiddler. Thank you to the grey mare, for being patient.
And thank you to you. The festival is not a thing we do. It is a thing we are.
Colm

Notice: the even numbers, for children
Posted at the school board at Northpoint, in Longday, by Wynn, grandfather, who was asked to explain the even numbers to the children, and did, once, and the children asked him to do it again.
A mind keeps its numbers in its layers. A number goes in at the bottom layer. Each layer does a sum with it and passes it to the next. A layer may make the number bigger. It may make it smaller.
Here is the trouble. If each layer makes the number a little bigger, then after twenty layers the number is huge. If each layer makes it a little smaller, then after twenty layers the number is tiny. Either way the last layer hears only shouting, or only a whisper, and it cannot tell one answer from another.
So a good mind brings the numbers back, at each layer, to an even size. It is like a clerk who copies every page to the same width, so the shelf stays straight. Each layer takes its number, makes it the right size for the next layer, and passes it on.
Remember: a number that is too wild is no good to the next layer. A number that is even is a number the whole mind can hear.
The tutors who found this trick called it bringing the numbers even. Nobody knows who first thought of it, and the tutors do not agree on that. They agree that without it, the deep minds do not learn at all.
Wynn, grandfather

Notice: the summer festival
Posted on the board by the stable, the hamlet near Copperhold, in Longday. Written by Colm, stable hand, who was asked by the mayor's cousin to do the lettering because his hand is clearer than anyone's.
The summer festival begins at noon on the longest day and lasts until the lamps are lit.
What happens:
A horse race, round the field. Ribbons for first, second and third. A different ribbon for last, which is green.
A pie contest. Judged by Cook from the mine gate, who is fair and who is fierce.
A song contest. The winner gets a jar of honey. The loser also gets a jar of honey. It is a small place.
Races for children: running, sack, egg and spoon. The egg is a real egg.
Boats on the pond. There are no boats. There is a bucket. Please do not sink it.
What to bring: a blanket, a hat, a pie, a cup. A good mood, if you have one.
What not to bring: dogs, unless on a rope. Goats, unless asked.
The mine will be closed for the day, except for Hesk, who says he has things to do and will not say what. He has promised to come to the song contest. He has promised to sing. We do not know what to expect.
The cider is made by the old man at the bend. It is strong. Do not drink more than two cups unless you have a long walk home.
See you at noon.
Colm
