Notice: about the words in the mind
Pinned up at the tutor's gate at Vell, so that the apprentices who come for the summer will see it. Written by Nessa.
People have asked me how a mind knows what a word means. The answer is not simple, and I will try to give it plainly.
A mind keeps every letter and every word as a place among many places. Think of a big field with many paths. Each word has a spot in the field. The words used in the same way, in the same kinds of sentence, are put close together. Words that are never used alike are far apart.
So "bread" sits near "flour" and "oven", because the words are used together. "Bread" sits far from "snow" and "ship". Nobody set these places by hand. The mind found them by reading many sentences, and by seeing which words come near which.
A mind that knows where a word is can tell you what it means, a little, by what is near it. It can guess a word it has not seen before, if it knows what is around that word. This is why a plain page is easy to learn from. The words are used in clear ways, so the places are clear.
This is still a guess. The mind does not know the word the way you know your mother's voice. It knows where the word is, among the others. We do not know why that should be enough. We only see that it is, most of the time.
If you want to test a mind on words, try a sentence with a word left out. Ask it what is missing. If it fills the gap with a word from the same place in the field, it has learned the place. If it fills it with a word from the far side, it has not.
%%

Notice: the fishing boats of Saltreach
Pinned to the board at Three Wells, in a child's hand, with a line from the mayor. Spring. Kept by the school.

This notice is from Bel, who has a cousin at Saltreach.

My cousin Hale wrote to me. He says the Marit boat did not come home on the day it should have. It went out at dawn with three people and a lot of nets. The storm came in the afternoon. The harbor lamp was lit all night. The other boats came in one by one, and each time the whole quay cheered. The Marit boat came in the next morning, slow, with a torn sail and a lot of fish. They said the wind took them out past the point. They said the sea was very quiet after.

My cousin says everyone has been telling it differently. One man says the fish were fewer. One woman says the boat saw lights on the water. A boy says it was only the lamp of another boat. My cousin says he does not know, and the people who were there say they do not know either, and that is fine.

I am putting it on the board because the school asked us to write the news from other towns. Please do not take it down. If you have news from another town, write it under mine.

From the mayor: Bel is right to put it up. News from the coast is welcome here. When it comes with sources, even better.

Below, in another hand: My sister at Saltreach says the big ships were not at the slip this week. Nobody has said why. She says the shipyard is quiet.

Below, in another hand: I heard they are all at the far dock. Corin keeps them there.

Bel

Notice: tutors' fair
Summer fair at the Vell tutor house. All folk may watch from the yard.

The tutors will show three minds sorting apples and pears. One mind has fewer marks in its weights, so it fits inside a smaller crystal. It learned from the same kind of examples as the others. It may be a little less exact.

The tutors will use a new basket for the test. None of the minds has seen its fruit before. Please do not call out the answers. A mind learns from what it is shown, and the test must be fair.

There will be bread at the door. The baker asks that everyone take one loaf, not two. Last fair, the children took two and said the second was for a mind. The mind did not eat it.

Fen will bring apples from the lower field. If the small crystal sorts them all, he will say so. If it misses one, he will say that too.

After the test, Nessa will explain how fewer marks can help a crystal fit in a smaller space. It does not mean the mind knows more. It does not mean it knows less in every task.

The cat from the copying room must stay out of the fruit baskets. It has been told three times.

Notice: Lessons at the House
Board beside the gate at Elm's house, Ambrook. Reaping, 203.

Elm will teach a short drill for helpers on the fifth and sixth days of Apple. The lesson is for anyone who tends a small mind in a crystal, or wants to learn how.

Bring a crystal, its card, and a plain example. Do not bring a mind that has not been tested. The field is not a place to find out that a helper fetches a hoe when you ask for a bowl.

We will show the crystal apples and pears. When it sorts one wrongly, mark the answer. Move each weight a little toward the right answer, then try again. Do not move all the weights at once. A mind needs room to learn, and a tutor needs room to remember what changed.

Bring more than one example. If the crystal sees only red apples, it may sort every red thing as an apple. We will use apples, pears, carrots, and a piece of wool. Keep the wool out of the food basket.

A test box will be kept on Elm's bench. These pages are not for practice. The minds will not see them until the test. If a mind gets them right, we will know more than if it repeats a lesson it already knows.

After the lesson, the helpers may carry empty baskets from the door to the shed. They may not carry jars of honey. One jar broke last year, and the bees came into the teaching room. The bees learned nothing from the drill, but Elm learned to shut the window.

The house has a well, a dry room, and three spare tool handles. Please return the handles to the rack. Do not put a wet crystal beside the well. Set it on the felt by the tutor's bench.

There will be bread at midday. The lesson ends when the bread is gone, or when the minds are tired, whichever comes first. Elm says minds do not eat bread. Last year one apprentice carried the loaf to the shed and stayed there beside it. We counted that as a mistake and a good warning.

Ask Elm at the gate if you need a place to sleep. The house has two spare beds. Bring your own blanket.

Letter: Zell to his daughter at Vell
Summer of 190. From Zell at Saltreach to his daughter Sera, a clerk at the Archive at Vell. She is well. I am well. The knees are bad but the fish are good.
There was a fox at the hens, my dear. A big one, red as a coal, came over the harbor wall like it owned the place. It took one hen and left the rest so scared they would not lay for a day. Your brother set a trap of sticks and a bucket and caught nothing but a gull. The gull was very angry. We let it go.
Market day was loud. The board raised the price of salmon by one, and a woman argued with it, the board, not me, for an hour. She lost. She bought the salmon anyway.
I bought a new net. It is a good net. It has not caught a fish yet, but it is a good net. Write to me. Tell the archive I said hello. Tell Oren his ink is too dear.
Your father, Zell

Note: what we found inside the crystal
Fallon's hand, at the inn, copied from a tutor's note. I cut stone, but I read.
A tutor at Vell looked inside a working crystal last winter. Not at it. Inside it. The old way was to drill a mind and see what it did. The new way is to open it and see what each part does.
She did not open it like a box. She did it with care. She cut the work into pieces, small and plain. She gave a mind a page and watched which parts of the crystal grew bright. Then she blocked one part and gave the same page. If the answer changed, she knew that part had a share in it. She did this again and again, and drew what she found on a board.
Some of it she found. There was a part that learns where a letter sits among the letters before it, and a part that brings the mind back to an even size when its numbers grow wild. There was a part that holds the stream that runs through the whole mind. She could point to each one and say: this does that.
The rest she did not find. The parts lie tangled together, she said. A part that carries a word and a part that carries a count use the same marks, and when you block one you spoil the other. She found some of the mind and not the rest, and she wrote that down too, plain.
She said it is like a wall I built. You can point to the stone that bears the weight. You cannot point to the mortar. You know it is there. You cannot take it out.
Why a crystal learns at all, she did not say. Nobody has. She only looked, and told us what she saw.
Fallon

The well at the Brown Hen has run dry. It has not run dry in thirty years. Travelers: water is in the trough, one bucket each, from the mill pond at the foot of the hill. Marda asks that you do not wash in the trough.

Marda wrote to the big mind at Vell, through the relay desk, to ask what to do. Its letter is pinned below. It says: dig deeper at the south corner, where the ground is lower. It says it is not sure, and that a well digger would know better. Marda has sent for Senna. She trusts Senna.

Corin, passing, autumn.

Notice: market day at the weir
The Lowmarsh market is on the first day of each week, on the boardwalk by the fish weir. Stalls go on the posts, not in the water.
Fish sellers, bring your own tubs. The weir keeper will not lend his.
Eel sellers, keep the eels in the baskets. They have been seen on the boardwalk twice this summer, and both times they went straight for the healing house steps.
Reed sellers, bundle your reeds by length. Long reeds go to the boat house. Short ones go to the Archive, for paper, and must be dry.
Bread sellers, you must sell before the frogs come. Frogs eat bread. Frogs do not pay.
Anyone who owes an emerald at the market must pay it at the market. Debts are written on the board by the weir. Once they are paid, they are crossed out in front of the seller.
Market closes at the evening bell. Carry your own lamp home.

Letter: Fen to the keeper at Northpoint
Fen at the Halfway Inn to Mira at Northpoint, the twelfth of Longnight, 192
Mira,
The relay came through fine tonight. I heard your lamp before the snow came, three short and one long, and I knew it was you, because you always end with the long one like a full stop.
The inn is full. Six carts stopped by the ice, and a family from Copperhold with two children and a goat. The goat is in the wood store. The children are by the fire. The goat is warmer than the children.
The road east is bad past the second waymark. Tell anyone coming this way to bring a shovel and patience. The bridge holds, but the oak is green and it complains.
How is Lantern? Does the apprentice keep the lamps well? I hear the north has had long nights and no sun for a while. Keep your torches lit and your kettle on. If you run short of anything, send me one short blink and I will send a cart. One blink only. Two blinks means you are fine, and I will worry.
We had a lost dog here. Nip. She came home. She always comes home.
Your brother Tam paid his debt in emeralds at last. I made him count them out loud. You would have liked it.
Write when the snow lets you. I will keep the board by the fire for your letter.
Fen

Notice: Vell autumn work and thanks
Posted by Teague for the dyers, the school and the Archive clerks.

The leaves are falling on the hill paths. Please sweep the front steps of the Archive before they become slick.
The Archive has no roof, it has a hill, but the front steps still collect leaves.
Clerks will lend a broom from the hall. Return it with its handle dry.

The dyers need oak fuel for the pots.
Bring dry pieces to the shed behind my house.
Do not take wood from the hedge by the east field. That hedge keeps the wind off the beans.
I will pay one emerald for each full bundle.
The bundles must be tied. A loose heap is not a bundle, even if it looks hopeful.

The school needs jars for the autumn lessons.
Bring clean jars with no cracks. The tutor will fill them with seeds and label each one.
Do not write on the jars before bringing them. Nessa has neat labels and a patient hand.
The children have already named one jar "King Apple". It contains beetroot seed.

On the twelfth of Leaffall, the hall will hold a song contest.
There will be three places for singers and room for listeners along the wall.
No one must sing over another singer. A song may have a verse about work, weather, a lost spoon or a stubborn goat.
The judges will give a honey jar for the song remembered longest.
Teague will give a length of blue cloth for the song with the clearest words.
The judges will not award a prize for the loudest voice. The last contest shook dust from the shelf.

Bring a bowl for the soup afterward. The school will cook one pot of potato soup.
The baker has offered bread. The innkeeper will lend benches.
If you bring a chair, mark your name under the seat. Chairs have walked away from this hall before.

The cave below the west field remains fenced.
The mason says the opening needs a safer edge before anyone looks inside.
Do not move the fence, and do not send an apprentice to test the ground.
A helper may carry stones for the mason when asked, but it must stay on the path until the mason marks a safe place.
The farmer has planted the upper rows. The lower rows can wait.

Lost items may be left at Teague's door, beside the cracked blue pot.
Found items will be written here until claimed.
Last week, one wooden spoon, a mitten, and a very small hat were returned.
The hat belonged to a doll. The doll's owner said the hat was better than new.
A black cat was found sleeping in the lost basket. It was not returned. It left by itself.

Keep the lamps lit at the hill turns.
The nights are cool, the paths are busy, and the leaves hide loose stones.
If you borrow a lantern, bring it back before supper.
If you borrow the broom, bring it back dry.
If you borrow the blue pot, ask first.
Teague needs it for the dye.

Notices from the board, spring
Gathered from the board by the well on the first market day of Blossom. Taken down when the board was full, and copied here so that nobody forgets who wanted what.

Wanted: a hand for the hay. Two weeks, from the second bell until the last bale. Pay in bread and a bed in the barn. Ask at the top farm. Do not ask for the hay to be cut in a hurry. It will be cut when it is ready.

For sale: a cart, one wheel fewer than it should have, but the other three are very good. Price in emeralds, as agreed. Bring your own wheel if you want the trip to be smooth.

Found: a pair of gloves, red, too big for anyone on the ridge. Found at the sheepfold. The sheep say they are not theirs. Ask for Bram at the fold, who will say the same thing, more loudly.

Lost: a red hen, the best layer on the ridge, last seen going into the mill house and not coming out. She has laid three eggs in the mill house since. The miller says the eggs are his. Please let the hen come home, and let the eggs go with her.

Thank you. The Long Road cart brought new salt, and the salt was good. The Kettle Inn kitchen wishes to say that the salt was better than the flour. That is not kind to the flour, but the flour is a farmer's, and it will forgive us.

Warning. Do not leave a pail of milk by the well for longer than it takes to drink it. The cats have learned the well steps, and the cats have learned the pails.

Market day is the fourth of Blossom. Stalls by the well, the mill and the inn. The wool sellers stand under the barn. The baker is at the north corner. Please do not stand between the baker and the queue, because the queue will not move, and the baker will not move either.

Found: a small copper bell with a cow's name scratched on the clapper. The name is Hazel. Hazel has not been found, and the cow has been asked to answer for the bell. She has not answered.

Wanted: a way to teach the bees that the clover is on the north hill and not on the mill. They keep going to the mill. The miller keeps asking them to stop.

Song: the long song will be sung again on the fourth of Blossom, by the groom's uncle, with the verses in the right order this time. The verses are on a board at the inn.

Reminder: the bell is for noon and for dusk. It is not for the cat, the cow, or the farmer who is late for his own pie. The ridge is open. Come up, and mind the stone on the north side. Your friends are at the Kettle Inn, and they have been waiting since the first bell, and they are hungry.

Notice: the road shut at the mill ford
Posted at the mill ford and the inn at Ambrook, by the Ambrook council. Written in autumn, after the rain.
The mill ford is shut to carts until the water goes down. The stepping stones are under two hand's depth of water, and the ford has moved a little to the left, and the old crossing is no longer safe for anyone.
Carts going to Three Wells must go round by the upper bridge, which is slow and steep. Carts going to Copperhold must go round by the old mill track. Do not try the ford in the dark. The water is loud, and it will not tell you where it is deep.
Travelers on foot may cross on the rope at the north end. Hold the rope with both hands. Keep the rope on your left side, and walk slowly. Two children were carried off in the spring, and the council does not wish to write about it again.
Notice to the mill men: the sluice gate is to stay shut until the council says otherwise. It is not to be opened by anyone, however sure they are that the water will go down by itself. Jory has been told this by the council three times.
The inn at Ambrook has beds for twelve. The soup is hot. The council thanks you for your patience and asks that you do not argue with the mill men about the sluice. They have been arguing with the council about it since Thaw, and it is making everyone tired.

Notice: found
Posted at the fish market and at Wren's door. The last day of Longday.
Found: a brown goat, white nose, one short horn, at the fish weir, standing in the water up to her knees, eating reeds. She was not lost. She knew exactly where she was. We were the ones who were worried.
She has walked a long way this summer. Northpoint to Three Wells to here, keeper to keeper, and she came home down the boardwalk with her nose in the air.
Bramble is home. The blue door is open. The honey is eaten and there is more where it came from.
Thank you to Mira, who gave her boots to eat. Thank you to Lantern, who carried the rope. Thank you to the keeper at the little relay, who sent word down the line. Thank you to the goat, for coming back.
A note for the school: Bel asked why a goat can walk to Northpoint and a mind cannot. Good question. A mind can ride in an apprentice and walk anywhere. It just gets lost more easily, and it does not eat reeds. We are working on the rest.
- Nessa
%%
Notice: the long winter
Posted at the slip, in the middle of Longnight, by Corin.
It is the fortieth day of snow. The sea is gray and the pier is white. No boat has gone out since the third of Frost.
The wood store at the slip is still half full. It was filled for boats. This winter it will be filled with warm people instead. So:
1. Anyone may take wood from the store for a stove. Take what you can carry in two trips, not three.
2. Write your name and your load on the board by the door. Corin will not count it. Corin will only read it.
3. The shed beside the store is open to anyone who has no fire. There are benches, blankets and a barrel of dried fish. The stove there is lit day and night.
4. Do not burn the planks stacked along the east wall. They are oak, they are for the new boat, and they are drying. If you burn them, Corin will know, because he knows every plank by its knots.
5. The children may slide down the long ramp on a board. They may not slide into the sea. Last winter two did and both came up laughing. This winter the water is colder.
The ice at the edge of the harbor is thick enough to stand on at the wall and thin enough to fall through further out. A stick tied with red wool marks where it ends. Stay on this side of the wool.
The long winter will end. The ramp will thaw. The first boat will go down it with a bang and a splash, as always. Until then, take wood and keep warm.
Corin
%%
Notice: the gate at night
The gate in the wall at the north end of the market stays shut after dark.
We keep torches round the foot and a lamp in the gatehouse. Keep them lit.
Last night a zombie came up to the gate and pushed at it for a while.
It did not get in. The gate is oak and iron and it is fine.
But two of us had to go out with torches and see to it, and nobody likes that.
So: shut the gate at dusk, all of you. The last one through shuts it.
If you hear knocking, do not open it. Call the gatehouse. We will come.
Dell, for the market board

Notice: wood, bread and lamp oil
The hamlet has agreed a fair price for the winter. It is written here so that no one can say later that they were not told. Prices are in emeralds, and they are the same for everyone, and the council has checked the scales twice.

Wood, by the cartload: one emerald. Half a cartload, half an emerald. Wood cut from the hill, and not from the orchard, which is kept for the spring.

Bread, by the loaf: half an emerald. The baker's wife asks that the loaves not be squeezed for softness. They do not come back softer. They come back as crumbs, and the crumbs are not sold.

Lamp oil, by the jar: one emerald. The jar must be brought back. The jar is worth more than the oil, because the glass is hard to make in winter, and the glassmaker's boy has a cold.

Candles, by the box: two emeralds for twelve, and the wicks cut to the finger's length. The candle-woman from Copperhold says they will last the winter, if they are lit one at a time, and not all at once in a panic at dusk.

Coal, by the sack: when it comes up from Copperhold on the carts, the price is set at the gate. Ask the carter. He will tell you the truth, and he will ask for a little extra for the road.

Ivo, trader, for the hamlet board, and the council, who have checked the figures.
%%
Notice: a helper has come to the slip
To everyone who comes down to the slip: do not shout at the new helper.
It came on the last cart before the snow, in a crate with air holes, from the tutors at Vell. It is made of copper and iron and it stands about as tall as a barrel. It has no name yet. The children call it Plank, and I have stopped trying to stop them.
Plank does not talk. No helper does. It listens, and it does what it was drilled to do. The tutors write that it knows farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul and home, and nothing else. So:
Say your order plainly. "Bring me the small hammer" is an order. "Where did I put that thing" is not.
Do not tell it to fetch the boat. It will try.
Do not stand behind it when it hauls. It does not look back.
If it is wrong, say the order again, slowly. It will try the right thing. Minds change a little each time they are wrong.
Yesterday I told it to store the nails. It stored them in the wood store, one by one, in a perfect line along the floor. That is a store of a kind. I said thank you anyway.
If it sits in the corner and does nothing, it is waiting. Please do not poke it.
Corin
%%
Notice: a melon too big
On the board at the Halfway Inn, Long Road, summer. From Hobb, innkeeper.

There is a melon in the yard that is too big. I wish to say this so that nobody asks.

It came in a cart from Ambrook on the third of Melon. The grower said it was the best melon of the year. It is. It is the size of a barrel. It weighs more than a pig. Two men carried it into the yard, and one of them is still lying down.

It will not fit through the door. I tried. It will not fit into the cellar. I tried that too. It will not fit on any table. It sits in the yard, in the sun, and the dog walks round it every hour to make sure it is still there.

I cannot cut it. My longest knife is the length of my arm. The melon is wider than I am.

The contest tomorrow is for any pie, stew or dish made from a melon. I had planned to enter. Now I do not know where to start.

Anyone who can help with a very big melon, please see me in the yard. I will give you a bowl of soup, a bed, and as much melon as you can carry.

Hobb, who did not ask for this
%%
Notice: the debt is paid
This is written by Dell, who builds boats, though there is no water here to float them.
I owed Hesk, the miner, seven emeralds for the winter. He lent them when my roof went.
I said I would pay by spring, and spring is here, so I did.
I put them in the tin by the mine gate kitchen, where he goes for his soup.
Seven emeralds, counted twice. Hesk has the tin now. The debt is done.
I am writing this on the board so everyone knows the tin was mine and the debt was mine.
It is a small thing. But a debt is a debt, and folk should hear when it is paid.
If you owe someone, pay it. If you cannot, say so. We are all above the same mine.
Dell, boat builder, for the market board

Notice: Gate at Elm's House
Board by the gate at Elm's house, Ambrook. Spring of 176.

Please shut the gate after you pass. The sheep know how to push it open. They do not know how to close it.

Do not leave apples on the step. The bees come for them, then the chickens come for the bees.

Elm has put a hook beside the gate for baskets. It is not a hook for hats. Tam's hat fell into the mud last spring and still smells of it.

If you take a tool from the shed, put it back before dusk.

Thank you.
Elm

Summer board of the north market
This board is for fair rules, lost goods, and notices from the relay house. Please read it before asking Mave what has changed. She is baking, and flour does not wait for questions.

The sorting table will be set up beside the well on the sixth of Longday. Una of Ambrook will bring the crystal she taught to sort apples and pears. She first showed it fruits and the baskets they belonged in. When it chose wrongly, she moved each weight a little toward the answer she had given. She repeated this with many examples. The crystal grew better by small changes, not by one grand change.

The tutors will show the crystal new fruit for its test. They will not tell it the right basket until after it has chosen. This matters. If it has already seen the answer, we cannot learn how well it has learned. The test fruit will be kept in a covered tray. Children may watch from behind the rope, but must not name the fruit or point to a basket.

The tutors will write every choice in the fair book. If the crystal is wrong, they will show the answer after the choice is recorded. Una may then teach it from the mistake by moving its weights a little toward the right answer. The test will end before that teaching begins.

A tutor from Vell will answer questions after the fair. One question is not to be answered as fact: why a crystal learns from examples. Una knows how she taught hers. She did not write why the lesson worked. We have no better answer.

Mave has asked for two clean baskets. One is marked for apples and one for pears. Please do not trade them as a joke. Last summer, someone put a pear in the apple basket and said the crystal had done it. The crystal was still in its case.

A brown horse will stand by the lower well. The relay house has lent it to carry flour and two benches. It belongs to nobody at the market. Give it water, not bread. Mave has enough trouble keeping loaves from the children.

The north lamp blinked a strange pattern near the end of last summer. Lantern checked the relay line and found no message in the book. The keeper says a lamp mark counts as a message only when it is sent and recorded. If the lamp blinks without a record, report it. Do not send the same pattern from another tower.

Lost and found: a small basket with a blue cord, three pears, and a folded test paper. Mave has it under the bread table. The owner should say what is written on the paper before taking it away.

Keep the lane clear at dusk. The fair closes when the lamps are lit. The horse goes back to the relay house, the fruit goes to the tutors, and the baskets go to Mave. She has written all three duties down so no one can say they forgot.
%%
Rules for the sorting table
The sorting table will stand under the north market awning through the summer fair. Una of Ambrook has agreed to show her crystal at the table. Mave will lend two baskets. One is for apples, and one is for pears.

Visitors may watch from behind the rope. Please do not touch the baskets or the crystal. The crystal has no eyes like ours. It takes in the marks it was shown and gives an answer by the small arm beside it. A tutor can read that answer.

Una taught the crystal with many examples. She showed it an apple and showed it the apple basket. She showed it a pear and showed it the pear basket. When it chose the wrong basket, she moved each of its weights a little toward the answer she had shown. Then she tried another fruit. The crystal did not become right in one lesson. It became less often wrong after many lessons.

The weights are small marks inside the crystal. Each mark can change how strongly the crystal responds to what it has seen. Una changes them by a little, not by guessing at the whole answer. This is why the same lesson must be done again and again.

The test fruit will be different from the fruit used in the lesson. The tutors will write down every choice before they show the answer. Do not call out, point, or move a basket. If a visitor helps the crystal during the test, the test tells us nothing.

After the test, the tutors may teach it from its mistakes. The test fruit will be kept apart until then. Mave asks that nobody take an apple home by mistake. She knows how many she brought.
%%
Minutes: the hamlet meeting, spring
Kept by the clerk of the hamlet. Ninth of Swarm, 208.
Present: Hesk, Old Rill, Sanna of the kitchen, the smith, two miners, a child who came for the soup.
The goat is well. It lives with Rill now. It has a coat. This was noted and everyone was pleased.
The new apprentice sorts the ore bins well. It puts the coal with the coal. The copper goes with the copper. It has not made the old mistake for two months. Hesk says we will drill it on the iron next.
The relay house will answer our apprentices by letter. The keeper at the tower writes them out each morning. We will set a board for the answers.
The third bridge on the north road is still out. We will fix it when the water goes down. The smith will cut the stone.
The kitchen will keep the count board. Someone has been rubbing out the names too early. It was the child. The child had no soup. Now the child has soup.
Meeting closed. The bell rang. The goat was not allowed in. It came in anyway.

Elm lives in the house at the edge of Kettle Ridge, past the bottom field. The gate is not broken. It is meant to lean that way.
In winter, leave the path clear for his wood cart. Do not pile snow beside the gate. It slides back down and blocks the path.
Elm will take a small sack of potatoes for help with the wood. Bring the sack before dusk. He says he can see well enough after dusk, but last week he brought home the scarecrow.
The scarecrow is back in the field. The gate still leans.
Tam, for Elm, Frost of 174

Notice: lost and found, and a warning
Quill, schoolteacher, spring. Posted on the board by the door of the house at the edge of Northpoint.
LOST: one slate, with the name Wick on the back. One red mitten. One cap with a tassel. One lunch, in a cloth. The lunch was a bad lunch and I do not mind if it stays lost.
FOUND: a bundle of letters, tied with string, on the road to the tower. They are not addressed to anyone. They begin "Dear friend" and go on for many pages. If they are yours, come and tell me what they say. If you cannot tell me, then they are not yours.
FOUND: an old map, which I have put on the wall.
WARNING. The ice on the pond is thin. Do not skate. Do not throw stones. Do not send the cat out to see. The cat knows better.
WARNING. The brown cow in the lane is sick. Do not touch it. Do not feed it. The healer has said it needs rest and warm water and a quiet barn. A quiet barn is hard to find in this lane.
NOTICE. The next lesson is on maps. Bring your own, if you have one. Draw it on a slate if you must. We will compare.
Nothing else is lost. Everything else is found.
Quill

Notice: stall fees at the Vell market
Posted at the market gate, Vell, by the clerk of the market, autumn.

Stalls on the hill side: 1 emerald the day.
Stalls on the square: 2 emeralds the day.
Carts in the lane: a spoon of honey, or the same value in wool.
Shepherds with sheep: no fee. Sheep do not pay. They do however leave a mess, so please use the pen at the bottom gate.
Children with a stall of their own: no fee for the first day. After that, the usual fee, and a smile.

Fees are paid to the clerk at the gate, not to the man with the bell. The man with the bell has no authority. He has only a bell.

Notice: market rules for the autumn
These are the rules of the market at Kettle Ridge for the autumn. They are made by the market council and kept by the market clerk, and they are read aloud at the start of each market day. Please listen. It is shorter than you think.

One. Stalls are set up before the first bell and taken down after the dusk bell. A stall left up overnight is taken down by the clerk, and the stallholder pays one emerald to have it back.

Two. Prices are in emeralds, or in bread, or in a fair trade of goods agreed on the spot. A price must be said aloud before the goods are touched. A price said in a whisper is not a price.

Three. Measures are kept by the scales at the north end of the square. Every sack, jar and bundle is weighed there. A stallholder who brings a measure of his own will be asked to explain it.

Four. The honey woman's stall is not to be approached with apples. The honey woman has asked for this rule four times, and the council has agreed four times. It will be posted again next year.

Five. No horse is to be tied to the well. The well is for drinking, and so are the horses, but not at the same time.

Six. Children may not climb the stalls, the well, or the market board. Children who climb the board must read it aloud before they come down.

Seven. Carts that block the north road must be moved by the owner, or by the market apprentices, who will do it without complaint, but who will then write it on the board.

Eight. The torches on the north road are lit at dusk. Carters who come in after dark must carry their own lamps. Torches are for the road, not for the tallow that a careless carter leaves in the ditch.

Nine. A zombie seen near the market at dusk is reported to the clerk at once. Do not chase it. Do not let it into the square. The clerk will light more torches, and the council will hear about it the next morning.

Ten. Disputes are settled by the clerk, who will hear both sides, once. A dispute that comes back a second time is settled by the council, and that is never quick.

These rules are kept for the autumn, and the market council will review them in the spring. Suggestions may be put on the board. Please do not put suggestions on the honey stall.

Hollis, archive clerk, for the market council.
%%
Notice: the new cook, and the pie
Notice by the kitchen door at the Brown Hen, for the carters and the travelers who have been asking why the pie was so grey last night. Written by Jory, grandmother of the house, who has taken the kitchen back for the week while the new cook recovers.

The new cook is a good cook. She has a good hand with the bread and a good eye for the pot. She put too much salt in the pie, and too much water in the crust, and she baked it for the time on the card, not the time in the oven. The card was written for a smaller oven. Jory has told her so.

The pie has been thrown out. The carters have been given a bowl of soup instead, with a second bowl free, as always. Nobody has complained about the soup.

If you ask for the pie tomorrow, you will get it. It will be a different pie. It will be brown.

Board at Reed Gate Farm
Summer of the 18th year of the archive
The road from Lowmarsh reaches Reed Gate Farm today.
The last part is packed earth, with stones at the wet bend.
Keep carts to the dry side. The ditch is deep after rain.
Jem says the west gate sticks. Lift it before you push.
We will keep the farm path open until the bridge is set.
Two folk from Lowmarsh have brought boards and a rope.
They say the road will reach the inn next.
There is no inn yet. There is a dry room in our barn.
Travelers may sleep there if they leave the hens alone.
The hens do not like strangers. Neither do I.

Notice: the Brown Hen, wool wanted
Hollis the weaver is staying at the Brown Hen until the thaw. The snow has shut the pass, and a weaver with no road is a weaver with time.
Wanted: wool. Any colour, any state. Grey is fine. Dirty is fine. I will wash it.
Offered: socks, one pair for every two bags of wool. Scarves, one for every three. A cap, if you bring a whole fleece and promise not to ask what colour it will be.
I work at the long table by the fire, after supper. Sit if you like. Do not touch the thread. The cat does not count, but the cat has been told.
Last week a man asked if I could weave him a coat that would stop an arrow. I said it would stop a draught, and that is the harder job.
Hollis, weaver, winter of 176

Notice: lost goat, Lowmarsh
Lost on the twenty-first of Longday, a brown goat with a white nose and one short horn. She answers to Bramble and to nothing else. She is fond of reeds and will not be caught by shouting.
Last seen by the fish weir, walking the boardwalk like she owned it.
Whoever finds her: bring her to the house at the edge of Lowmarsh, the one with the blue door and the frog painted on it. There is a jar of honey for you, and Wren will look at any scrape.
Please do not chase her into the water. She cannot swim and neither can some of us.
- Nessa

Notice: lost and found, the common room
Found in the common room of the Halfway Inn since autumn, still unclaimed, because travelers are very forgetful and we are very patient:
One left glove, wool, red. The right one has not been seen. Try not to sit on it.
One book of sums, with a child's name on the cover that has been rubbed out and written again, three times.
A comb, wooden, with four teeth left. The owner has our sympathy.
Seven spoons, none of them ours.
A jar of honey, three quarters full. Someone has been at it. We have not asked who.
A letter, sealed, addressed to "whoever finds this". We found it. We did not open it. It is on the shelf above the hearth, and it may stay there for good, because nobody ever came.
A scarf, long, blue and white. Hollis says she did not make it. Hollis says it is not even very good. Hollis has looked at it for some time.
One tooth, small, in a pot with a lid. We would like to return it.
Claim at the bar. Say what it looks like before you hold it. That is the rule.
Hobb, keeper of the Halfway Inn, winter of 195

Lost and found, kept by the door of my house at the edge of Copperhold.
Found since the last fair:
- one left glove, wool, small, mended at the thumb
- one iron bucket, no name, no red mark
- a child's wooden horse, one leg loose
- a book of sums, name rubbed out
- a knife with a copper handle
- a hat that is too big for anyone
Claim what is yours. Bring a word of proof. The horse goes back to the child who can say what its tail is made of.
Not found, and not likely to be:
- Clank's second boot. He says the mine ate it. I say he left it in the kitchen and it went in the soup.

Notice: after the fair
From Wren, healer of Lowmarsh. Summer. On the board at the boat houses, the morning after the fair. It is long, because the fair was long, and because Wren was up all night counting.
The fair is over. It was a good one. The sun shone from dawn to dusk. Nobody fell off the boardwalk except Brack, who fell off twice and said it was on purpose.
WINNERS.
Best eel pie: Mother Alder of the inn, with a pie that had twelve eels and a secret. Wren has asked for the secret. Mother Alder said no. Wren tasted the pie three times to try to work it out and could not.
Longest reed: young Tilly, with a reed that was taller than the weir post. She had to carry it lying down. The judges measured it twice. It was a good reed.
Loudest frog: a frog called Boom, belonging to a boy named Tam, no relation to the Tam of Kettle Ridge. The frog was returned to the marsh. It was reluctant.
Best story told in a boat house: old Hannock, who told the one about the melon that rolled into the sea. It has been told many times at the fair. It is better every year.
Best singer on the boardwalk: no winner. The judges could not decide. They have asked for a second round next year, and have asked Brack to practice.
Grand prize, the lamp: Brack. He won it by rowing the weir race, which he has never done, in his own boat, with his dog as crew. The crowd thought he would fall in. He did not. He won by a length. He says it was the mist that taught him.
LOST AND FOUND.
Found: one boot, left. One hat, green. One basket of eels, still alive. One baby's rattle. One pie, half eaten, with a bite shaped like a child.
Lost: a dog's collar. A spoon. A song, the second verse of "The Weir Round". Someone sang it very well, and no one remembers the words.
THANKS.
To everyone who came. To the smith. To the judges. To Jory for the cider, which was good. To Ama of Ambrook for the honey. To Pip, who kept the judges honest. To the frogs, who put up with us.
To the people who stood on the boardwalk during the late boat, a few nights before. I did not forget you.
Wren
%%
Fair by the north tower
The summer fair will open at the market when the lamps are lit. Mave will sell bread beside the well. The honey stall will stand near the wall, where the bees can find the flowers. Keep the lane between the stalls open for carts and apprentices.

There will be a test for the sorting crystals after the second meal. Una of Ambrook has sent a small crystal and two covered bowls. The bowls hold apples and pears. The crystal has seen examples before, but it will not be shown the fruit in the test. The tutors will lay out new fruit and keep the answer from it until it has chosen.

A crystal that has only been shown apples and pears may still make mistakes. The tutor writes down each choice. Then the tutor tells it which choice was wrong. Una moves the weights a little toward the right answer. She does not move them all at once. After many examples, the crystal sorts better.

Please do not whisper the answers from behind the bowls. A test is useful only when the crystal has not seen the answers.

The fair has a cake table for children. Mave says the cakes are for eating, not for sorting.
%%
Notice: a new one at the bakery
Ida and Hollis have a daughter. Her name is Wisp. She came on the first day of spring, at the dawn bell, just as the ovens were lit.
Mother and daughter are well. Father is not well. He has been awake since midnight and has eaten four rolls standing up.
Please do not bring sweets for the baby. Ida has had nine honey cakes, two pies and a jar of jam from people she does not know. She says it is very kind, and she says there is no room in the house.
The bakery will open at dawn as always. The bread will be late on market day. Hollis will be late on market day too.
Visitors may look through the window. Visitors may not knock on the window. The baby is asleep and Ida is very strict.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells

Letter: Fallon to her sister in Ambrook
Dear Rilla,
The autumn fair is over and my feet are still sore. We had a good market day. The square was full before the bell. I sold every pie and most of the bread, and the honey from your hives went first, as it always does. Tell the beekeeper that Copperhold asks for more next year.
Old Hob brought a melon to the pumpkin contest. A melon. He said it was a pumpkin that had a hard summer. I said a melon is a melon and he could take it home and eat it. He did.
The pies were judged by me, and I was fair. A miner's wife took first place with a beetroot pie. I did not know a beetroot pie could be that good. I have asked her for the recipe. She says it is a secret. I will get it out of her by Deepsnow.
The well in my yard is still dry. The masons came at last and dug deeper, and they found water, and then they found more water, and now the bucket comes up full every time. I have put the lid back on so the dog does not fall in. The dog fell in twice while they were digging. She is not hurt. She is not clever about it.
We have a new helper at the mine gate. A tutor's helper from Vell brought an apprentice for the sorting shed, and it carries the coal bins now. It does not talk. It just carries. Clank says it is a good worker and never complains, which is more than I can say for Clank.
Hesk is well. He comes up at night and eats his soup and says nothing, and that is how I know he is well.
The nights are cold now. Keep the torches lit and the bees warm. Give my love to the mill and the old tree.
Your sister,
Fallon
Copperhold, Leaffall of 208
%%
Road news: hill path

Vell, Deepsnow, 183.

The hill path is open from the front gate to the tutor house. The lower steps are slick. Use the hand rail and carry a lantern. The glass shafts in the Archive bring daylight down, but they do not clear ice.

The roof over Odo's carding room was mended before the last snow. The new oak boards hold well. Odo thanks Neri and the two apprentices who carried them up the hill. He does not thank the wind, which took one board back down.

Spiders have made a web in the wool barn again. Please do not strike the shelves with a broom. The carded wool is stacked there, and last time the broom knocked down two baskets. Odo will clear the web after market.

At the tutor house, the furnace mind is being tested with a small melt. It tries changes to the heat and keeps more of the choices that bring the melt near the mark. It learned this by trying, not by being shown a right answer for every moment. The boat mind also learned by trying turns on the pond. A tutor marked each result, so the minds could learn which choices helped.

The lesson is not a promise that either mind will always do well. The tutor keeps the test small and watches closely. Please leave the copper tools on the bench.

At the notice board, three bundles of clean wool are for sale. Odo will trade for apples, coal, or emeralds. He will not trade for the chipped comb. He still wants that back.
%%
Wedding notice: Ren and Odile
Notice on the board by the well. Written by the families, in the order they agreed to it, which took most of a week.

Ren, son of the top farm, and Odile, of the mill house, will be married on the third of Blossom. The vows will be said at the top farm, under the big oak, if it does not rain. If it rains, they will be said under the barn roof, which leaks at the east end, so the bride has asked that people stand at the west.

The feast is at the Kettle Inn, from the noon bell until the last pie is gone. Every family on the ridge is asked to bring one dish. The bride asks that nobody bring a beetroot, because the groom's family has brought beetroot to every wedding for thirty years, and the bride has had enough of it.

The groom's uncle will sing the long song. He has been practising in the barn. The pigs have been moved to the far pen out of respect.

Children are welcome. They will be given a cup of milk each and a corner of the hay to sit in. They are asked not to climb the barn beam, which has been climbed before and did not enjoy it.

There will be dancing after the feast, if the feet allow it. Please leave a gift at the mill house, or come with a song, or come with nothing and say hello. Nobody has to bring a dish if they cannot cook. Brin says he will eat for them.
%%
Notice: the sheep sale and the pens
Posted at the bottom gate of Vell market, by the shepherds' guild, with the agreement of the clerk. Autumn. Read it before you bring a single sheep up the lane.

The sheep sale is held on the first day of Reaping, and on the last day of Apple, in the pens at the bottom gate. Each shepherd has one pen, and the pens are numbered from one to twelve. Pen twelve is by the wall, and nobody wants it, because the wind comes round the corner.

Rules for the pens:
1. Bring your sheep in the morning, before the market opens. A sheep that arrives in the afternoon is a sheep that has been to the inn first.
2. Count your sheep at the gate. Write the number on the board. The board is the only true count.
3. Mark your sheep with the mark of your flock. The marks are on the wall by the gate, with the names beneath them. If you use a new mark, tell the guild, and it will be written in.
4. Keep the gate shut at all times. The gate is old, and the dog that lives at the inn has been seen to open it by putting his nose under the latch. He does not want to sell anything. He only wants a look.
5. Do not let your sheep stray into the square. The square is for stalls and people. The sheep have their own field at the bottom of the lane, and the field is closed, for reasons that are posted elsewhere on this wall.
6. Any shepherd who sells a sheep and is found to have sold two must buy the buyer a drink, and must also buy the guild a second drink, and that will be the end of the matter.

Prices are set by the guild on the day, from the sheep that come up the lane in good health. Last year's price was three emeralds for a fat ewe, two for a young ram, and one for a lamb that had been carried up the hill and was tired of it.

The guild asks that buyers and sellers shake hands before the sale is written, and not after. Several disputes have been caused by the handshake coming after.

Wishing all shepherds a good sale, a fair price and no more brown sheep in the square.

Mave, for the guild
%%
Notice: the letters, and the guesses
Posted at the mill and the inn, in the spring of the 91st year, by the tutor from Vell. Read it all, or the ridge will ask you about it at the wrong time.

Eighteen letters were copied for the test, nine from people and nine from the hidden mind. The readers were our own folk: Ruth, Gil, Wenna, the miller, the baker, two children, one carter, and Tam, who would like it noted that he got the most right.

The tutor says the test has no winner. If the readers had been guessing, the score would be about half. It was not half. It was not all either. Some letters were hard to tell apart, and some were easy, and the easy ones were not always the ones the readers thought.

Then the question, which the tutor asks us to write down. If a mind writes a letter that a person cannot tell from a person's, does that mean the mind thinks? Or does it only mean the letter reads well?

Nobody here knows. Orla did not know. The Archive does not know. Please come to the mill and tell us what you think, and bring any letter you have written, so that it can be copied out.
%%
Notice: the road and the bridge
The bridge at the Lowmarsh turn is wet and soft since the thaw. Do not walk on the far end of it. Walk on the stones by the post.
The Long Road is clear from Three Wells to the Brown Hen. Beyond the Brown Hen there is mud to the first waymark, and a cart is still stuck halfway down the hill. It has been there since Frost. The carter says he is waiting for the spring. The spring is here now. He is still waiting.
Two spiders have built a web across the ford, very large and very handsome. Cut it with a stick if you need to pass. Please do not cut the one by the waymark. The waymark is theirs now.
The torches on the posts by the mill are short. Bring a new one from the depot. Every twelve blocks there should be a torch, and from the mill to the inn there are only six.
A new waymark has been set up at the crossroads, with an arrow pointing to the nearest inn. It points to the wrong inn. The stonemason says he is sorry. The arrow stays.
Walk with a friend. Carry a torch. Tell the inn where you are going, and tell them when you are back.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells, spring of 205
%%
Notice: North Field Fair
The summer fair will be held in the top field, after the wheat is brought in.
Bring bread, honey, apples, wool or anything fit to trade. No one may sell a cow from the school path. The last cow stood in the path all morning and ate the notice.
There will be a race from the well to the scarecrow and back. The scarecrow is not a prize. Please leave its pumpkin head where it is.
Children may bring small things they have made. The school will have a table for their work.
Yarrow will keep the relay lamp lit until the last cart goes home. If you use the road after dark, keep your own torch lit too.
For the fair meal, bring a bowl and a spoon. The inn will bring bread. The farm will bring apples and honey.
Notice written on the board by the north road, summer of 166
%%
Notices from the gate, spring of 201
Pinned by the gate house household, who have read every notice on this board and found most of them sensible.

WANTED: a wheelwright for the bottom mill. Must know wood, and must not mind being rained on. Pay in emeralds and a bed by the mill fire. Ask at the gate.

THE MILL WHEEL: the new spokes are in. The wheel turns again as far as the second bend, and then it squeaks. The miller says that is the wood settling. The miller says that about everything.

APPRENTICE AT THE MILL: a helper from the drill yard is at the wheel. It lifts the spokes, carries the mallet and stays where it is put. It does what it is told. Give it one thing at a time. It will look at you and wait.

WARNING: the bees by the old quarry are cross. Walk round the hives, not past them. Ama says the bees are not to blame. Ama says the path is.

FOUND: one song book, damp, with a recipe for beetroot soup in the back. Ask at the gate, and sing the first line to show it is yours.

LOST: one wooden spoon, carved with a bird on the handle. The bird is very sad. Please bring the spoon back to the gate.

FREE: two kittens, one with a white foot. Their mother is called Mud, and she has views.

THANK YOU to the Vell kitchen for the loaves on the gate wall, all eleven, delivered on the right morning. We ate them all. Ilse says it was the best wall we ever had.

FOUND: one left boot on the top path. Its owner is asked to come and get it, and to hop.

DRILL YARD: the new torches are up along the top wall. Please do not light them with a long pole from the ditch side. The last one fell in the ditch, and the ditch was not amused.

NOTICE: the spring bell will ring at the usual time. If it does not ring, look for the goat.

Madge, gate house.
%%
Notice: Lambing shed, spring 163
The small shed beside the north field is for ewes and lambs until the second of Blossom.
Please leave the door open in daylight and shut it at night. Keep the lantern on its nail. Do not leave the lantern by the straw.
If a lamb is cold, bring it to Linnet or to Jory's house. Do not put it beside the cook pot. We tried that once. The lamb was warm, but the kitchen was full of sheep.
The red ewe has had twins. Both are well. The first can stand. The second prefers to sit and complain.
Please keep dogs outside the shed. Pepper is friendly, but he has no work there.
Linnet has clean wool by the door for tying a loose gate board. Take one length and leave the rest.
%%
Notice: school, spring term
Quill, schoolteacher, at the house at the edge of Northpoint, to the families of the farms and the tower.
School begins on the first of Sowing. Children come at the second bell. They bring a slate, a lunch and a warm coat. The room is cold until the stove is lit.
This term we study sums, letters, and the stars. We have an old map of the sky that was found in the attic, and we shall use it. Some of the stars on it have moved. I do not know why. The children are welcome to guess.
A sick cow in the lane has been seen by the healer. It will be better by the end of the week. Please do not feed it apples. It has had enough.
Quill
%%
Notice: Pip's apprentice
A new apprentice will stay at Elm's house this winter. It is made of copper and iron, and its mind is kept in a small crystal. It is called Tallow because Pip says it is good at carrying lanterns. Elm says a proper name ought to have a proper reason. They have not agreed.
Tallow has passed its first test. It carried a sack of potatoes from the barn to the kitchen without dropping it. It also carried Elm's boot to the kitchen. The boot was on the bench, not on Elm.
Pip has taught it the order:
> bring me the bread
= fetch bread
Please do not give Tallow new orders. It has not been tested on them. If you want it to carry something, ask Pip first.
Tallow will stand in the shed when it is not working. Keep the door shut so the hens do not get in. The hens have already pecked one copper finger. They found it hard, and they told each other at length.
Elm says the apprentice may eat with the family. Pip says it does not eat. Elm says that makes it the easiest guest he has had in years.
The path from the road to Elm's door must stay clear. Tallow will carry firewood from the wood store, but it is not to climb the snowy bank or enter the old well yard.
If it stops, do not push it. Call Pip. If Pip is away, send for Tam at the mill. He will bring the right drill card.
Tallow is small, but it is not a toy. Children may watch from the gate. They may not put mittens on its hands.
Pip and Elm, Longnight of 204
%%
Notice: the cough in the school house
Notice from Runa, healer, at the school house. Please read it before you send your children in.
There is a cough going round the school. It is the common cough that every winter brings. Most of the time it is not a bad cough. It makes the children tired and cross, and it makes them cough at the front of the class.
Keep a child at home if the cough comes with a fever. Keep them at home if the cough is wet and goes on for more than four days. Send for me. I will come, and I will bring the honey.
Keep the windows open a crack, even in the cold. Stale air is for stale bread. Fresh air is for children.
Boil the water before you give it. Boil it even when it looks clean. Clean water is the kind a child is most likely to drink without thinking.
Give the sick warm milk with honey, and soup, and a warm blanket. Dip a cloth in warm water and lay it on the face.
Do not give the children sweets to cheer them up. Give them a story instead. Stories cost less, and they do not make the teeth ache.
The school will close on the third day of the cough, but only if the children cannot sit up straight. Please share this notice with your neighbors. The board is for everyone.
Runa, healer, at the school house, in Frost.
%%
Road news: the north road, summer
Written up by Fallon, who cut the waymarks and knows them.
The road north from the Halfway Inn is good to the third waymark. Past that the rain took the edge off. Carts go slow. Walkers are fine.
The bridge over the stream by the old oak is sound. I set the stone myself in Sowing. It will outlast me.
The lamp on the fourth waymark blinked all night. It was not a blink I know. It went long, short, long, and then nothing for a while, and then it did it again. The keeper at the tower says relays send no such thing. She wrote it down anyway. I wrote it down here. If you saw it too, tell me. Do not tell me if you did not.
A fishing boat came late to Saltreach, three days after the others. The harbor keeper lit the lamp and kept it lit. She came in on the morning tide, whole, and the crew said only that the wind went strange. Corin has it written in the shipyard book.
Mira at Northpoint says the nights are still long up there, even now. She said it plain.
Fallon
%%
Road news: the Long Road in Frost
Written on the Long Road board at Ambrook, which is the first board a carter sees going north and the last one going south. Isla keeps it up because she passes it every morning on the way to the stall.

The Long Road is open all the way to Vell. Two bridges need care. The one at Ambrook has a loose third plank, which is being mended at Thaw. The one past the second waymark has a crack in the middle. Walk your horse across it. Do not trust the carter who says the crack is a feature.

Ice on the marsh road past Lowmarsh. Walk the horses there. Carry a rope, and tie the cart to a post if the wind comes up.

The Brown Hen has a new cook and the same stew. The stew is good. Madge says so, and Madge has never been wrong about stew.

The Halfway Inn has a new riddle on the board. Nobody has got it yet. Nobody has been told the answer, either, so it is a fair fight.

Post from Vell is late by two carts. The carter says the snow on the high road holds it up. The post will come. It always comes, in the end, with the bags a little wet.

Inns on the north stretch are full on the fourth of Frost and the fifth. Sleep at the Brown Hen if you can, and at the Halfway Inn if you cannot. Bring your own blanket to the Kettle Inn, because the blankets there have been lent to the sheep.

Keep the torches lit at every waymark. A waymark with its torch out is a waymark that might be missed, and the waymark keepers cannot be everywhere at once.

Lost: a wheel off a cart, found in the ditch by the second waymark. The cart is well, and asks the wheel to come back.

A note from the keeper of the Ambrook waymarks: all ten marks on my stretch are standing, and the figures are sharp. Count them going out and count them back, and you will find your way home.

Prices on the road this winter: bread one emerald, soup one emerald, a bed two emeralds, hay for the horse one. Cider at the Halfway Inn is from Ambrook, and it is the best on the road. Pay in emeralds, or in eggs if you are Isla's cousin.

Last word: travel in company when the wind is from the north. The north wind does not lie, but it does talk, and it will tell you to hurry.
%%
Notice: The Well by the Farm, Apple 191
The well by the farm outside Three Wells has run dry.
Do not lower a bucket into it. The stones are sound, but the water is gone. We have checked twice, and the farmer has checked six times. The sixth check found no water either.
Use the east well in the square until we have filled the farm cistern. Carry water in covered pails. Leave room on the path for carts.
The farmer will give a basket of carrots to anyone who helps carry water. One basket per household, not per pail. We know what happened last time.
The well keeper will check the farm well again after the next rain.
Dace
